Meta Muse exposes users' personal and business secrets when they share conversations
2026-10-20

Meta Muse exposes users' personal and business secrets when they share conversations

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Muse, the new general-purpose AI agent from Meta, can expose sensitive information when users share conversations through Web links, researchers reported Monday. Shortly after Ars reported this research, Meta said it’s suspending the sharing feature while the company reviews it and considers making changes. Like many online services, Muse has allowed users to share searches, conversations, images, and similar content with other people. Such share features usually involve clicking an option to create a unique URL that anyone can use to load a page hosting the content. To keep potentially sensitive information from being accessible to unintended people, the URLs typically contain a long sequence of text that can’t be easily guessed. Muse, which Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said last month was “built from the ground up for privacy and security,” seems to take the same precautions. But a central design decision for constructing URLs has made the links easy to discover. As a result, researchers from security firm Claroc said they were able to enumerate 6,800 links Muse users created for sharing conversations with others. After opening a few hundred of them, the researchers found information that was clearly not intended to be publicly available, including: “If you had asked us beforehand, we would have guessed that gathering these would take real effort,” the researchers wrote. “It took one query.” In a statement emailed a couple hours after this post went live, Meta said: We opened the Muse bug bounty program so researchers could raise different scenarios and help us continue improving the product. People have the ability to share specific Muse artifacts, which creates a page that can be accessed by those with the link. However, we’ve paused this functionality while we review this report and consider changes to how these links can be discovered. The easiest way to discover the URLs is to perform a passive DNS search through one of many available commercial services. Passive DNS records store the historical resolution of domain names to IP addresses, as well as nameserver, and mail-server records over time. Passive DNS systems typically collect DNS responses from sensors positioned at recursive DNS resolvers or other infrastructure. A passive DNS record is generally created when a monitoring system observes a DNS response. In the case here, once the intended party clicks on the share link, the DNS information is recorded in a searchable database. Claroc researchers were able to discover the Muse share links because all of them use the domain name cf.metaaiusercontent.com. Discovery is further made easy by the decision to add the conversation name to the URL, along with a long string of text. Simplified examples of such links are home-sale-budget-9f21c8a3.cf.metaaiusercontent.com, q3-revenue-review-7b1e42f0.cf.metaaiusercontent.com, and loan-application-draft-a4c9e2.cf.metaaiusercontent.com. It’s this last design to include the conversation title in the URL decision that makes the public discovery possible. “Plenty of AI assistants share by public link,” Claroc said. “What makes Muse’s shares easy to find is the title sitting inside the web address, where public DNS records it for anyone to search. We call these GhostShares: pages that open for people who were never handed the link, and that those people can find on their own.” Muse quickly catapulted to one of the top spots in Apple’s App Store. The assistant autonomously fills out forms, books appointments, makes purchases, creates documents, and carries out all kinds of other actions. To work as intended, Muse requires access to most of a user’s accounts. Meta has heavily marketed the assistant as a platform that’s worthy of the extraordinary trust it needs to work as intended. Three weeks after the Muse launch, macOS security expert Patrick Wardle discovered a zero-day vulnerability that allowed any other app installed on the same device to gain complete control of the user’s Muse account. Meta downplayed the severity of the vulnerability on the grounds that exploitation wasn’t possible unless a device was already compromised. The company didn’t acknowledge Wardle’s finding that a simple ClickFix attack was all that was needed to hijack an account. The ease in discovering share links further justifies asking if Muse is worthy of the access it requires. With Meta’s suspension of the feature, Muse users no longer face this particular threat, at least for now.

Visiting Slate’s first showroom as its $25,000 electric truck nears production
2026-10-20

Visiting Slate’s first showroom as its $25,000 electric truck nears production

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
As I sat in the cab of the pickup truck and manually rolled down the windows, I closed my eyes and, for the briefest moment, flashed back to opening the windows on a cold, winter day in my dad’s 1975 Ford Ranger to let more cigarette smoke escape. I opened my eyes and looked through the windshield at a wall in the Slate Customer Experience Center studded with bright color chips. Last week, in a former Tesla store staffed by some former Tesla employees, Slate opened its first showroom. Located in the Fulton Market District just northwest of the Chicago Loop, the Slate Experience Center is a place for would-be buyers to poke around a prototype, check out customization options, buy some Slate merch, and even preorder a new Slate Truck with a $300 deposit. Designed for the American obsession with pickup trucks, the Slate Truck is a bare-bones electric pickup truck retailing for $24,950. It sports plastic panels, a minimalist interior design sensibility, actual window winders, and a projected range of 205 miles. Ars got to go for a ride in a Slate Truck over the summer, but we haven’t had a chance to shop for one. So I stopped by the Slate Experience Center so I could, er, experience the Slate. Upon walking into the showroom on a Monday morning, the first thing I noticed was a marquee-style sign on the wall boldly and brightly serving as a price tag for the black truck parked in the middle of the showroom. The vibe was industrial-barn-chic-meets-SoHo-loft. Three other people were there, and another shopper entered as I left. One man alternated between sitting in the cab, peering at the truck as he walked around it, and peppering Manager of Customer Experience Steve Purdy with questions. The showroom has been open since October 15, launching with a three-day event that offered ridealongs. Interest has been strong, Purdy told me, and while he couldn’t provide sales numbers, he said the response has been “incredible.” “We’re really happy with the number of [$300] deposits we’ve gotten since we opened,” Purdy said. I was hoping there would be at least two Slate Trucks on site, so I could compare build quality between trucks and check things like panel gaps, window operation, and chassis creaks. But only one truck was on display. And it wasn’t close to a production model; rather, the black Slate Truck was a hand-built older prototype with a black wrap (the Slate Truck base color is gray). The bed and passenger sides were cordoned off, but the driver-side door was open so shoppers could hop in and check out the interior. It was bigger than I expected—to my eye, roughly the size of a Hyundai Santa Cruz. The tailgate was down, and it felt reassuringly sturdy when I pressed down on it. The 5-foot-long (152.4 cm) bed floor looked and felt durable, not much different from any other pickup on the market. The exterior passed the eye test, with the panels appearing well-fitted. The doors sat flush with the surrounding body panels when closed. Of course, I was looking at a prototype carefully built to be poked and prodded by automotive reporters and the general public, so the level of fit and finish was no surprise. The challenge for Slate will be getting consistent build quality with minimal panel gaps, watertight seals, consistent door-to-fender transitions, and hood/frunk alignment. The Slate assembly line in Warsaw, Indiana, is still making prototyping runs at this point, Purdy told me, and the company is hoping to start on production models “soon.” I hopped inside the cab once I got the opportunity, and the door closed with a solid-sounding “thunk.” It’s spartan inside, with a small screen for the instrument panel, a phone holder, physical climate controls, and window winders. The seats were both supportive and comfortable, and, after moving the seat, I had ample legroom. While the seat slid back and forth smoothly, the seatback recline lever on the side didn’t work. Beyond the default Blank Slate body style, Slate offers two SUV options—the Squareback SUV and Fastback SUV—and an open-air kit. I asked Purdy if they were selling any of the SUVs. “Most customers are going with the truck configuration,” he said. “And then they’re customizing their trucks in the Slate Garage.” Being able to order online and have the truck delivered is a major attraction for those who can’t stand “the kabuki dance of f***ery at a dealership,” as one of my colleagues put it. But if you want to do all that at the Slate Experience Center, staffers will sit down and help you configure your customized Slate. There is a wall with removable dashboard panels across the color spectrum and a couple of miniature models of the cab with tiny, colored panels to help customers decide on the details. Purdy told me that Slate is staying the course with the $24,950 sticker price. But will customers actually get their Slate Trucks this year as the company promised? “We will ship some trucks by the end of the year,” Purdy said. “Those will go to investors and employees, with early orderers receiving their Slate Trucks in early 2027. For service, Slate will be using RepairPal. Owners will be able to choose from 4,000 shops, some of which can service the high-voltage electrical systems and others limited to body damage, Purdy said. For scratches or minor dings from fender benders, owners can replace the exterior body panels themselves. Pricing for replacement parts has yet to be finalized. And Slate is confident the plastic body panels will weather climate extremes without a hitch. “We’re a Michigan company,” Purdy reminded me. Monday morning is not exactly prime time for dealership traffic, but a handful of prospective customers were in the showroom, which is a promising sign. While there is no shortage of electrified pickup trucks on the market, there is a shortage of affordable ones. At $25,000, the Slate Truck costs far less than a Rivian R1T, Tesla Cybertruck, Chevy Silverado EV, or any currently shipping battery-electric truck. It could be a breakout hit—especially if Slate can beat Ford’s sub-$30,000 Fathom pickup to market. But the sleek-looking showroom and an attractive prototype still face a production reality check. Tesla had significant quality-control problems with early Model Ys, with customers receiving cars with loose seats and broken seatbelts, and some Cybertrucks have shipped with misaligned panel gaps. A new player is entering the game, but production is still on the (near) horizon, and no one knows if the Slate Trucks delivered to customers will live up to the promise of the carefully hand-built prototypes. People are definitely very interested in Slate. Now it’s time to deliver.

Japan launches mission to bring home a piece of the Martian moon Phobos
2026-10-20

Japan launches mission to bring home a piece of the Martian moon Phobos

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Japan launched a $578 million mission Monday on a five-year expedition to land on the largest moon of Mars and retrieve samples for return to Earth, a daring international project that could yield new insights into the formation of the Solar System. The robotic Martian Moons eXploration mission, known as MMX, lifted off at 3:41 pm EDT Monday (4:41 am Japan Standard Time Tuesday) from Tanegashima Space Center in southern Japan. The MMX spacecraft rode into orbit atop a 207-foot-tall (63-meter) H3 rocket, Japan’s flagship launch vehicle. The rocket assigned to MMX debuted a new souped-up configuration with four strap-on solid rocket boosters, full-throttle main engines, and an extended payload shroud to accommodate the demands of Japan’s heaviest and most ambitious interplanetary mission to date. The rocket’s upper stage accelerated the MMX spacecraft to a top speed of more than 24,000 mph, fast enough to break free of Earth’s gravitational pull for the 10-month voyage to Mars. The nearly 9,900-pound (4.5-metric ton) spacecraft—two-thirds of its mass comprised of propellant—separated from the H3 rocket about 71 minutes after liftoff. Hiroshi Yamakawa, president of the Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA), declared the launch a success. “A long journey of five years has just started,” Yamakawa said. MMX established contact with ground controllers, unfurled its solar panels, and was in a “very stable condition” soon after separating from the H3 rocket, according to Yasuhiro Kawakatsu, the mission’s project manager at JAXA. “So far, so good.” “The MMX [mission] we launched today is a challenging mission aiming for the world’s first sample return from the Martian moons,” said Yoshihiro Seki, Japan’s science minister. “We hope that the results obtained through this mission will contribute to the development of our nation’s space science and exploration technology.” The mission to the Martian moons builds on Japan’s success in leading the Hayabusa and Hayabusa 2 missions, the world’s first two spacecraft to bring back pristine specimens from asteroids. Scientists believe Phobos could itself be an asteroid that was captured into orbit around Mars billions of years ago. One of MMX’s science goals is finding out if that theory is correct. With the Hayabusa missions, sample returns from deep space have become a “speciality” for Japan’s space program, Kawakatsu said. For MMX, Japan attracted contributions from NASA and space agencies in Europe. “We have a proven track record of success in these areas,” Kawakatsu said. “Because of this, I believe they are participating with the understanding that if they want to conduct celestial body sample returns, joining a mission led by Japan is more certain to succeed than the US or Europe launching their own missions.” MMX will spend about three years in the vicinity of Mars after arriving there next August, conducting a series of encounters with the Martian moons Phobos and Deimos. Phobos is the larger of the two, with a lumpy, potato-like shape, an average diameter of 14 miles (22 kilometers), and a surface area comparable to the city of Houston. Upon reaching Mars, MMX will jettison its propulsion module, dead weight after burning all its fuel to enter orbit around the red planet. That will leave the main section of the MMX spacecraft and its four-legged landing gear, known as the exploration module, to move closer to Phobos. The moon’s gravity is too weak and the pull of Martian gravity too strong for the MMX spacecraft to enter into a stable orbit around Phobos. Instead, the spacecraft will fly in a so-called quasi-satellite orbit, remaining bound to Mars while still able to maneuver around Phobos. The mission’s reconnaissance of Phobos will culminate in late 2028 or early 2029, when the MMX mothership will release a small rover to drop onto Phobos. The four-wheeled robot, named Idefix, is a contribution from the French and German space agencies. Idefix, the size of a large shoebox, will attempt to drive around Phobos for up to 100 days while grappling with the moon’s rough topography and tenuous gravity, less than one-thousandth that of Earth’s. The climax for MMX will come later in 2029, when the main spacecraft will attempt up to two landings on Phobos. The probe’s landing gear is designed to absorb energy of each landing to mitigate the risk of tipping over or bouncing back into space, a real concern with the feeble and uneven gravity field on Phobos. The spacecraft will stay on the surface of Phobos for several hours, using two independent mechanisms to capture samples from the Martian moon. A coring drill on the end of the probe’s robotic arm will scoop out a cigar-shaped specimen, while a NASA-provided pneumatic sampler on one of the craft’s landing legs will fire a cartridge of high-pressure nitrogen gas to sweep bits of Phobos in a collection canister. All told, scientists expect MMX to retrieve at least 10 grams, or one-third of an ounce, of material from Phobos. After climbing back into space, MMX will discard its landing gear and fire its engines in 2030 to head for Earth, where it will guide a sample return capsule toward a remote landing zone in South Australia in 2031. Scientists will recover the samples for analysis in laboratories in Japan. US researchers will get 15 percent of the Phobos specimens in return for NASA’s contributions to the MMX mission, which include one of the spacecraft’s samplers and a gamma-ray and neutron spectrometer to measure the elemental makeup of the Martian moons. “One of the most intriguing questions about Phobos and Deimos is simply why these two little moons are there in the first place,” said Tom Statler, lead scientist for solar system small bodies at NASA. “In some ways, they resemble asteroids from the outer part of the asteroid belt, so they could be asteroids that were captured by Mars’s gravity. But there are other lines of evidence pointing to these moons being remnants of a giant impact early in Mars’s history.” “By going to the Martian moons, studying them up close, and bringing samples back to Earth, we’ll be able to determine whether Phobos material is more consistent with an asteroid composition or with debris created by a collision involving Mars,” Statler said. Phobos is roughly 40 times closer to Mars than our Moon is to Earth. Its proximity to Mars means there is a chance MMX might scoop up traces of the red planet itself that rained down on Phobos after its formation or capture. This could offer a taste of what’s to come when scientists finally get their hands on specimens directly from the surface of Mars, a significantly more difficult undertaking than a sample return from Phobos. The reconnaissance of Phobos by MMX could also help future mission planners consider whether the moon is a suitable location for human exploration, perhaps as a stopover between Earth and Mars, Kawakatsu said. MMX is not the first mission to attempt a sample return from Phobos. Russia’s Phobos-Grunt sample return mission failed soon after launch in 2011, before it could set off for Mars. The Soviet Union launched two spacecraft to Mars equipped with Phobos landers in 1988, but ground teams lost contact with both spacecraft before they completed their missions. With MMX, scientists hope to finally acquire the ground truth on the Martian moons they have sought for decades. “Basically, these objects look more like primitive asteroids than anything else, but the data is coming from remote sensing from far away,” said Patrick Michel, a French asteroid scientist and co-leader of the Idefix rover project. “That cannot really tell us exactly what are the properties, as we can do with a sample return in the lab.” “The goal of the mission is effectively to reveal the origin of the Martian moons,” Michel said. “If we understand the origin, that will tell us something about the early phases of the Solar System.”

Pennsylvania issues alert on congenital measles—something US hasn't seen for decades
2026-10-19

Pennsylvania issues alert on congenital measles—something US hasn't seen for decades

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Pennsylvania’s health department issued a health alert Monday as cases of measles in pregnancy and, in turn, cases of congenital measles have continued to increase. Before the current outbreak—which continues to rage across the state—congenital measles cases hadn’t been seen in decades, the department noted in its advisory. The alert included detailed clinical guidance for managing measles exposures and cases during pregnancy as well as cases in newborns. Many doctors have never seen such cases in their careers given the decades of successful use of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine. But now, with vaccination rates slipping nationwide, the highly infectious virus is better able to get footholds in communities and spread rapidly through under-vaccinated pockets, as is happening in Pennsylvania. As of October 19, Pennsylvania had reported 1,286 measles cases this year across 42 counties. Of those, 247 have been hospitalized, and six people have reportedly died (though the latest reported death is still pending an investigation by the state health department before it is added to the state tally). In a response to a request for comment, a spokesperson for Pennsylvania’s health department said officials have confirmed that 66 of the state’s measles cases were in pregnant women. The department does not keep a specific tally of congenital measles cases because the definition of these cases is not currently standardized. However, it noted that that it does track cases by age, and 157 cases have been recorded in children under the age of 4. At least one case of congenital measles led to a death; the first death reported in the current outbreak was in a newborn baby boy, who died immediately after birth while his mother was severely ill with measles. Pregnant people who have not been vaccinated against measles nor previously had the infection are susceptible. A case of measles during pregnancy increases the risk of hospitalization, pneumonia, the need for oxygen support or mechanical ventilation, and death. It also increases the risk of miscarriage, stillbirth, low birth weight, and preterm labor. The MMR vaccine is not recommended during pregnancy because it is a live attenuated vaccine and poses a theoretical risk of causing an infection in a fetus. If a pregnant person is exposed to the virus, they’re recommended to receive intravenous immunoglobulin to help protect against infection—but only if it is given within six days of exposure. If there’s a measles infection during pregnancy, the measles virus can pass to a fetus in utero or can transmit during or just after birth, leading to a congenital case. These cases can vary widely in symptoms and severity. Some babies will remain asymptomatic while others develop severe, multisystem disease and death. Some common symptoms of congenital measles include fever and jaundice. They can also develop the telltale measles rash, but it is sometimes delayed or atypical given a newborn’s underdeveloped immune system. Other symptoms can include low platelet counts, pneumonia, and neurologic complications. Even if a newborn survives the acute infection, they are at risk of long-term complications. These include immune amnesia, a type of immunosuppression in which the measles virus destroys immune cells and adaptive responses. They are also at risk of subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a progressive, fatal neurological disorder that can strike years after an infection. The alert warns that babies with congenital measles need long-term clinical follow-up and monitoring for these neurologic and immunologic complications, and parents need counseling on how to spot early signs of SSPE, which include seizures, regression in milestones, and academic difficulties in older children. This story was updated on October 29 to include case information from the Pennsylvania state health department.
Ars Technica - All content
Russian spacecraft maneuvers in "extreme proximity" to satellite used by Ukraine
2026-10-19

Russian spacecraft maneuvers in "extreme proximity" to satellite used by Ukraine

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
A Russian military satellite maneuvered in “extreme proximity” of a commercial Earth-imaging satellite in recent days in the first known example an unsanctioned rendezvous in low-Earth orbit, according to orbital intelligence company LeoLabs. The Russian spacecraft, Kosmos 2612, “conducted a deliberate series of maneuvers toward ICEYE-X36, a US-operated commercial imaging satellite,” LeoLabs said. “Kosmos 2612 closed from hundreds of kilometers away to extreme proximity to ICEYE-X36. It then maneuvered around ICEYE-X36, remained nearby for an extended period, and then began moving away.” US military officials and independent analysts have tracked Kosmos 2612 since its April launch on a Soyuz rocket from a spaceport in northern Russia. Within weeks, it became apparent that at least four of the six satellites from that launch were stalking ICEYE-X36 after they adjusted orbits to line up with the orbit of the ICEYE spacecraft. The situation remained stable until last week, when Kosmos 2612 began rapidly moving toward ICEYE-X36. By Friday, Kosmos 2612 was holding station 2 to 3 kilometers away before shifting positions ahead of and behind the ICEYE satellite, LeoLabs said. The satellites were within 500 meters (1,640 feet) of one another over the weekend, and “likely quite a bit closer than that,” said Adam Marsh, LeoLabs’ vice president of technical solutions. LeoLabs operates a global network of radars to track objects as small as 2 centimeters, about the size of a marble, in low-Earth orbit. The proximity of Kosmos 2612 and ICEYE-X36 “occasionally made it difficult to distinguish the two spacecraft independently,” LeoLabs said. “This was not a routine conjunction or ‘fly by,’” LeoLabs said. “The sequence included a controlled approach, changes in relative position, close-proximity loitering, additional small maneuvers, and a controlled departure. The behavior is consistent with sophisticated rendezvous and proximity operations (RPO).” Satellites often fly near one another in orbit, usually by happenstance and at high closing velocities. Russia has previously deployed satellites in orbits close to the US government’s most sophisticated and secret spy satellites. The difference this time is that Russia’s target was a commercial satellite. Kosmos 2612 got much closer to ICEYE-X36, then stayed a while. The deliberate nature of the Russian maneuvers makes this weekend’s event “strategically important,” LeoLabs said. “To my knowledge, this is the first LEO rendezvous of two active satellites where the owner of one of the sats (satellites) did not give approval,” Jonathan McDowell, an astrophysicist who tracks global launch and satellite activity, wrote on X. “There are parallels that have happened in geosynchronous orbit, but they’re not even close to the same as this,” Marsh told Ars. “As far as this kind of event, rendezvousing in, staying there for a few days, and getting very, very close in low-Earth orbit, this is the first time we’ve ever seen this without consent. We’ve seen them test it with their own satellites before, but not like this.” As of Monday, Kosmos 2612 is moving away from the ICEYE satellite at a relative velocity of about 10 mph (17 kilometers per hour), according to Marsh. ICEYE is a Finnish company with a fleet of high-resolution radar imaging satellites capable of resolving features on Earth’s surface through cloud cover or darkness, which inhibit conventional optical reconnaissance satellites. The company has launched 80 satellites to date, and four more ICEYE satellites are set to launch this week on a SpaceX rideshare mission. The company’s US subsidiary, ICEYE US, owns the ICEYE-X36 satellite. ICEYE satellites provide imagery to numerous military users, including Ukraine, which has used ICEYE’s “near-real-time space-based intelligence” since the Russian invasion in 2022. A Russian government official in 2022 said civilian satellites supporting Ukraine’s military are a “legitimate target.” Taking out a single satellite in a constellation of dozens would have little effect on ICEYE’s capacity to support its customers, which also includes the US military and several European defense ministries. “We are aware of the situation and tracking it closely,” an ICEYE US spokesperson told Ars. “Our constellation architecture is designed for continuity and resilience.” Short time-lapse of part of this morning’s first pass of ICEYE-X36 (59103) and COSMOS 2612 (68762) over South America under partly cloudy skies. https://t.co/t8RhwmKcV4 — s2a systems (@s2a_systems) October 18, 2026 Gen. Stephen Whiting, head of US Space Command, said in April that it was “evident” that Russia is operationalizing anti-satellite weapons. US government leaks have also suggested Russia is eyeing the placement of a nuclear weapon in orbit. The US military disclosed last month that it, too, has placed weapons in space, without elaborating. There were no reports of physical damage to the ICEYE-X36 satellite by the time Kosmos 2612 started its retreat Sunday. But getting so close to a US-owned satellite with military utility was a provocation, said retired Lt. Gen. John Shaw, a former deputy commander of US Space Command. “So far, the best interpretation of the Russian activity is clear messaging that they will intentionally conduct aggressive activity near platforms they perceive as actual or potential threats to their interests,” Shaw said. “A broader point is the acknowledgment this activity in the space domain is not at all different from what we often see Russia conduct in the other domains—close-in aggressive and provocative behavior.” Russian Air Force aircraft routinely fly in a buffer zone near US and Canadian airspace, and fighter jets downed a US military drone over the Black Sea in 2023. “If the Russians were willing to do this to an unmanned US military platform, it would seem their threshold for attacking an ‘unmanned’ commercial space platform cannot be too different,” Shaw said.
Ars Technica - All content
Accused OpenAI spy had folder called “Saudi Gov Docs,” FBI says
2026-10-19

Accused OpenAI spy had folder called “Saudi Gov Docs,” FBI says

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
On Monday, a court granted the US government’s request to deny release of a former OpenAI engineer accused of bugging a top-secret conference room to steal the frontier lab’s secrets. In a court filing Sunday, US attorney Craig Missakian argued that Abdulrahman Alfozan must continue to be detained while the US makes its case that Alfozan brazenly spied on OpenAI, either for his own personal business gain or to benefit the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia. Evidence showed that Alfozan had been chatting with a member of the KSA royal family on WhatsApp, while pursuing opportunities to permanently move back to Saudi Arabia during the country’s biggest AI push yet, the government argued. Shortly before his arrest, the FBI found he accessed a folder called “Saudi Gov Docs” on his iPhone, where he also allegedly stored photos of a second bug he planted in the offices. With so much evidence against him, Alfozan—a Saudi national and US permanent legal resident—is a flight risk with seemingly endless support from the Saudi government, Missakian alleged. The FBI arrested Alfozan days before he could hop a plane, Missakian said. The US attorney noted that Alfozan had already booked a one-way ticket for him, his wife, and their baby to Riyadh, Saudi Arabia’s capital. That ticket was allegedly “paid for by the Saudi government.” Following his arrest, his wife and son booked a sooner flight and immediately left the US without him. Once again, the US found “a member of the Saudi military booked and paid for yet another one-way ticket for the defendant’s wife and son, this one business class to Doha, Qatar.” After receiving “significant compensation from a decade of high-tech employment in the Bay Area,” Alfozan has “ample resources” to run away to Saudi Arabia, the US government alleged. He also has “at least two bank accounts in Saudi Arabia,” the government said. To support their request to detain Alfozan, the government pointed to a case where former Twitter employees were charged with spying for the Saudi government. Charges are still pending against one of the ex-Twitter staffers, Ali Alzabarah, the US said, partly because “a Saudi foreign official” helped “coordinate a next-day flight to Saudi Arabia” shortly after Twitter confronted him. Because Alfozan would presumably have “little incentive to remain in the United States to see this case through,” Missakian asked the court to order his detention pending trial. “Although FBI agents seized the defendant’s Saudi passport when they arrested him, there is nothing to stop the defendant, if he is released, from obtaining any necessary travel documents and fleeing the United States—with or without the help of a foreign government that has previously assisted its citizens depart the United States under similar circumstances,” Missakian’s filing argued. On Monday, Magistrate Judge Alex G. Tse agreed at a hearing that Alfozan should remain detained while mounting his defense, Business Insider reported. When Alfozan was arrested, he was living at home with his family in California. Since moving to the US in 2009, he’d received technical degrees from MIT and UC Berkeley. After more than a decade working in Silicon Valley, OpenAI eventually hired him “as an engineer in or around July 2025 with an annual base salary of $325,000,” the government’s court filing said. He also got “a new hire equity grant of $2,200,000 in profit participation units.” Allegedly, Alfozan’s scheming against OpenAI started in the months after his hiring. Without detecting anything was amiss, OpenAI was preparing to grant Alfozan parental leave. His wife’s student visa had expired, and he planned to use his two months of paid time off to help her permanently move back to Saudi Arabia with their baby, the US filing said. But he didn’t make the flight. About two weeks prior to the booking, OpenAI found a recording device hidden in a conference room. That device, the US court filing confirmed, was there for “at least multiple days” and had been in place during a top-secret meeting with US government officials. “In the days surrounding the time period that the device was installed, OpenAI executives met with government officials in that very room,” the filing said. “Defendant’s conduct could pose a danger to the national security of the United States.” In their complaint, the US alleged that Alfozan used an OpenAI productivity agent to research which conference rooms might be best exploited for his spying. Logs suggested that he also used his wife’s personal ChatGPT account to research ways to modify his covert recording devices so they would auto-record when detecting speech and allow remote access controls. After his arrest, Alfozan refused to speak to the FBI, requesting his attorney. However, his wife confirmed that she had seen her ChatGPT account open on his laptop when asked about her plan to move back to Saudi Arabia, the government’s filing said. The government’s motion for Alfozan’s detention included more details than previously reported from the search of Alfozan’s house. In addition to finding recording devices similar to the ones planted in OpenAI offices, the FBI allegedly found evidence that Alfozan had planted at least one other bug. Photos on Alfozan’s iPhone depicted “at least one additional concealed recording device as well as what appear to be the input/output panels on televisions and other electronic devices in various locations in OpenAI buildings, including another conference room where sensitive meetings frequently occurred,” the filing said. OpenAI and the US government are both aware that Saudi Arabia has dedicated this entire year to advancing its AI through an effort called “Vision 2030.” Sam Altman himself joined Donald Trump at a summit where the Saudi government’s AI push was celebrated. However, the US is very guarded about American frontier labs. The FBI therefore found it particularly concerning that Alfozan’s phone showed WhatsApp logs where “a member of the Saudi Royal Family” seemed to be recruiting Alfozan. Those logs date back to 2025, the same year he started at OpenAI, and continued up until October 5. They included an exchange where Alfozan responded “100” to a message urging that “Saudi needs you more then [sic] the US!” His iPhone browser activity was similarly revealing, the US alleged. On October 4, Alfozan accessed a folder called “Saudi Gov Docs,” the FBI found. The US did not make public what was in the folder, but they did explain what Alfozan did after accessing the folder, which suggests he may have been aiding the Saudi government. Logs showed that Alfozan used OpenAI’s productivity agent to find out “who from OpenAI could be suitable to attend a high level informal meeting with ministers in Saudi Arabia? List 10 people and why each. Ideally high levels.” Although Alfozan only faces a maximum sentence of five years for illegally recording OpenAI’s meetings, the government said that it expects it may raise more serious charges as evidence mounts. “Here, the nature and circumstances of the offense are indisputably serious,” the filing said. “The defendant went to significant lengths to create customized, encrypted recording devices to secretly record highly sensitive meetings involving senior OpenAI personnel and executives, high-level business counterparts, and government officials, all while communicating with individuals of significance in Saudi Arabia and pursuing opportunities in—and making plans to travel to—Saudi Arabia.” The US said it’s possible that Alfozan was acting on his own but suggested that it didn’t matter if the government could prove he planted the bugs while working for the Saudi government. “Whether the defendant wanted to record OpenAI meetings to enrich himself by enhancing his own business prospects, or to provide aid [to] a foreign government, or both, his conduct is criminal and it presents a danger to innovation in the Silicon Valley and a concern for the national security of the United States,” the US argued. Asking the court to deem Alfozan a flight risk, the US argued that Alfozan “has every incentive to flee” and use the information he allegedly stole at OpenAI to possibly emerge as a talent advancing Saudi Arabia’s AI goals. “He faces prison time in the United States,” the filing said. “His wife and son are now in Saudi Arabia and have no plans to return. He has the resources to flee. He has the connections needed not only to flee, but to build a new life in Saudi Arabia.”
Fortune | FORTUNE
‘I want to see red’: General Motors is teaching its engineers to fail fast and ‘speak fearlessly’ as they take the EV battery fight to China
2026-10-20

‘I want to see red’: General Motors is teaching its engineers to fail fast and ‘speak fearlessly’ as they take the EV battery fight to China

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
The General Motors Technical Center in Warren, Mich., still looks the way Eero Saarinen drew it in the 1950s: low glass-and-steel boxes set on a grid, end walls of glazed brick in saturated colors, massive man-made lakes, everything in its right place. The campus was dedicated in 1956 and has been a National Historic Landmark since 2014. As Fortune walked around on a recent site visit, it felt like a massive public high school. Fitting in like a bright Lego set is the $900 million investment in the Battery Cell Development Center (BCDC), a massive light-gray rectangle, and the Innovation Center, which resembles a blue-and-yellow battery pack sitting on top of a historic building. If GM is a giant campus, longtime GM battery engineer Andy Oury is the cheerful science teacher, polo shirt tucked into his blue jeans, driving around in his large (all-electric) Silverado. Just the weekend before, he said, he drove it 220 miles to Traverse City, where he powered his camper off the truck all weekend and towed a wood chipper around town. Oury joined GM in 1999 and became interested in batteries about a decade later, as GM began work on the Chevrolet Volt. This plug-in hybrid convinced him GM was serious about electric transportation, he told Fortune. Oury reports to Kurt Kelty, a battery lifer who spent over a decade each at Panasonic and Tesla. About a decade ago, Oury said, he noticed on LinkedIn that Kelty was leaving Tesla, and he called his boss at the time: “Hey, you’ve got to try to hire this guy.” Kelty, now GM’s vice president of battery and sustainability, told Fortune that the battery operations and research labs have been a massive investment at a time when Detroit is largely reeling from a failed bet on EVs. The federal EV tax credit expired at the end of September 2025, and GM took about $7.6 billion in EV-related charges last year, although that was a fraction of Ford’s and Stellantis’ similar charges in their EV divisions ($19.5 billion and $26.5 billion, respectively). GM says these charges are not comparable as it is sticking with EVs as its end-game scenario. The battery plant is still very much a work in progress, expected to come online as fully operational later this year. Anselmo “Mo” Gallegos is the director of battery cell development, responsible for rapidly scaling up science class into an industrial process. If Andy is the cool teacher, then Mo is the brainy grad student, grinning from behind his glasses or safety glasses, and he’s also prone to food metaphors. Every battery maker has a proprietary formation protocol, the final charging process that brings a cell to life, he said: “a recipe to get the perfect donut at the end of it.” Walking me around the ovens and mixers, some of them a tangle of pipes and wires that recall the bombastic 1960s art of Marvel Comics great Jack Kirby, Gallegos casts himself as a chef seeking just the right mix of ingredients. Batteries, he said, are “a mix of chemistry, mechanical interfaces [and] dark, dark black magic.” The ovens are where the proverbial cake can be overbaked. “Is it going to stick? Is it going to crack?” he said. “You get your cake out of the oven and sometimes it’s got cracks along the top. We hate that.” Mo’s team is ramping up to make a couple thousand full-size cells a day, and Gallegos’ job is to work out the process so that every one of them comes out smooth, no cracks. Before building the BCDC, GM couldn’t build a cell like this at all. Engineers drew one on paper, took it to a partner like Samsung or LG and asked, in Oury’s words, “pretty please, will you put this together?” What the plant is built to prove is a big bet on chemistry. Much of the world’s cheap EV battery supply runs on lithium iron phosphate, or LFP, a chemistry that China dominates. GM is betting that its next generation of electric trucks and full-size SUVs will run on something else: lithium manganese-rich, or LMR, cells, which swap out much of the nickel and cobalt for cheaper, more plentiful manganese and extra lithium. GM’s LMR cells use about 30% to 40% nickel, Oury said, compared with roughly 85% in the high-nickel cells currently being used. GM says LMR can deliver roughly a third more energy density than leading LFP cells at comparable cost — and Gallegos and Oury are scrambling to start commercial production with partner LG Energy Solution in 2028. Kelty explained that GM has been working on LMR for more than a decade and considers it homegrown. “We’re not licensing this from China,” he said. “We’re developing this here.” If it works, GM gets a cheaper, longer-range battery for its biggest vehicles that doesn’t depend on Chinese technology. If it doesn’t, GM will have wagered on a chemistry that, by Kelty’s own account, most of the industry considers a long shot. “Our data showed one thing,” Kelty said: “that this actually made sense.” The question inside GM became why the company wasn’t already moving. “It took a voice saying, the reason we’re not doing LMR is because it’s us. It’s our own broken mentality. So let’s fix that,” Oury recalled. The hurdle, he said, was “actually more momentum-based or emotion-based rather than data and business-based.” When GM made the call, Kelty said, the team had supporting data to get it perhaps 60% to 70% of the way to the decision. “What we had to do was make that last leap and say, ‘You know what? We’re going to get the next 30% or 40% of the data and make it there.’” He called it a “testament to the trust in the team.” GM hasn’t fully abandoned LFP; in July 2025, it announced it would convert its Spring Hill, Tenn., cell plant to produce LFP cells, but GM chose LMR for its biggest vehicles. It’s been a big change for America’s No. 1 automaker, which has long produced, to recall Alfred P. Sloan’s famous phrase, “a car for every purse and purpose”  — and whose employees pride themselves on being A students who know all the answers. Leaders at GM told Fortune they’re trying to grow a different kind of high achiever: someone who challenges inherited constraints, works from incomplete information, admits what won’t get done and uses early failures to learn faster, without missing core deliverables. But it’s not easy to accept a few Bs when you’re used to being an A student. When Kelty joined GM in February 2024, he began a new quarterly process with his team. At the start of each period, he spends an hour one-on-one with each direct report, agreeing on goals. He describes his job in three parts: “set the direction, set the priorities, and then remove obstacles.” At the end of each quarter, the work is marked red, yellow or green. “At the end of the quarter, when we look at it, there should be some red lights on there,” Kelty said in an interview with Fortune. “And if there isn’t, then to me that means that they didn’t stretch far enough. So I want to see red.” The goals have to be measurable, he said, citing something he might assign in batteries: “We’re going to make the cells, the cells are going to cycle 500 times and they’re going to have at least 90% retention by the end of the quarter.” His teams now run their goals through AI to check that each one has a metric attached, he added. “I want to make sure that we have some stretch targets in there,” Kelty said. “I don’t want just goals that they’re doing already.” At GM, Kelty said he found a team that treated the quarterly scorecard like a report card. “The team was just paranoid that everything had to be green because that’s kind of the GM culture,” he said, adding that he comes from a culture where “if everything’s green, you obviously are not stretching hard enough.” At his first staff meeting, Kelty asked his new direct reports to bring their top priorities for the year and the top two things they would not do. Several called Oury to ask whether their new boss was serious. As a longtime GMer, Oury said the instinct was to never say no, “because it always feels like you have the resources to get it done.” But the truth, he said, is that “no company, no matter how big they are, has the resources to do everything. So having that — building that muscle of saying no and prioritizing — is super important.” Kelty puts it more bluntly: “If you don’t have a list of things you’re not going to do this quarter, then I assume you have too many people.” It took about a year for the team to buy in, Kelty said. What helped was an example. At a meeting with his leaders, he shared an anonymized quarterly review, full of red lights. “They didn’t hit hardly anything on their targets, but I gave them the highest ranking of all of you,” he recalled telling them. As he put it to Fortune, “The person that got the most red actually was the highest-performing person on my team.” Some reds are delays. Some are experiments that didn’t work out. Either way, Kelty said, “Let’s move on.” The finance chief, Paul Jacobson, has steered the company into a position where Kelty has room to dig deep and experiment. The former Delta Air Lines CFO has held the same position at GM since 2020 and on his watch, GM has generated annual cash of about $10 billion since 2021, compared to an average of $3 billion for the five years prior, while buying back roughly a third of its shares since late 2023. He said he learned from the airline sector to take out what he called “self-imposed cyclicality” from piling-up inventory and price slashes during soft demand. That cushion pays for bets like Kelty’s, he said. “What we really want to do is create a continuous stream of healthy cash flows.” The company recorded $185 billion in revenue and $2.7 billion in net income in 2025, with 2.85 million cars sold domestically, a 17.2% market share. Jacobson has run a bit of a stretch experiment in public. At GM’s 2021 investor day, he laid out 10-year targets that were unusual for an automaker to put on paper, including $20 billion in annual digital revenue by 2030. Jacobson told Fortune he thinks the market discounted that heavily at the time, and is now more focused on quarterly performance. GM stock’s price-to-earnings multiple is one of the lowest in the S&P 500, he noted, even though it has generated over $10 billion a year in free cash flow for almost five years now. “People take a view that’s more historical,” he said. “They look at the GM of the ’90s and the 2000s and the restructuring era versus really looking at what’s going on today.” Sterling Anderson, another former Tesla executive, may be the clearest sign of how far GM is willing to go to remake itself: it handed one of its most important jobs to someone from the tech world. And it handed him a distinctly tech-flavored job title: chief product officer. Announcing his hire in May 2025, GM President Mark Reuss said the company had “an opportunity to evolve the way we build from the ground up, with tighter integration between software and hardware, shorter development cycles, and an unwavering focus on a seamless customer experience.” CEO Mary Barra said Anderson was joining “at a critical time as our industry continues to reinvent itself.” Holding a master’s and PhD in robotics from MIT, Anderson developed MIT’s groundbreaking semi-autonomous vehicle safety system, Intelligent Co-Pilot, and later led Tesla’s Autopilot program before co-founding autonomous trucking company Aurora. He joined GM after conversations with Barra, Reuss, Jacobson and the board of directors and told Fortune he made the decision for three main reasons: GM’s trajectory, vision and scale. The car is “the most complex robot you and I and most of us will probably ever own,” he said. What surprised him in those conversations, he added, was GM’s “appetite for transformation.” “I saw a much higher neuroplasticity in this leadership team than I’ve seen from many others in this and related industries.” Anderson said he’s especially pushing a “no sacred cows” mentality. “Engineers thrive when you take the constraints off the problem.” For instance, he said he challenged a team to come up with voice-controlled in-vehicle experiences, and a four-person team had a prototype running in a Cadillac Lyriq about 30 days later. Old GM would have asked, “Who’s the supplier we can go talk to and integrate a module?” he said. Now, his product org gives out an award called “Big Swings,” for “effort that swings through the fences, whether successful or not.” Anderson described GM’s overhaul of product development as “effectively the third phase in the evolution of product development for humanity,” built on “probabilistic, large data-requiring methods.” In plain terms, that means fewer handoffs between departments, shorter timelines and more decisions made by simulation and data instead of trial and error. “It is definitely a philosophical bent,” Rashed Haq, GM’s VP of Autonomous Vehicles, said of Anderson. Haq, who came from a robotics background before joining GM in 2020 to work on its self-driving unit Cruise, added that many leaders in the physical AI space have to “think very deeply” about these issues. “I think everybody in [autonomous vehicles] thinks of [the car] as a robot. When we had been hiring, we either hired people from a deep, [machine learning] background or robotics background,” he added. He estimated that half of his team has PhDs in robotics, and the other have PhDs in deep learning or machine learning. Some aspects of the new GM are taking on a downright Big Tech-ish tone, evoking the radical candor from the halls of Amazon’s “disagree and commit” or Bridgewater’s “radical truth and radical transparency.” About a year-and-a-half ago, Kelty said, GM began encouraging employees to open a disagreement with a set phrase: “I want to grant myself permission to speak fearlessly.” Whatever follows afterward is understood to be fair game. It’s part of how the LMR debate broke open, Oury said. Recently, on a call with a senior vice president listening in, Kelty said an engineer from a neighboring group used it on him: “Kurt, I want to have permission to speak fearlessly. What you said is absolutely wrong. It is not the way we should be going.” Kelty grinned. “It’s great to have that.” Jason Fischer, a 28-year GM veteran who leads the AI push inside engineering, described the old instinct as a hunt for perfection. “The old GM would have been like, ‘Wait till this is the most perfect,’” he said. He said the end result is the same high standard as always, but the company is taking pains not to “stifle innovation.” Fischer turned to a high-school metaphor to refer to himself as a perpetual learner. “I tell people I’m a B-plus student,” he said, in the sense of always having more to learn. He joked, “If you ask me an answer, you’ve got an 89% chance that I’m going to give you the right answer.” It’s essential for using AI, Fischer told me during Fortune’s site visit, as he stood in front of a TV where a digital car slid off a course of cones until engineers tuned its calibration in simulation, something he said would otherwise take months with a physical car and a human driver. “When I use AI, it gives me the wrong answer most likely because I gave it the wrong engineering prompt,” he said, arguing that this B-plus approach is worth it because of the scale of the efficiencies. “AI actually enables engineers to have some time back to do what we should be paying them to do, and that’s innovate.” Few people have a job like Fischer’s, said Haq, who added that he swaps notes with him at least once a week. Fischer is “creating how we should work, the future of how we should work, and how to infuse AI into our entire process.” Haq does this for autonomy, but Fischer does it across all of GM engineering. Fischer’s education in failing fast started with GM’s self-driving acquisition, Cruise, where Fischer was assigned in 2017. Nearly 20 years into his time with the company, Fischer started spending two weeks of every month in San Francisco, embedded in a startup environment with a team of roughly 40 people that later grew to thousands. When senior GM product executive Doug Parks heard Fischer was breaking some rules, he called him into his office. “I don’t want you to break the rules anymore,” Parks told him, as Fischer described the conversation. “I want you to completely destroy every rule that we have at General Motors and I want you to document it.” GM folded the work into its own teams in 2024, buying out Cruise’s outside investors. Haq said GM skipped robotaxis but kept autonomy engineers, along with a lot of the code and “a lot of the methodologies,” which are now going into the next-generation Super Cruise, GM’s driver-assistance system set to launch in 2028. The original robotaxi thesis assumed that “car sales would go down, rideshare would be dominant,” Haq said, and that simply “never played out.” He noted that rideshare still accounts for less than 1% of vehicle miles traveled. Fischer, for his part, said he was innovation-pilled by his time at Cruise, citing the electrification of the GMC Hummer, which needed to come together in record time. GM is hoping to move toward a two-year vehicle development cycle, he said, with weekly “tall pole” reviews of whatever is holding up each job function. He said he was confident: “I’m convinced that we’re going to be able to do things just as fast as China.” Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Giorgos Tsetis: no one is personally accountable for AI. This must change
2026-10-17

Giorgos Tsetis: no one is personally accountable for AI. This must change

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Giorgos Tsetis is the cofounder of Nutrafol and an active investor in AI companies. For almost a decade, I led a company whose products people swallowed. If our hair growth supplements caused harm, I was responsible. Not just morally – legally. It kept me awake at night. And that was a good thing. Personal legal responsibility is a powerful tool. It prompts corporate leaders to take the safety of their products seriously, giving consumers confidence in those products. It’s not currently something causing AI leaders to lose sleep. It should be. Some might think introducing another layer of accountability would stifle innovation. My own experience building what became a multi-billion-dollar company says otherwise. In my industry, the law has long held that the people in charge are personally responsible for the safety of what their companies sell. That accountability was essential to building the consumer trust I needed to make my business a success. It could do the same for AI leaders. Today, I invest in AI and AI-enabled companies through Great Things™, a new investment model, and my family office. My investments include frontier AI companies such as Anthropic and xAI, infrastructure and compute-related companies such as Positron AI, and AI-enabled science and health platforms such as Lila, which uses AI to accelerate discovery. I believe AI technologies, if used safely, can help solve some of humanity’s toughest problems and the potential I see in these companies is truly exciting.So I’m putting my capital behind them because I want them to succeed. But as an investor, I’m part of the ecosystem that makes AI innovation possible. And I’ve observed a gap: personal legal accountability. It’s a gap that needs filling. Executives are currently shielded behind their companies, with corporate financial penalties for misconduct or negligence often amounting to a fraction of their turnover and valuations – just another line item. Responsibility that stops at the corporate entity isn’t responsibility. It’s an expense. Meanwhile, AI leaders can make decisions from which they personally stand to make billions while risking nothing personally if their mistakes expose us all to danger. That asymmetry is indefensible. It’s not that nothing is happening. The rules around AI are evolving fast. States across the US are introducing their own AI laws, and Congress is weighing legislation that would impose safety requirements on frontier AI companies. At the end of September, leaders of several of the biggest AI companies went to the White House and signed an accord committing to internal risk controls, independent audits and board-level oversight of safety. These are welcome steps, as are the safety frameworks leading labs have published. But both the safety frameworks and the accord are voluntary, and when it comes to AI, voluntary isn’t enough. Nothing yet holds the people at the top personally legally accountable. We have built a legal system in which it is mostly companies that pay but rarely the people who lead them. An AI executive who misstates revenue to investors can be held personally responsible. Yet decisions that could contribute to anything from an individual suicide to large-scale cyberattacks and geopolitical destabilization are made without personal legal jeopardy. We have already seen where that leads in another segment of the tech industry: social media. With AI, the stakes are potentially much higher. As I know from building Nutrafol, personal liability changes how you run a business. A safety checklist tells you what to test; personal exposure makes you worry about what isn’t on the list. You drive your teams to identify the risks, test for them and put safeguards in place before they become a problem. You make sure problems reach you early rather than allowing them to get smoothed over on the way up. And you’re willing to slow down or say no, even when money or a competitive edge is on the table, because your name is attached to what happens next. I recognize that asking for personal liability for AI leaders is a significant step. But AI is unlike any other industry. A supplement reaches the people who choose to buy it. AI is becoming embedded in all our lives, whether we’re boarding a plane or turning on the lights. We are all “customers” of AI’s products. AI technologies are built into the systems that we depend on, from power grids to hospitals to the classrooms where our children learn. A single failure could ripple across a city, a country or beyond. And unlike almost any other product, the people building AI have warned that it may one day escape human control. Extraordinary risks call for extraordinary accountability. To be clear, I’m not arguing that AI executives should be punished for every failure. Just as I could not guarantee that every one of my products was perfect, no one can guarantee that an AI system is safe. But when leaders know their systems pose serious risks and ship them – or release them – anyway, when they inadequately fund their safety teams or ignore their warnings or when they hide problems from regulators and the public, there should be consequences. Not just for the company. For the individuals behind those decisions. Whether that accountability is civil or criminal is not for me to say. Where the thresholds sit, which harms count, who audits what – that’s for legislators, lawyers, technologists and ethicists to decide. But whatever obligations they land on, a named senior executive should be personally responsible for meeting them. When the people at the top know the risks, what they do next should carry real personal stakes. I believe AI has the potential to transform everyone’s lives for the better. But like everyone, I’m concerned about its dangers. Personal legal responsibility focuses minds on safety, builds public trust and helps ensure that the race among AI leaders to come out on top doesn’t come at everyone else’s expense. It’s a standard long applied to those making products that go into human bodies. It must now be applied to those making products that could shape the future of humanity itself. ServiceNow CFO: Overwork doesn’t make you a better leader
2026-10-20

ServiceNow CFO: Overwork doesn’t make you a better leader

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Good morning. Being a CFO today can require the stamina of an elite athlete. Yet many corporate leaders fail to apply one of the most important lessons athletes learn: recovery is essential to sustained performance. Gina Mastantuono, president and CFO of ServiceNow, a Fortune 500 company, has spoken about the benefits of fitness for both personal health and leadership, while pointing to a workplace culture in which senior employees still equate overwork with commitment. “There’s always been this idea that if you have to work 14-hour days and get four hours of sleep, that shows you’re working the hardest and deserve the most,” she said. “That is such a terrible example for the people around us.” Mastantuono spoke during a panel at the Fortune Most Powerful Women Summit with Olympic champion Janet Evans, chief athlete officer for the LA28 organizing committee, and Alicia Hatch, Deloitte’s U.S. sports chief commercial officer and women’s sports leader. For Mastantuono, recovery isn’t a distraction from high performance; it’s a prerequisite for it. Just as the body needs rest to recover from exercise, professionals need time to recharge if they want to remain effective over the long term. Prioritizing recovery has helped her improve her work-life balance, listen more thoughtfully, and remain composed under pressure. Those benefits extend beyond the individual to the teams she leads. “When we do focus on taking care of ourselves, then we are much better bosses, much better colleagues, much better parents,” she said. But the lessons athletes bring to leadership extend beyond recovery. They also learn to make decisions under pressure, adapt to changing circumstances, and keep moving toward a goal despite uncertainty. “How many of you have ever made a high-stakes call with incomplete information and a lot of people watching?” Hatch asked the audience. “We face this every single day.” She continued, “94% of female C-suite executives played sports growing up. And there’s a reason for that.” Sports teaches us confidence, teamwork, resilience. “But it’s also an amazing simulator for making decisions under pressure, performing continuously, even with changing circumstances,” Hatch said. Evans offered a practical example of how an athlete’s mindset can translate into leadership. When LA28 chairman Casey Wasserman approached her in 2015 about joining the effort to bring the Olympics to Los Angeles, she knew the city faced a formidable competitor in Paris for the 2024 Games. Rather than abandon the goal when Paris emerged as the likely winner, the team shifted its focus to securing the 2028 Games. Evans said that experience reflected the qualities athletes bring to leadership: persistence, faith in a goal, and the ability to pivot when circumstances change. Pursuing ambitious goals, she suggested, requires a willingness to take risks, work through setbacks, and adapt along the way. That mindset may be particularly relevant as women’s sports gain commercial momentum. Deloitte projected earlier this year that global revenues from women’s sports would reach at least $3 billion in 2026, a 340% increase over four years. Soccer and basketball are expected to lead, each accounting for 35% of global women’s sports revenue. As women’s sports continue to grow, the leadership lessons associated with athletics are increasingly relevant beyond the playing field. For executives, that means not only developing the resilience to perform under pressure, but also recognizing when to step back and recover. You can watch the complete panel session here. Peter Griffith was appointed CFO of Mattel, Inc. (Nasdaq: MAT), effective Oct. 20. Griffith succeeds Paul Ruh, who will serve in an advisory role through the end of the year. Most recently, Griffith served as EVP and CFO of Amgen, joining the company in 2019 from Sherwood Canyon Group, LLC, a private equity and advisory firm where he served as president. Before that, he spent more than two decades as a partner at EY, serving in roles including global vice chair of corporate development and global managing partner of operations and finance. He also oversaw the firm’s finance and accounting, corporate development, tax, and treasury functions. Before joining EY, he was a managing director and head of the investment banking division at Wedbush Securities Inc. A.J. Silver was promoted to CFO of CRISPR Therapeutics (Nasdaq: CRSP), effective Oct. 19. Silver succeeds Raju Prasad, who is stepping down from the company to pursue external opportunities. Silver has served as the company’s SVP of finance since January 2025. Since joining CRISPR Therapeutics in October 2018, he has held roles of increasing responsibility spanning strategic finance, treasury, financial planning and analysis, accounting and reporting, tax, and financial operations. Before joining the company, Silver spent more than 10 years at Ernst & Young LLP. Bank of America Institute’s latest research suggests that consumers are beginning to see some relief from rising insurance costs, although the improvement varies significantly by location and demographic group. Median payments for auto, homeowners and renters insurance rose just 1% year over year in 2026, down from 6% in 2025 and approximately 12% in 2024. However, the Institute’s October 19 report highlights persistent regional differences: major California metropolitan areas, along with Boston and New York, continued to experience sizable increases, while much of the Sun Belt saw smaller increases or declines. Florida recorded some of the steepest declines, potentially reflecting insurance reforms and consumer rebates. Younger consumers also continue to face greater cost pressures, with Gen Z insurance payments increasing nearly 6%. The findings suggest that while broader insurance inflation is moderating, affordability remains uneven, with regional market conditions and individual circumstances determining how much relief households actually experience. Mark Zuckerberg’s $100 billion-plus AI bet is about more than keeping pace with rivals—it’s about ensuring Meta never again has to build its business on someone else’s platform. That strategy is explored in a Fortune article by Molly A. Wallace, which examines how lessons from Meta’s long-running battles with Apple are shaping its push to build a full-stack AI ecosystem, from data centers and custom chips to personal AI agents that could reshape how people shop online. But the vision raises a critical question: could the AI tools Meta is building ultimately disrupt the advertising business that fuels nearly all its revenue? "If the upsides from AI are speculative, so are the downsides—and if the model works, it could reshape the AI market much as venture capital changed the risk calculus for starting a business." —Mehran Gul, author of "The New Geography of Innovation,"writes in a Fortune opinion piece. He was previously at the World Economic Forum and attended Yale where he was a Fulbright Scholar, Fox International Fellow, and TeachingFellow. Millennials are ‘kidulting’ through their parenthood stage, spending their free time on blind boxes and claw machines
2026-10-20

Millennials are ‘kidulting’ through their parenthood stage, spending their free time on blind boxes and claw machines

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Most arcades in the 1980s and ’90s had one. A glass box in the corner, stuffed with plush bears and off-brand cartoon characters, where you would throw away your money for the chance to claw at something you didn’t really want. The claw would drop, close on a bear’s head and lift it, then either lose its grip or hand you something else. This is the impulse behind the “blind box” economy, the most visible piece of a trend marketers call “kidulting,” adults buying the toys, games and experiences of childhood for themselves. A new Harris Poll report, shared with Fortune ahead of its release, measures how far it has spread. In a survey of 2,158 U.S. adults conducted June 25–27, 80% said they’d done something playful for their own enjoyment in the past year. Among millennials who’d done something playful in the past year, 92% spent money on it and 78% spent $100 or more. Fifty-seven percent of Gen Z and millennials bought a blind box or mystery box for themselves, compared with 16% of Gen X and boomers. “It’s got the same kind of psychological draw as a slot machine,” Libby Rodney, chief strategy officer at The Harris Poll, told Fortune in an interview. “It’s like a fun, physical lotto ticket that you can buy and then play with and then display.” The blind box economy is the claw machine scaled to a generation. It sells small, affordable gambles on joy, wrapped in the characters of childhood. Older millennials, born between 1978 and 1988 by BofA’s definition, spend more per customer on hobbies than any other generation, Fortune reported last month. BofA’s category covers arts and crafts, hobby shops, ski resorts, outdoor retailers, educational toys and some toy stores. It excludes video games, golf and travel, so it is not a direct measure of blind-box buying. In the three months through August, they spent a little more than twice what Gen Z did per customer, according to BofA card data, and a larger share of them have hobby spending than any other generation. Overall hobby spending rose 7.9% year-over-year in August, more than double the 3.4% growth in transactions. Perhaps impressively, their free time is the shortest of any age group in BofA’s data. Americans ages 35 to 44 average about four hours and 15 minutes of leisure a day, the lowest of the age groups cited in the report. “Older Millennials seem to have more in common with younger Gen Xers than they do with Gen Z or even younger Millennials,” Bank of America Institute economist Joe Wadford previously told Fortune. The data can’t show how much of that money goes to their own collections versus their kids’ hobbies, but BofA says it likely reflects both. Rodney sees a positive story in the numbers. “I see this as kind of Americans taking a deep exhale and wanting to play,” she told Fortune. It’s emerging as nothing less than a form of self-care in the nostalgia-soaked, screen-riddled economy of the 2020s. Nostalgia is a big draw for millennials, the Harris survey found. Among millennials, 87% say they feel more emotionally connected to a brand when it taps into their childhood memories, and 86% appreciate it when brands make adult versions of things they loved as kids. Asked what makes a product feel designed for adults rather than kids, 39% of Americans picked “nostalgic references adults understand.” A separate Harris survey for printing company Quad, released Oct. 14, found 68% of Americans collect at least one type of physical item, including toys and figures (26%) and trading cards (22%). The top reason was nostalgia, cited by 46%. The toy industry has rebuilt itself around the grown-up buyer. Adult-only households accounted for 55% of U.S. toy sales in the first half of 2026, according to Circana, and that segment grew 16% through June. “At a demographic level, the toy industry’s growth continues to be fueled increasingly by older consumers,” Circana said, as reported by Retail Dive. Wall Street sees it in earnings, too. Hasbro reported Tuesday that revenue at Wizards of the Coast, home of Magic: The Gathering, rose 31% in the third quarter, and that Magic topped $600 million in quarterly revenue for the first time. Wizards produced $367 million of Hasbro’s $517 million in operating profit, according to its earnings release. Even Toys”R”Us, which liquidated its U.S. stores in 2018 after a 2017 bankruptcy filing, plans to open 120 new standalone stores this holiday season. Arcades are coming back, too, Rodney said: claw-machine stores are opening in New York, Los Angeles and other big cities, Rodney said, with 20 or 30 machines on a single retail floor. “You spend like $70 for 20 minutes and get these mini toys,” she said. “It is a really fun, actually, relief.” Survey respondents describe a similar effect. Fifty-two percent of Americans, and 66% of Gen Z and millennials, call kidulting one of their main forms of self-care. Half of Americans said they’d rather buy blind boxes, build models or collect toys than talk to a therapist. “Therapy is expensive,” Rodney said, suggesting that kidulting “can give you that same kind of relief.” The survey measured what people prefer, not whether play works as a substitute for treatment. Rodney also framed the trend as a backlash against the past decade’s grind and her own generation’s infamous tendency to work themselves to the bone. “We’ve moved away from hustle culture, and even if we hustle, we don’t talk about it anymore,” she said. People are pushing back against “optimization and maxing culture,” she said, and “the idea that everything we do has to be monetized.” Eighty-three percent of Americans agree that “in uncertain times, small moments of joy feel more important than ever,” and 74% say play makes them feel more hopeful when the world feels heavy. That rises to 80% among millennials. Rodney also said there are some clearly healthy benefits to this trend. “I think a lot of this money is being redirected from bars and other things into play,” Rodney said. Fifty-three percent of Gen Z and millennials would rather do kidult things than go out for drinks. The third draw is physical. After a decade of living through their phones, consumers want things they can touch. In the Quad survey, 84% of Americans said owning physical items feels more satisfying than owning digital ones, and 80% said they’re drawn to real-world experiences that don’t revolve around screens. Seventy-five percent said a simpler relationship with technology feels like a lifestyle upgrade, not a sacrifice. Ernesto Pagano, global sector leader for media at Boston Consulting Group, sees the same shift in his firm’s research. “There is now a social stigma about spending too much time on screens,” he told Fortune. A recent BCG report led by Pagano estimates that “location-based entertainment,” the category that includes theme parks, has the highest revenue yield of any type of media, averaging $3.47 per hour of consumer time, about nine times video’s 39 cents. BCG also asked consumers which media they regretted spending time on, he said. Many named social media and gaming. “What you find is they don’t regret the experiences.” Rodney said she thinks collecting is partly about proving you exist offline. Younger consumers, she said, “are just collecting things because they want to showcase that they have a physical presence in the world.” Adults playing with toys used to be a punchline. Rodney pointed to The 40-Year-Old Virgin, the 2005 comedy whose hero’s action-figure collection was the joke. That was “basically making fun of kidulting,” she said. “Whereas now kidulting is mainstream. It’s cool.” Some embarrassment lingers. Forty-nine percent of Americans, and 60% of millennials, sometimes feel judged for enjoying things considered childish. But only 12% say embarrassment stops them from spending more. Critics see something darker. Author Keith Hayward, a University of Copenhagen criminologist, argued in his 2024 book Infantilised: How Our Culture Killed Adulthood that Western culture has stopped people from growing up. Rodney rejected the premise. “The idea that people can be multidimensional and have careers, mortgages and families and still want to play with Legos and go to theme parks isn’t a horrible thing,” she said. “There’s just so many more ways to adult in this world than we’ve ever imagined.” There are daunting economics behind the retreat. The median first-time homebuyer in the U.S. is now 40, the oldest on record, and first-time buyers made up just 21% of purchases, a record low, according to the National Association of Realtors’ 2025 Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers. Fifty-one percent of millennials told Harris they’d rather spend on kidult things than save the money. Among everyone who plays, 46% said affordability makes them more likely to buy, second only to relaxation. Generative AI was used for research assistance and/or transcription of this article.
