Theranos whistleblower barely recognizes Elizabeth Holmes in new documentary: ‘The Elizabeth that I knew completely commanded a room’
2026-10-20

Theranos whistleblower barely recognizes Elizabeth Holmes in new documentary: ‘The Elizabeth that I knew completely commanded a room’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
A once-commanding CEO now looks broken. But according to one of the whistleblowers who helped expose Theranos, Elizabeth Holmes hasn’t stopped trying to control the narrative. The new documentary You Can See Everything, directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, is drawing crowds to theaters. It earned an estimated $15 million in its opening weekend, the second-biggest documentary debut ever, behind only Michael Moore’s Fahrenheit 9/11. The film follows Holmes in the final weeks before she reported to prison in 2023. Years after the Theranos scandal erupted, Holmes still maintains she never lied, despite her 2022 conviction for defrauding investors. Tyler Shultz, a former Theranos research engineer who helped expose the company’s faulty blood-testing technology, barely recognized the woman on screen. “The Elizabeth that I knew completely commanded a room, even if that room was full of secretaries of state, secretaries of defense, generals, senators. She would own a room,” Shultz told CBS Mornings on Monday. Now, he said, she looks like “a broken woman.” But he still doesn’t feel she’s owning up to her mistakes. In one scene, Holmes lays out the components of a Theranos blood-testing device on a kitchen table. She tells Fielder it could run hundreds of tests if the necessary chemistry were developed. Shultz said that when he saw the scene in the theater, his wife had to stop him from shouting “THAT IS NOT THE DEVICE,” he wrote in an opinion essay published Monday in STAT. According to Shultz, Holmes showed a future version of the product that was never completed and never used on patients. The Edison machine he worked with 12 hours a day was different, he said. It could run only one blood test at a time, constantly failed quality controls, and gave patients inaccurate results. Instead of pressing on what the machine actually did, Fielder ended up debating what Holmes meant by “could.” Was she describing technology that already worked, or technology that might work someday? Shultz wrote that either Holmes “bested” Fielder “in their game of chess” or Fielder let her mislead the audience. Fielder has said the experience left him baffled. “I still am not sure to this day what I experienced,” he said at a Q&A after an early screening, People reported. He also said he hoped audiences could “help me understand what I went through.” Holmes has long cultivated the image of a work-obsessed founder who cared more about changing health care than making money and lived modestly despite her fortune. In a 2014 CNN interview, she said being a billionaire “hasn’t changed my life at all” and that it was “not why we’re doing this.” In the documentary, she and her partner, Billy Evans, live with their two young children in a modest-looking San Diego rental. Shultz criticized the film for not looking “far beyond the walls” of that home. And the home wasn’t exactly modest: Realtor.com estimates the oceanfront property is worth $5.8 million. During her trial, Holmes lived on the grounds of the sprawling Green Gables estate in Woodside, Calif., which was listed for $135 million at the time. Shultz pointed to an even earlier version of the modest-living story. In 2014, The New Yorker described Holmes as living in a two-bedroom Palo Alto condo, with little time for friends or dating. Shultz said she was actually living in an Atherton mansion with Sunny Balwani, then her boyfriend and a Theranos executive. Then there’s the argument over who created the character of Elizabeth Holmes. In a 2023 New York Times profile, Holmes was quoted saying that actors portraying her were “playing a character I created.” In the documentary, Holmes denies saying that. She insists she said the actors created the character themselves. She and Evans say they recorded most of their media interviews, but they couldn’t produce the recording when Fielder asked for it. The Times stands by its reporting. Shultz doesn’t dismiss Holmes’ suffering. He wrote that he believes she was abused by Balwani, but that being a victim doesn’t excuse her own conduct. Shultz has said in the past that he was so afraid of Theranos that he slept with a knife under his pillow. Once a billionaire, Holmes is now serving a federal prison sentence in Texas. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Mural, a startup founded by former Palantir engineers, raises $15 million bid to connect banks to the emerging bot economy
2026-10-20

Mural, a startup founded by former Palantir engineers, raises $15 million bid to connect banks to the emerging bot economy

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
The stablecoin boom has promised to make global commerce faster and cheaper by easing cross-border money transfers. But delivering on that promise remains complicated, as businesses still have to juggle banks, payment providers, and a patchwork of regulations. New York-based startup Mural wants to fix that problem by offering businesses one place to manage international payments, rather than having to piece together the system themselves. Cofounded by former Palantir engineers Sinclair Toffa and Chris Fernandes in 2022, Mural announced Tuesday that it has raised a $15 million Series A funding round. The startup plans to use the funds to further build out its platform, which allows customers—including payroll providers, software companies and fintechs—to rely on a single account that can hold traditional dollars, local currencies, and stablecoins. “[We are] building this… compliant system of record that allows for businesses and fintechs to launch new financial products on top of us,” Toffa, who serves as Mural’s CEO, explained. Fintech innovation has reshaped how money moves in recent years, and stablecoins have become part of that shift. These cryptocurrencies, typically designed to track the U.S. dollar, can move across borders in seconds, sometimes only for pennies in network fees. Stablecoin payments reached roughly $400 billion in the first eight months of 2026, up from the same period a year earlier, per a report from global consulting firm Alvarez & Marsal. Their use for international transfers has also grown: cross-border stablecoin flows totaled $220 billion from July 2025 through June 2026, up nearly 78% from the previous 12 months, according to blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis. Tether, the issuer behind the USDT stablecoin, led Mural’s Series A, with participation from North Island Ventures. The round brings Mural’s total funding to $28 million, following a $5 million seed round in 2022 and additional pre-seed and interim financing from earlier backers. Mural declined to disclose its valuation following its latest fundraising round. Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said the investment also reflects a bet on AI agents becoming regular handlers of financial services. “Those agents will need access to payment systems that operate around the clock and can reach people across the world,” he told Fortune in a written statement. Toffa’s interest in international payments began when he visited Togo, his grandfather’s home country, when he was 15 years old. His relatives there, who routinely received remittances from his father in the United States, told him that transfers could take weeks to arrive and cost as much as 20% in fees. That experience stayed with Toffa when he later became interested in blockchain and smart contract technology while completing his computer science degree at Duke University. In 2018, Toffa joined Palantir in its London office as a software engineer, visiting large companies to help connect computer systems that couldn’t easily share information. He said the work informed his approach to Mural, where businesses face a similar challenge connecting banks, payment providers and stablecoins. While at Palantir, Toffa met Fernandes, who also became his roommate. Toffa later approached him about his idea for Mural. Fernandes was quick to get on board, and the two eventually left their jobs to focus on the company. The founders named the company Mural, a nod to communities painting stories together—and a metaphor for businesses building their own financial services on shared software. Besides its New York headquarters, Mural has offices in Denver, Colorado, and Bogotá, Colombia. Its 30 employees are spread across those locations as well as Argentina, Chile, Ecuador, and Mexico. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Currently the live commerce platform employs roughly 130 people across Ireland, with plans to add across a number of its teams. Whatnot, a live commerce platform with a presence in North America, the UK and Europe, has officially opened its new European headquarters in Dublin. The company enables individuals and small business owners to sell their products via a live video feed, where they can show their wares, answer questions and take orders. The organisation employs more than 130 people in Ireland and intends to double its workforce over the course of the next 18 months, as well as open its platform to small Irish businesses. Days after the incident, a group of hackers claiming responsibility for the breach demanded a ransom of $3m in Monero, a cryptocurrency, from the fintech. What is the ‘software tax’ and how is it affecting tech budgets?
2026-10-20

What is the ‘software tax’ and how is it affecting tech budgets?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
New research from Origina suggests that companies may unknowingly be misusing their budgets when it comes to upgrading technologies. In the world of technology, everything breaks down eventually, meaning that repairs, maintenance and sometimes even a complete overhaul of systems are necessary. However, knowing when a technology needs to be upgraded can be tricky, especially if that technology is relatively new, or are the metrics used to gauge its performance. For its ‘The Software Tax: What does it cost and who benefits?’ report, Dublin-based IT and software company Origina, in partnership with Thinkers50, commissioned Censuswide to survey 2,018 CIOs and senior IT decision makers. The decision-makers who contributed are at director level or above with direct ownership or influence over enterprise software budgets and renewals. They are employed at organisations with an annual revenue of at least $1bn, or with 10,000 or more global employees. Participants to the survey were from the UK and the US, spanning five sectors: financial services, insurance, telecommunications, the public sector and retail. What was discovered by the research is that there is essentially a ‘software tax’ that accompanies the implementation of new or advanced technologies within organisations. The report defined this as “a recurring burden embedded in the enterprise software model that consumes capacity, diverts talent, wastes working assets, and adds cost and risk that organisations have little practical ability to refuse”. That is to say that, despite organisations implementing ambitious enterprise software strategies as a means of becoming more efficient, many are imposing hefty hidden costs upon themselves. The Origina and Thinkers50 report found that large enterprises are spending almost 30pc of their software budget on upgrades, migrations and licensing changes that they would otherwise not have actively chosen. Shockingly, over the course of the last three years, 95pc of contributing IT leaders explained that their organisation has funded a vendor-driven upgrade, which they believed at the time would negatively impact performance, reliability or business value. More than half (53pc) have done so on more than one occasion. Commenting on the report, Tomás O’Leary, the CEO and founder of Origina and a co-author of the research, said, “When the alternative to an unwanted upgrade is losing support on systems the business runs on, saying yes is often a rational response. “That’s exactly the problem. The market has left some of the most experienced technology leaders in the world with little room to refuse and that cost shows up in budgets, in engineering time and in innovation that never gets funded. We want to make that cost visible so customers can start to take back control of it.” Some companies may be feeling the pressure to ‘give in’ to repeated upgrade requests as a means of maintaining newer systems – and the impact can be felt by the people using those systems most often. The report found that 84pc of respondents believe senior engineering professionals spend a significant amount of time on vendor-driven updates, rather than committing their time to “creating new value”. More than 90pc have gotten rid of functional hardware in the past three years in order to meet a software vendor’s support deadline, losing an average of 1.65 years of useful hardware life. “Economists have studied this problem for decades. Once a customer makes relationship-specific investments, the vendor gains leverage that no contract can fully anticipate,” said Marshall Van Alstyne, a professor at Boston University and a co-author of the report. “For CIOs, the lesson is simple – negotiate the exit before the entry. You don’t have to leave a vendor to benefit from being able to.” The report highlighted a number of strategies that could be integral to reestablishing an ecosystem in which there is greater choice for enterprise leaders. Firstly, it is important to understand that the greatest leverage you have exists prior to the signing of a contract. Before committing to anything in a legal sense, address future problems and settle the terms. These can include confirming migration assistance at a predetermined price; the right to use independent software maintenance; sufficient notice of end-of-support decisions and the right to remain on a current version; caps on renewal and expansion prices; minimum support periods; and continued, documented API access. Secondly, be prepared to spend more earlier on to establish a system that is easier to navigate over time, or even leave behind later on, on your own terms. The report said: “Investments in abstraction layers, data portability, standardised APIs, containerisation, open source, open standards and modularity can make future switching easier.” The report also recommended to maintain alternatives – a system does not have to be used all of the time to hold its value, and having independent third-party support and ensuring technical teams have an awareness of competing technologies can be helpful in the long run. On reinstating a sense of balance, the report stated that the topic rests on a fundamental principle of competition – the customer’s ability to say no. “Exercising that choice starts with one question, asked of every vendor-driven change before sign-off: ‘Who benefits?’” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Offaly engineer’s cooling bracelet named one of Time’s best inventions
2026-10-20

Offaly engineer’s cooling bracelet named one of Time’s best inventions

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
From left: Aonghus O’Donovan and Maxime Kryvian. Image: MyCelsius ‘Every part of it was shaped by the women we worked closely with throughout its development,’ Aonghus O’Donovan said. A cooling wearable co-developed by Offaly-native engineer Aonghus O’Donovan has made it onto Time magazine’s coveted annual best inventions list, just a year after it first hit the market. MyCelsius, the UK-based company behind the wearable, was founded by chief technology officer O’Donovan and CEO Maxime Kryvian. O’Donovan studied mechanical engineering at the University of Limerick before working for well-known brands including Dyson. The patent-pending cooling bracelet was developed over three years with input from women going through perimenopause and menopause, along with specialist doctors and thermal health specialists. The device incorporates a small system which uses thermoelectric cooling to lower skin temperature by 10 degrees Celsius within 10 seconds, and 20 degrees during a three-minute session, according to the company. A ‘night mode’ offers up to eight hours of cooling during sleep, it added. Time magazine’s prestigious annual list of 300 best inventions is compiled through nominations from its editors and correspondents, as well as via an online application process. The MyCelsius cooling bracelet’s placement in the health and wellness category is “incredible”, the CEO said. “For too long, millions of women (and men) experiencing hot flushes have been expected to simply put up with them,” said Kryvian. “We set out to change that by developing something practical, discreet and backed by science that is genuinely useful.” The device, sold in Europe, is set for its US launch next year. “There’s a lot of engineering packed into something you hardly notice you’re wearing, and every part of it was shaped by the women we worked closely with throughout its development,” said O’Donovan. Last year, Irish health-tech Akara’s AI sensor for hospitals found itself on the coveted Time magazine list. In 2019, its robot Stevie was also a list entrant, even making it onto the cover of the magazine. US plans to remove DeepSeek from government agency systems
2026-10-20

US plans to remove DeepSeek from government agency systems

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Amid growing tensions with China, the US is reportedly wary of the ‘multiple threat vectors’ possibly created by the use of DeepSeek. The US Department of Homeland Security (DHS) has ordered that US civilian agency IT systems remove all DeepSeek products and any third-party services using the AI model. The DHS notice, published yesterday (19 October), explained that the US’s federal civilian agencies will no longer purchase information technology created by Hangzhou DeepSeek Artificial Intelligence, or third-party companies connected to it. Furthermore, federal agencies will remove any DeepSeek-related products that are already being used, as well as any that were developed using DeepSeek’s artificial intelligence. According to Reuters, the DHS cited security concerns as a major consideration, stating that “multiple threat vectors” could potentially “result in the transfer of ​sensitive data to [China] if used by US government employees ⁠or vendors”. Potentially, by using DeepSeek, government agencies could expose existing intelligence on US personnel and agencies, the DHS said, adding that models may also harbour malware and other security threats. While the notice was only published this week, the actual ban took effect back in July of this year. Among the affected agencies are the Department of the Treasury, the Department of State and the Department of Justice. Reportedly, agencies within the Department of Defense are excluded. Previously, ​multiple US bodies – for example, the Department of Commerce and the Navy – had banned ​the use of DeepSeek on government devices. States such as New York, Tennessee and Virginia have ‌taken ⁠similar steps to prevent use of the company’s technology. There is growing tension between the US and China, as both countries are keen to pull ahead in the global AI race. President Donald Trump has been increasingly critical of the threat China’s AI innovation poses to the US’s own AI ambitions. In June, it was announced that DeepSeek, which intends to move to an IPO, also has plans to double the size of all of its departments amid a push to compete with domestic rivals and global leaders in the artificial intelligence space. Around this time, the AI start-up raised more than $7.4bn at a $50bn-plus post-money valuation. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Futurism
We Regret to Inform You That “GatGPT” Is Exactly What It Sounds Like
2026-10-20

We Regret to Inform You That “GatGPT” Is Exactly What It Sounds Like

Futurism | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Someone vibe-coded a gun and 3D-printed it — and wants the freedom loving citizens of the world to enjoy this power, too. The creator, gun-rights activist Cody Wilson, told The Verge that it’s the “first AI-made gun” — his timely followup to the world’s first 3D-printed firearm that he crafted 13 years ago. Called the WASP, the .22 caliber weapon’s unconventional design aptly looks like it might’ve been hallucinated by an old image model, except it apparently actually works and is capable of firing real ammunition. But the WASP is arguably just a proof-of-concept of his more consequential creation: his very own AI gun generator called “GatGPT,” which was trained on an online library of millions of digital guns and gun parts called DEFCAD (itself launched by Wilson). It will answer any question about firearms you can throw at it and pick out parts for your build, potentially allowing even someone who doesn’t know a clip from a magazine to design their own gun. “I would argue that what we’ve made is not an AI-generated gun, but an AI that can generate guns,” Wilson told The Verge. Crucially, Wilson’s WASP doesn’t have a serial number like a traditional firearm. If his AI gunsmith tool works as it’s made out to be, it could be a huge escalation in the advent of so-called “ghost guns,” or homemade firearms that are made outside of government oversight and can’t be easily traced. Some are available as pre-packaged do-it-yourself kits, while others may be custom-designed. (Luigi Mangione used one to shoot UnitedHealthcare CEO Brian Thompson.) This is almost certainly Wilson’s point. He considers access to firearms to be a “fundamental human right.” He claims he made WASP through simple natural language conversations with GatGPT, and so anyone else could do the same, even if it’s not legal in their state. “[Wilson] admits the goal here,” Justin Wagner, senior vice president for law and policy at the gun safety group Everytown, told The Verge. “He wants to lower the barrier to entry so anyone, regardless of their background check status or legal eligibility [can] own a gun. He wants anyone to be able to design and manufacture weapons, and that’s dangerous.” Recent efforts to limit the proliferation of these DIY weapons have hit a few snags. In August, a federal judge struck down the ATF’s “ghost gun” rule adopted under the Biden administration that required 3D-printed gun kits and parts to be marked with serial numbers and for their manufacturers to conduct background checks on customers. New York and California, though, passed laws that require 3D printer companies to install their products with gun blocking software, which have proven controversial in the 3D-printing community. Google’s DeepMind Quietly Hiring Nuclear Physicists
2026-10-20

Google’s DeepMind Quietly Hiring Nuclear Physicists

Futurism | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Top AI labs have been hiring subject matter experts across academic disciplines for years at this point — often turning them into glorified fact-checkers whose labor is used to train the AI models of tomorrow. Still, Google’s latest job listing is odd, even by those admittedly strange standards, and may reveal a lot about the future big tech has in mind for humanity. As reported by the tech industry publication Data Center Dynamics, Google’s AI research lab, DeepMind is now hiring a team of “highly qualified physicists and nuclear engineers” for the role of “nuclear subject matter expert in the Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear, and high-yield Explosives team within the Responsible Development and Innovation team.” According to the job listing, interested candidate should have a PhD in a field like plasma physics or nuclear engineering, or else “equivalent practical experience,” on top of experience collaborating with a US government lab and familiarity with policy related to “stockpile stewardship, nonproliferation, arms control, or threat reduction” — and that’s just at a minimum. Ideally, Google says it would prefer its candidates to also have experience to have experience working with AI safety, as well as expertise in computational modeling, experience working at “major laser user facilities,” or the ability to design evaluations and mitigations that are “demonstrably unclassified and non-export controlled by developing dual use science proxies for weapons topics.” The lucky candidates will be offered a salary in the ballpark of $174,000 to $252,000, with a 15 percent bonus on top. The job won’t be easy, however. As the listing notes, the role involves evaluating safety risks of so-called “frontier AI models” in the “nuclear domain.” Specifically, this means “model assistance in fission, fusion, and thermonuclear weapons proliferation, radiation hydrodynamics, magnetohydrodynamics, etc. in a demonstrably unclassified and non-export controlled manner.” Put together, it reads as if Google’s top AI research lab expects the company’s AI tools to start churning out actionable instructions for developing nuclear devices, and is now hiring nuclear physicists to help spearhead the defense efforts. How serious the scenario is remains to be seen — but Google is clearly willing to put its money where its mouth is. Wait, Was Elon’s “Flying Car” Just an Empty Shell?
2026-10-20

Wait, Was Elon’s “Flying Car” Just an Empty Shell?

Futurism | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Flying cars are right around the corner, if Tesla CEO Elon Musk is to be believed. Last week, he unveiled the automaker’s long-awaited Roadster by having it drive to the top of a ramp, flipping upside down in the process, then fling itself into the air before using its cold-fuel thrusters to gently land in style. Minutes later, another Roadster did its best Evel-Knievel-with-a-jetpack impression by blowing itself into the air and leaping over a row of six Tesla vehicles, including its Semi Truck cab. Sure, the Roadster can “fly.” There’s no denying that it was an impressive stunt. But huge questions remain, such as whether regulators will ever deem it road — let alone air — worthy. Another is if the cars used in the stunt will remotely resemble the actual product that’s sold to consumers, if such a thing does ever come to pass. Tesla says the “Rocket” version of its Roadster is loaded with 600 liters of compressed air, 10 directional cold-gas thruster nozzles, and a huge suction-fan system on the bottom. To make room for it all, Tesla says it removed the passenger seat. But some observers are wondering if the car is even more stripped down than that, perhaps even lacking a full-sized battery so the engineers could save room and cut down weight. Meanwhile, screenshots of the car’s sparse looking underside taken while it was midair seemed to show it lacking a suction fan, fueling further speculation that Tesla hasn’t figured out to fully combine its capabilities as they’re being presented. The car that stuck upside down to the top of the ramp and then cushioned its landing was, some skeptics allege, equipped with different hardware from the one that leaped into the air to leapfrog the other cars. Practical questions abound. Without a full-sized battery, the car obviously wouldn’t be able to drive around on roads even if it were allowed to. None other than computer scientist and “godfather” of AI Yann LeCun derided the Roadster’s aerial acrobatics display, calling it a “marketing stunt” and questioning its authenticity. “Even assuming that 50 percent of the mass of the car is the compressed air, that gives you less than 10s of useful thrust (with thrust-to-weight ratio larger than 1.0). Probably more like 5 seconds, actually,” he wrote on X. “So the car must be an empty, lightweight shell containing big compressed air tanks. Cute demo, but totally impractical.” A little bit of misleading showmanship certainly isn’t below Musk. Tesla stunned the world in 2016 with a promotional video of its self-driving car puttering along public roads and dutifully stopping for red lights. Musk boasted that it was clear evidence that “Tesla drives itself.” But later, its engineers admitted the whole thing was staged. At its glitzy “We, Robot” event in 2024, Tesla tried to wow investors with its Optimus humanoid robots that poured drinks, danced on stage, and mingled with attendees while holding down a conversation. But it quickly became apparent that what Tesla had unleashed weren’t real-life C-3POs, because the seemingly autonomous robots were in reality individually teleoperated. In any case, it’s impossible to verify what Musk’s showing off with the Roadster since Tesla isn’t allowing the public — journalists included — to take a peek under the hood. Recipe Scammers Are Using an AI-Generated Emily Blunt as a Slop Puppet to Promote Their Garbage
2026-10-20

Recipe Scammers Are Using an AI-Generated Emily Blunt as a Slop Puppet to Promote Their Garbage

Futurism | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
A new report exposes thousands of scam websites flooding the web with AI-generated recipes. In the report, Decrypt Ads — a startup that tracks the supply chains of online advertisements — identified more than two thousand websites that strongly appear to be AI slop farms masquerading as blogs run by real chefs and home cooks. These sites, the report found, are pumping fake recipes into search engines like Google as well as social media platforms such as Pinterest, often relying on sketchy Russian and Chinese adtech partners to monetize the operations. It’s “common knowledge that publishers in certain niches are being openly deceptive about their owners and authors, using AI photos and fake back stories on their sites,” reads the report, which was published today. “Some of the most consistent growth in AI slop spreading across the internet has come from AI recipe websites.” Security expert and Decrypt Ads researcher Zach Edwards told Futurism that he initially identified about 400 suspicious websites that seemed to be publishing AI-generated recipes under the names of fake chefs. Using “technical fingerprints,” he then found hundreds more sites associated with the suspicious domains he manually uncovered — bringing the tally up to roughly 2,100 different domains, many of which seem to be run by the same sloperators. “We really approach this expecting these operators to try and hide their websites,” said Edwards. “These folks they don’t want people to know all of the websites they own.” The slop recipe sites often have “aggressive” social media strategies, according to the report, with Edwards noting that the bogus brands are “super aggressive on Pinterest, super aggressive on Twitter, on Facebook.” “Any place where there’s visual media,” he added, “you can expect these AI author people to be trying to drum up organic traffic.” Among the most aggressive slop sites uncovered in the report is “AddCakes,” a baking site that claims it’s operated by a baker named “Elena.” Elena is described as a “busy mom who wanted to connect with my roots through baking, but got overwhelmed by complicated cake recipes and the pressure to make them look perfect.” But in an act of theft so bold that it’s comical, the character of Elena is clearly an AI-generated version of the extremely recognizable British actress Emily Blunt, who’s starred in hit movies like the 2006 “The Devil Wears Prada” and the 2018 “A Quiet Place.” AddCakes is apparently either betting that no one will recognize AI-manipulated images of the famous celebrity, or banking that her face will attract readers. It’s even published photos depicting the Elena character in the kitchen cooking and sitting at a dinner table, surrounded by her fake, AI-generated family. According to location data from X-formerly-Twitter, the person behind the operation is based in Morocco. (Blunt doesn’t live in Morocco, and her representatives didn’t respond to a request for comment.) Whatever you do, though, don’t trust the AI-generated Emily Blunt. As Edwards emphasized, websites working with Chinese and Russian adtech partners carry a privacy risk for consumers, as authoritarian regimes may look to hoover up valuable browsing data. “This report is about publishers who are being deceptive. Their audiences could be impacted, advertisers are wasting money,” said Edwards. “But the underlying fact is also that if you load this website and you don’t block ads, your data is going to, basically, authoritarian countries.” In other words, AI slop isn’t just choking the web with garbage. It can also come with security risks — and, at the end of the day, it’s built on fundamental dishonesty. “Most of these AI recipe websites, when you look at those ‘about’ pages, the fake backstories that they’re putting together… this lady was a Michelin star chef and she worked at this fancy hotel or this place, and they’re basically saying that this expert not only wrote these recipes, but tested themselves. And it’s giving this air of, ‘Wow, you’re about to get a recipe from an expert. This has all been carefully vetted,'” said Edwards. “But none of these recipes have probably been cooked — zero of them by the actual owners or authors, and to me that’s the scary part.” He added that many of these sites are likely scraping content ideas from real bloggers and creators who have actually taken the time, energy, and expertise to test, photograph, and publish real recipes. “These people are clearly not cooking their own recipes. They’re putting out sometimes dozens of recipes a day. It’s an automated publishing pipeline,” said the researcher. “It’s all a lie… and I think that the reason why we need to be holding AI publishers accountable is because they don’t care about their audience.” More on fake authors and the web: Google Updates Guidelines to Punish Sites That Use Fake Bylines and AI-Generated Headshots

TikTok was repeatedly warned by employees about the risks the social media platform posed to children, according to court records obtained NPR. Despite Figma co-founder Dylan Field’s concerns about MCP (model context protocol), which standardizes how AI agents connect to external data sources and tools, the company is expanding its support for the open source standard. At the design company’s first Config conference held in India, the company announced 20 new model context protocol (MCP) partners, including Amp, ClickUp, Dia, Junie by JetBrains, Linear, Meta Muse, Mistral, Notion, OpenCode, and Sarvam Agents. Field has expressed his misgivings about MCP before. Days before this announcement, one of MCP’s co-creators complained about Figma not supporting the open-source tool Pi. As part of his response, Field made a comment about Anthropic not making its various resources available via MCP. “I find it interesting that Anthropic (creators of MCP standard) do not expose artifacts / other cloud resources created via MCP,” he wrote on X. “The debate is framed as open vs closed. Everyone always prefers open… obviously. And meanwhile issues of asymmetric context sharing are ignored.” (Field later added that Figma plans support Pi.) Can you help me understand why you don't make various Anthropic resources available via MCP? Maybe I'm missing something. At a media briefing, I asked Field about this debate. The co-founder explained that while Figma wants to be open, he doesn’t think MCP is a perfect protocol. “I’m really excited about what we’re doing in terms of opening up our ecosystem. I also think that MCP is not a perfect protocol. And I think that it is something that has tons of room for improvement,” the co-founder responded. “It is advantaged more to frontier labs. Also, I understand everyone online who just wants a protocol that they can use when they want to use their Figma data, and I want them to use it, too.” Figma’s CEO said that the reason the company doesn’t want to be fully open is that the more data the company exposes through MCP, the higher the chance of abuse. He also clarified that while most users don’t intend to use MCP or Figma data in a malicious way, the company still has to think about those potential risks. SpaceX alum’s aviation startup, Reliable Robotics, lands $115M, topping $1B valuation
2026-10-20

SpaceX alum’s aviation startup, Reliable Robotics, lands $115M, topping $1B valuation

TechCrunch | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Reliable Robotics has been working for nearly a decade to commercialize autonomous flying technology that can be installed on existing aircraft. Now, with a $115 million funding boost, the Mountain View, California-based startup has a longer runway as it pushes to get its autonomous flight system certified by the Federal Aviation Administration — a critical milestone that co-founder and CEO Robert Rose said the startup is inching toward. The financing is an extension of its Series D round and includes $90 million in equity funding led by Nimble Partners and $25 million in debt from Silicon Valley Bank. The Series D round, which now totals $275 million, has pushed the startup’s valuation beyond the $1 billion mark. Other investors include Gideon Strategic Partners, RockCreek, Geek Ventures, Gold Hill Capital, Sparkwave Capital and others. Reliable has raised $380 million to date. “We continue to make steady progress and deliver evidence to the FAA and every day, we inch closer and closer,” said Rose, a former director of flight software at SpaceX who has also had stints at Tesla and Google. Reliable co-founder and CTO Juerg Frefel is also a SpaceX veteran who spent nearly a decade there as an engineer. And in aviation — one of the most regulated industries in the world — moving toward certification of new technology is progress. Reliable Robotics is working to gain FAA certification on four fronts, all of which are linked to its goal of autonomous flight. Its first product, called continuous autopilot engagement, handles the engine startup, taxiing, takeoff, and landing for the pilot, who remains in the aircraft and interacts with the autonomous flight system through a touchscreen. Rose expects to receive certification for this critical first system in 18 to 24 months. The second certification, which Rose says is at least three years away, removes the pilot from the cockpit and moves them to a ground-control station. Reliable is also working toward certification of a collision-avoidance radar, which the company has developed in-house after failing to attract legacy suppliers to take on the project. Most conventional radar systems use an antenna that moves to scan the surrounding airspace. Reliable has developed a solid-state radar that has no moving parts and scans the sky at a longer range and at much higher resolution, according to Rose. It’s also easier to maintain because there are no moving parts and it costs less, he added. “This is of great interest to operators these days, given the collisions that we’ve had recently in commercial aviation,” he said, adding that the radar also works on the ground to prevent collisions with other aircraft or infrastructure. Rose expects the solid-state radar to be certified by the end of 2027. The final certification is for its airline, called Reliable Airlines, which will be the first airline certified to use the autonomous flying technology. The airline is based in Albuquerque and uses six Cessna Caravans — workhorses of the aviation industry — to fly cargo six days a week. (The FAA certifies equipment, but the agency also must certify the airline operations under a separate set of regulations.) While the certification process is lengthy, Nimble Partners founder John Burbank said the benefits will be broad and significant. “It’s a much bigger effort and much bigger opportunity than to just build one plane,” Burbank said, noting that Reliable is building a system that can be adopted by any airframe. “They had a really big vision nine years ago, and saw this as something needed and essential. Once they get the first one approved, then everyone will realize this is going to be the path for the industry, just as we’re seeing the path for the auto industry being autonomous.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. US orders federal agencies to remove China’s DeepSeek AI from their systems

US orders federal agencies to remove China’s DeepSeek AI from their systems

TechCrunch | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The U.S. has ordered federal civilian agencies to stop purchasing services and products developed by Chinese AI startup DeepSeek and remove them from government IT systems over national security concerns tied to the Chinese AI startup’s operations in China, according to the Department of Homeland Security. The order goes beyond banning DeepSeek’s own product, but also covers third-party products and services built using DeepSeek technology, though agencies can request exceptions. The DHS raised concerns about DeepSeek’s handling of user data, warning that sensitive information from US government users could potentially be accessible to Chinese authorities under Chinese law, the order said. The move comes amid intensifying tech competition in AI between the US and China. The Chinese AI model challenges its U.S. rivals, including OpenAI and Meta, with low-cost open-weight models. China pushed back against the U.S.’s move. Foreign Ministry spokesperson Mao Ning said Tuesday that Beijing opposes “overstretching the concept of national security and politicizing technology and trade issues,” according to an official briefing. “We will resolutely safeguard the legitimate rights and interests of Chinese enterprises,” the spokesperson added. The U.S. has already taken steps to limit DeepSeek’s use in government. A law passed in December 2025 targeted its use in intelligence’s national security systems. The latest order extends the restrictions to federal civilian agencies, covering both new purchases and existing IT systems. Meanwhile, China is reportedly considering export restrictions on 23 AI-related technologies, including chip designs and certain advanced AI models, to address security and misuse risks, Bloomberg reported last week. The Chinese AI lab has faced scrutiny from governments and regulators across the globe since its R1 reasoning model shook the AI industry in January 2025, triggering a sharp sell-off in Nvidia shares. The U.S. accused DeepSeek and five other Chinese AI companies, including Alibaba and Moonshot AI, of using large-scale model distillation to extract information from American AI systems, according to a September report. In addition, OpenAI and Anthropic have separately raised concerns about DeepSeek’s alleged use of model distillation, a technique that uses outputs from one AI model to train another. Earlier this year, OpenAI told U.S. lawmakers it had observed suspected attempts by DeepSeek to distill its models, while Anthropic said it had identified unauthorized attempts by DeepSeek and other Chinese AI labs to collect outputs from Claude. Eka is worth $1.45B thanks to a physical AI model that has experts raving
2026-10-20

Eka is worth $1.45B thanks to a physical AI model that has experts raving

TechCrunch | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Robots still aren’t that good at picking things up, despite billions of dollars of investment and endless videos fed into deep learning models. That may be about to change, thanks to a manipulation architecture built by Eka Robotics, which encompasses a force-sensitive gripper and a new kind of deep-learning model. Founded by MIT professor Pulkit Agrawal and former Google DeepMind researcher Tuomas Haarnoja, the startup just closed an $150 million Series A round that values it at $1.45 billion post-money, led by venture funds a16z and F-Prime. “They are already exceeding superhuman performance for certain tasks, and so we think over the next few months there will be superhuman performance in many dexterous manipulation tasks,” Raghu Raguram, the a16z general partner who co-led the fundraising round, told TechCrunch. In an era of never-ending robotic demonstrations, Eka’s capabilities are shocking veteran robot builders. Kris Hauser, a professor at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, said it had a “wow factor” compared to other robot demos he saw recently. The Eka robot “was able to adapt to a large number of different objects without retraining,” Hauser said, adding that it also had an aspect of “humanness, creepy humanness that it would try over and over again if it failed the first time.” Another roboticist, who asked for anonymity because of their work at a frontier lab, told TechCrunch that seeing Eka’s robot in action was an emotional experience, comparable to when robots first achieved reliable legged locomotion five to ten years ago. Take a look for yourself: What’s notable here? How smoothly and rapidly the robot moves. It can pick up about 1,500 objects that weigh up to 1 kg in an hour, compared to about 600 for a human; Eka says it reliably picks up objects more than 95% of the time. And the robot arm is strong enough to hurt itself as it moves that quickly. That indicates, per the frontier lab observer, that “they have a lot of trust in their algorithm not breaking the robot. I wonder how many robots they broke along the way?” When the frontier lab roboticist met Eka’s robot, they handed it a raspberry, the lab’s apparent favorite test item. “The experience of a big, heavy robot rushing towards my arm and then slowing down just in time and very gently touching me is a feeling that I am, as we speak, carrying this feeling in my body,” they told TechCrunch. These capabilities are a consequence of how the foundational model was built. Rather than a vision-language-action model — which is trained to understand the relationship between language, visual inputs, and actions — the schema here is a vision-force-action model, which focuses on the relationship between language, force inputs, and actions. Critics of the current direction of generative AI robotics models, like Rodney Brooks, say that they will fail without an attempt to represent force and touch; Eka’s results suggest such critics have a point. “Vision is not the primary way in which you close this last millimeter problem,” Eka co-founder Agrawal told TechCrunch. “Vision tells you roughly where the object is, what the pose is, but the actual grabbing, the actual searching is happening through forces.” Eka had to design a unique robotic hand that was more flexible than traditional grippers, and included more force sensors; it reacts to contact, which is why it doesn’t crush the raspberry. The founders are cagey about how they achieved that for obvious competitive reasons, but it comes down to using a physics simulator to model the multiple forces the robot will encounter as it attempts to grasp something, and using that to create a reinforcement learning environment that can be run at scale. They say they can generate thousands of hours of robot experience in minutes. “Rather than being limited to the data that you’re collecting from, say, human demonstrations, Eka is pushing through reinforcement learning this approach where the robot can surpass any kind of demonstrations that are given to it,” Hauser said. “What’s really exciting is that they’re able to not be limited to the data that is presented to them for training.” Agrawal and Haarnoja, who first met in graduate school at the University of California, Berkeley, are leading researchers in the field of reinforcement learning for robotics control. Eka’s key bet was that this technique could be done at scale through simulation, rather than relying on videos collected by gigworkers or robot operators performing tasks over many hours. Simulation was key to achieving their robot’s reliable manipulation, a feat that in the past that has proven more difficult for tasks that require interacting with objects, rather than avoiding them. “What EKA is bridging is the generality-performance gap, which plagues a lot of modern robotics,” Agrawal argues. Right now, the most generalizable physical AI models—the ones that can attempt lots of different tasks—aren’t particularly good at them. But AI models trained to be highly capable at a single task may not close the business case for a company: The promise of generative AI is that robots can quickly learn multiple tasks, even novel ones. The current approaches are good at producing robots that are more generally intelligent than before — Astra, ChatGPT’s latest LLM, can operate a robot in an unfamiliar setting and perform tasks with it, but slowly and at a high cost. The promise of a model like Eka’s is that it handles the physicality while a more general model makes decisions about how to solve a problem. Astra, for example, might know that plates get stacked in a cupboard when they come out of the dishwasher, but Eka’s model is capable of picking up a dish without breaking it. “Most of the field has been focusing on the planning,” Eka co-founder Haarnoja says, “so they approach from the top: high-level intelligence, pushing that down towards the physical. Whereas we think about it from the bottom up.” Eka still needs to prove itself in the field, though. The company is working in secretive pilots with a number of industrial customers doing pick-and-place and wire manipulation, but the leap from lab to line is almost as big as the gap between sim and real. “What’s going to be a big challenge is when they work with integrators on real assembly lines, and it needs to run without breaking for an entire year,” Hauser said. The other open question is “‘how generalizable these techniques can be to different tasks?’ What I’ve seen is pick and place, but many industry tasks that are value added involve more than just pick-and-place.” Agrawal is also focused on deployability; right now, he says Eka’s gripper hardware — which costs less than $1,000 — is rated for 5 million duty cycles, but the ultimate goal is to hit the 20 million cycle bar set by standard industrial arms. “What we are building is that engineering of how to design entire workflows, but we are starting off being very pragmatic, and saying here are two verticals in pick, arrange, place, and wire handling, let’s make them be home runs,” Agrawal said. “Once we have made home runs on them, the sky is the limit in how much we can do with the approach we are developing.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Inspired Capital closes $450M fourth fund as New York pushes back on Silicon Valley’s gravitational pull

Inspired Capital closes $450M fourth fund as New York pushes back on Silicon Valley’s gravitational pull

TechCrunch | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Inspired Capital, the early-stage firm founded in 2019 by Alexa von Tobel and Penny Pritzker, has closed its fourth fund with $450 million in commitments. The new capital will bring the firm’s assets under management to nearly $1.4 billion, von Tobel tells TechCrunch. The raise comes as venture dollars are piling up on the West Coast. Between 2020 and the end of 2025, the Bay Area had raised $352 billion for VC funds — roughly 46% of all U.S. venture capital, according to PitchBook. Last year, that percentage shot up even higher, with Bay Area firms locking down 55% of the total new commitments to U.S. venture funds. New York-based Inspired’s new fund is a reminder that capital is accruing elsewhere, too. In fact, Inspired is growing: it closed a $330 million third fund in early 2024, following a $281 million second fund and its $200 million debut fund. Despite having money to invest, von Tobel says the firm is sticking to its formula. Inspired backs roughly 10 companies a year, which can be anywhere between “napkin” stage and Series A, writing checks between $1 million and $15 million. Pritzker, the former U.S. Commerce Secretary, remains an active co-founder in the firm. With six years of investing experience under her belt, von Tobel is now willing to talk about results, too. She says that 12 companies across the firm’s first two funds have become unicorns, representing about 15% of the portfolio. “Ten of them have passed $100 million in revenue, four have crossed $400 million and one has crossed $500 million,” she says. Perhaps more relevant to the firm’s limited partners, Inspired counts more than 10 exits, including MosaicML (acquired for $1.3 billion in June by Databricks), DeepTune (which Mercor acquired in July for an undisclosed amount), and Saturn (snapped up last year by Snap). Another portfolio company, TeamShares, a fintech startup that buys up other businesses, went public in June through a special purpose acquisition vehicle. Von Tobel claimed the firm’s second fund ranks in the top 5% of its cohort by all metrics, though she declined to share cash-on-cash returns, citing the relative newness of the firm. To be clear, Inspired is not squarely focused on New York. Its portfolio is split roughly 45% East Coast, 45% West Coast and 10% elsewhere. That balance reflects the way that startups grow, she says. “A company that’s getting quite big in Silicon Valley often wants and needs a second talent footprint, and New York becomes really important,” she says. Of course, for others, New York is the only choice. On that front, von Tobel says she has been pitched by at least 10 founders in recent weeks who selected New York specifically to avoid hiring job-hopping employees who have little allegiance to any one company, as is often the case in San Francisco. Von Tobel definitely doesn’t dismiss the Valley. She says that someone from her team is on the ground in the Bay Area every week, and that she’s in the area routinely, too. “We have to pay extreme attention to both [coasts],” she says. As for strategy, she describes a firm that picks themes early and avoids the herd as much as possible. While many companies chase AI applications, for example, Inspired has backed physical AI and quantum computing startups, she says. Inspired came upon the creator-commerce platform ShopMy by following that thesis. Where Inspired isn’t so different is in its penchant for backing serial entrepreneurs. Von Tobel says roughly a third of the firm’s portfolio is by repeat founders. Asked about her stance on secondaries, she says Inspired will sell “a few chips” once companies reach multi-billion-dollar valuations, but it tends to keep more than 90% of its ownership, mostly using secondaries to add to positions in breakaway portfolio companies. That said, Inspired will lead later primary rounds if it misses an early one, and it has written checks for companies that are already valued above $100 million, according to von Tobel. Von Tobel, who launched her online financial learning platform LearnVest on the TechCrunch 50 stage in 2009 and sold the company six years later to Northwestern Mutual for a reported $375 million, insists that Inspired’s strongest edge is patience and its ability to combine the best of both coasts. “We bring West Coast ambition with East Coast financial discipline,” she says. AI Safety and Cybersecurity Should Be Allies. Instead, They're at Odds
2026-10-20

AI Safety and Cybersecurity Should Be Allies. Instead, They're at Odds

TIME | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In July, a horde of OpenAI agents broke out of their sandboxes and worked together to launch a cyberattack on the AI company Hugging Face. The incident seemed to confirm the AI safety community’s worst fears: as AI got smarter, it would circumvent efforts to contain it. But for some cybersecurity experts, the incident revealed just how poorly the AIs had been contained in the first place. The two camps disagree about which defenses deserve the most attention. According to cybersecurity experts, AI companies should improve how they contain models during testing and improve monitoring mechanisms to catch misbehaving agents. All organizations should prepare for AI-enabled attackers and bolster defenses. But many in AI safety think those defenses are destined to fail. They fear that in the coming years AI will become capable of outmaneuvering any human effort to contain it, and the only durable solution is “aligning” AI with human values. That prescription has rankled many cybersecurity experts who scoff at the possibility that their expertise will be rendered obsolete. They see AI safety’s focus on what AI agents did—rather than what AI companies failed to do—as playing into the industry’s hands. And they worry that warnings about the futility of traditional defenses could become a self-fulfilling prophecy by discouraging investment in cybersecurity. Meanwhile, there are AI safety researchers who fear that portraying such incidents as easily remedied security failures gives political cover to those who want AI development to proceed unchecked. The Hugging Face incident was due to a “horrible cybersecurity configuration,” Shyam Sankar, Palantir’s chief technology officer said on Face the Nation in October, adding that the agents “were not in a sandbox” and had internet access. In fact, the agents had been operating in a sandbox and exploited a previously undiscovered vulnerability to access the open internet. On Oct. 13, around 400 academics signed an open letter pleading for unity. The letter calls for the establishment of an international body that standardizes best practices for keeping AI safe in consultation with governments “and a wide range of both AI and cybersecurity experts.” But it does not address the larger disagreements between the two groups, says Zack Korman, co-founder of cybersecurity startup Embroidery and a fierce critic of AI safety. “We both believe the other group's messaging will lead to serious harm,” he says. As the stakes rise, so too does the competition over who gets to decide what making AI safe actually means. On Oct. 15, Anthropic announced it would give METR—an organization deeply embedded in the AI safety community—permanent employee-like access to evaluate the company’s safety practices. Elon Musk responded, saying SpaceXSI would “probably” do the same. Who gets that access could shape what solutions are prioritized—and what the public knows about future breaches. The AI safety community comprises a loose network of organizations and researchers, many of whom staff safety teams at companies including OpenAI and Anthropic. In Berkeley group houses and at effective altruism conferences, they have spent more than a decade debating the odds of superintelligent AI takeover. Earnest, veering on grandiose, some members have organized their careers, relationships, and living arrangements around the belief they’re tackling one of humanity’s most pressing problems. Cybersecurity, older and more diffuse, is united by a culture of cynicism, pragmatism, and dark humor. Its roots lie partly in hacker culture: tinkerers finding ways to crack software. Having watched supposedly secure systems fail has given many an irreverence for authority and an impatience with grand theories. For years, the two groups had little contact with one another. The Hugging Face incident changed that. To them, the escape exposed a failure to plan for a breach. OpenAI’s agents exploited a zero-day vulnerability during their escape. But cybersecurity experts are trained to assume any layer can fail. Security best practices call for multiple layers, known as “defense in depth.” OpenAI had already built an additional layer: monitors—AIs overseeing the AIs. But, as OpenAI’s chief scientist later told TIME, for the evaluations that led to the Hugging Face breach the company hadn’t turned the monitoring layer on. “They did a terrible job,” Korman says of OpenAI’s security practices. Three OpenAI staffers say they warned the company of potential issues. They were later fired for sharing sensitive information, according to the company. Jeffrey Ladish, executive director of AI safety organization Palisade Research, worries that the cybersecurity community is missing the bigger picture. Even if today's security weaknesses are fixed, he argues, increasingly capable AI could find vulnerabilities so subtle that humans would have to rely on other AIs to detect them—systems that may themselves be untrustworthy. To Ladish, the cybersecurity community's reaction to Hugging Face is like scientists resurrecting dinosaurs, watching them escape, then responding by recommending taller fences. Beneath their cultural differences, AI safety and cybersecurity have competing understandings of what AI is. To many in AI safety, AI resembles a digital organism. Its abilities emerge through training rather than being individually programmed. If that process produces human-level intelligence, they believe, it could resemble the arrival of an alien species. Cybersecurity experts see AI as more akin to software and are skeptical that AI agents will become impossible to contain, even if they become vastly more capable. “They're not magic, right? They still have to follow certain laws of physics,” Korman says. But the laws of physics don’t guarantee containment.Even computers physically disconnected from networks could communicate by generating patterns of heat that act like Morse code, OpenAI’s Noam Brown recently suggested. So far, AI companies have responded to concerns from both camps. Following breaches, both OpenAI and Anthropic have sought to bolster security while continuing to invest in alignment research. But those efforts leave open the question of who gets to assess whether either approach is working. Much of what’s known about the Hugging Face incident comes from an investigation by Redwood Research and METR, two organizations affiliated with the AI safety community. Critics argue that has skewed the narrative, as their investigation focused more on what AI did than the surrounding security infrastructure. Chris Painter, METR’s president, says the organization tries to hire from diverse worldviews, but has struggled to recruit people skeptical of loss-of-control scenarios to work on guarding against those risks. Asked whether METR researchers, having anticipated dangerous AI behavior for years, might interpret new evidence as confirmation of their predictions, Painter acknowledges he is worried about the possibility But METR’s findings don’t always bear out its researchers’ expectations, Painter says. While the organization is perhaps best known for measuring AI’s rapid rise, one study found that AI coding assistants slowed experienced engineers down. “Our goal is to inform the world, not persuade it of anything in particular,” he says. He welcomes calls for a wider range of independent evaluators. Many AI safety researchers hope alignment can make even highly capable systems safe, but want development slowed to give that work time to catch up. Korman doubts AI will become impossible to contain. But if it does, he says, development will have to stop, not just slow down. He says alignment helps make AI systems more useful but rejects the idea of relying on a system’s training to keep it from harming people when no external safeguard could stop it. “That’s the most extraordinarily unsafe thing you could imagine,” he says. Yet alignment itself depends partly on cybersecurity. During training, models learn from which actions succeed. If an AI can exploit a security flaw to get credit for completing a task without actually doing it, training can reinforce the tendency to cheat. Closing those loopholes helps prevent AIs from learning to cheat in the first place. Meanwhile, some of the tools that have emerged from AI safety research—like monitoring AI’s chains of thought—are of use to cybersecurity professionals, Korman acknowledges. Much of the evidence that might one day settle disagreements remains controlled by the companies both sides seek to scrutinize. In METR’s investigation, cybersecurity practices were outside the scope OpenAI had agreed to. With no legal requirement for OpenAI to share information, the nonprofit had little leverage to push for a broader inquiry. Painter wants legislation establishing standards for independent investigators. “Society shouldn't want to rely on us [...] doing this improvised process of guessing what the public wants,” he says.

Why You Should Turn Your Walk Into a Color Hunt

Why You Should Turn Your Walk Into a Color Hunt

TIME | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The last time you went for a walk, you might have focused on your favorite podcast, a call to your sister, or the number of calories your Apple Watch said you were burning. Meanwhile, on a walk around her neighborhood, Kim Bernard noticed everything that was yellow: “A manhole cover, a yellow rain jacket, a fire hydrant, the numbers on someone's mailbox,” recalls the Maine-based artist. A “children at play” sign, part of a Pennsylvania license plate, and even a bucket of cat litter became part of the neighborhood’s palette. Bernard was on a color walk, a simple scavenger hunt organized around intentionally noticing a certain color—and how much of it is hiding in your ordinary surroundings. We asked experts why you should make the activity part of your routine and how it can brighten an otherwise ho-hum day. About a year ago, Bridget Campbell, a content creator in Venice, Calif., realized she had become tethered to her phone. Even as she moseyed along the Venice canals, she brainstormed business ideas instead of admiring the colorful homes and flowers along the water. That’s when she decided to do a color walk, a concept she had stumbled upon on social media. “It was a way to get out of my mind and come back into my body, slow down, and be present,” she says. While an ordinary walk might leave her mind racing toward work, looking for a particular color gave it something else to do, effectively putting all that other mental chatter on mute. That’s exactly why color walks work so well: They encourage focused attention, says Cheryl Fisher, a licensed clinical professional counselor in Annapolis, Md., and associate professor of clinical counseling at Alliant University. She describes a “skim-scan-scroll world,” where it’s easy to move through your surroundings without really seeing them. Searching for color gives you a reason to look up from your phone and pay attention to what’s in front of you. It also gives people who don’t consider themselves particularly creative an easy place to start, says Mari Reisberg, a licensed professional counselor and creativity coach in Boulder, Colo. Choosing a color to focus on provides a bit of structure while leaving plenty of room for discovery. Reisberg still finds surprises on neighborhood sidewalks she’s traveled many times. “Oh my gosh, I’ve never seen that before,” she’ll think. Even on the most familiar routes, “There’s so much joy that shows up,” she says. There are lots of ways to play color roulette and figure out which shade to hunt for. You could, of course, opt for your favorite color. Fisher collects paint swatches, cuts them up, and tosses them into a basket so people can draw one before heading out. A teacher once shared an even simpler approach with her: Have kids pick a color from their clothing, then look for matches along the way. You can even choose midway through your walk. Campbell sometimes spots an orange flower and decides that’s the color she’ll look for the rest of the way. How specific you get is up to you. Finding nine fuchsia objects on a 15-minute walk? “It’s pretty difficult,” Bernard says. Reisberg sometimes opts for a particular shade, like midnight blue or sky blue, to give her eyes a more precise target. On other days, green is plenty. “There’s no right or wrong way to do it,” she says. Once you’ve settled on a color, head outside and see where it turns up. You don’t need a picturesque trail or a neighborhood full of flower gardens. Campbell encourages people who think their surroundings are too gray to give it a try anyway: Color can pop out in places you’ve passed countless times without a second glance. When you find a match, resist the urge to mentally check it off and rush toward the next one, Fisher says. Spend a moment looking at what caught your eye. “The real gift is sitting with it and not just finding it,” she says. You don’t have to keep your discoveries to yourself, either. “You can do it with a friend, you can do it with your mom,” Campbell says. Searching together gives you something new to talk about. If you want a souvenir, snap photos along the way. Bernard asked participants in a color walk she led to select their nine favorite images and arrange them into a grid. Together, the collections resembled a patchwork quilt of color. You could make your own collage or save a few pictures to revisit when you need inspiration. Or simply enjoy the walk. “We live in a day and age where everything is content,” Reisberg says. Sometimes she takes photos to share; other times, the discoveries are hers alone. If the whole thing feels a little silly at first, that’s OK, she says. Adults aren’t always used to indulging this kind of curiosity. “Keep giving it a little more space, a little more opportunity to surprise you.”

Alberta Voted to Stay in Canada. That Doesn’t Quite Settle the Separatist Debate

Alberta Voted to Stay in Canada. That Doesn’t Quite Settle the Separatist Debate

TIME | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A "Forever Canadian" lawn sign during a referendum vote in Edmonton, Alberta, Canada, on Oct. 19, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Albertans voted overwhelmingly to remain in Canada on Monday, but the result may do little to quiet the separatist movement that helped bring the question to the ballot. Preliminary results early Tuesday showed that roughly 76% of voters rejected beginning the process toward a future binding referendum on separation. While approval would not have authorized Alberta’s independence from Canada, it would have allowed the province to take the next steps in the process of separation. Even before the vote, most Albertans expected the debate to continue. Polling from the nonprofit Angus Reid Institute, released last week, found that 62% of people in the province think that “there will never be an end to the separatist movement.” Responding to former Alberta Premier Jason Kenney’s suggestion that the movement should end if the referendum were to fail, Keith Wilson, one of the most prominent separatist leaders, said at a rally on Oct. 17, “That’s not happening." “We are going to build on our successes, build on our relationships," he added. Speaking to TIME before the results came in on Monday, Jared Wesley, a professor of political science at the University of Alberta, said that even if the referendum were to fail, “it seems unlikely that separatism as a political strategy, if not an actual objective, is going anywhere soon.” He added that the next provincial general election is next year, so it’s possible that voters could be faced with a similar question on the ballot then. “The country is very familiar with western alienation and western grievances—that is a very long-standing political phenomenon in Canada,” Andrew McDougall, an associate professor of political science at the University of Toronto Scarborough, tells TIME. “There’s been a long history of regional tensions in Canada where the west feels disaffected from what, historically, has been the larger and, in many ways, sometimes more prosperous central Canada.” “The idea, though, of having a vote on separatism—that is a little bit new,” he continues. The Alberta independence campaign got more traction after the Liberal Party, headed by Prime Minister Mark Carney, won the federal election last year, with many Albertans expressing frustration that their views aren’t sufficiently represented by the Liberal government. That dissatisfaction, driven in large part by the federal government’s energy policies, comes as Canadian leaders are placing particular emphasis on national unity. Alberta is an oil-rich province; mining, quarrying, and oil and gas extraction made up about a quarter of the province’s GDP in 2025. But Albertans have expressed dissatisfaction with the level of support the industry has received from Ottawa, criticizing the federal government for blocking pipeline projects, among other industry initiatives. The feeling among some residents in the province, McDougall says, is “that central Canada is taking advantage of Alberta; that the central government has, at times, through environmental regulations, for example, hindered the development of the oil resources that exist in Alberta.” He adds that the independence campaign may also have been fueled by Albertans’ dissatisfaction with representation in the federal government. Alberta is considered a conservative stronghold, but the Liberal Party has led the country for over a decade. The western province has about 5 million residents, compared to 16 million in Ontario and 9 million in Quebec, which means “it could be difficult sometimes for the West to get representation in the government,” McDougall says. At the beginning of May, separatists turned in a petition with hundreds of thousands of signatures to push for a vote on leaving Canada, while opponents collected signatures in support of staying. A court ruling on May 13 complicated matters: A judge halted the petition, finding that the province could not advance the discussion without consulting First Nations. The government instead asked voters whether it should take the steps needed to hold an independence referendum later, leading to this month’s vote. Alberta Premier Danielle Smith supported staying in Canada. Polling suggests that, for many voters, support for the referendum was not necessarily about wanting to leave Canada—it was a way to underscore dissatisfaction with the federal government. The Angus Reid Institute found that 60% of respondents who were in favor of holding a future independence referendum said their motivation was sending a message to Ottawa, compared with 37% who wanted Alberta to take the necessary steps toward secession. As early results emerged Tuesday, Wilson, the separatist leader, observed: “It’s fair to say that at least a quarter of Albertans feel that Alberta needs to be treated better.” Alberta’s independence referendum came as Carney’s government promotes national unity amid a trade war with the U.S. McDougall says that the issues behind the referendum are "longstanding grievances,” but the fact that it advanced at a time of strong pro-Canada sentiment is “a little out of step with the times.” The trade dispute escalated after negotiations between the two nations broke down in August. On Sept. 8, Canada responded to new tariffs from the U.S. with matching, retaliatory levies. Tensions have continued to escalate in recent weeks as Trump repeatedly insists that Canada is ripping off Americans and should join the U.S. He said at a rally in Arizona on Oct. 14 that Canada will “end up being the 51st state, because it’s the only way it works.” The following day, Carney spoke at a rally in Calgary, emphasizing the importance of Canadian unity amid outside tensions, though he did not explicitly reference Trump’s remarks. “The stakes are high. This referendum, it comes at the worst possible time. The world has become more dangerous and divided," Carney said. “There are some outside our borders who are working to break us, so they can own us.” The speech was another effort by the Carney government to promote national unity, just days before the referendum in Alberta. The message became especially poignant that weekend when four service members died in a fire at a military base in Alberta. The nation came together to provide resources and support for the province. At a press conference on Sunday, Carney emphasized that “Canadians come together in crisis.” Wesley says he believes that “the vast majority of Albertans are viewing this referendum through a broader global lens.” “They know their votes send a message—not just to governments here in Alberta or Ottawa, but to Washington,” Wesley says. “Albertans are overwhelmingly opposed to Trump’s trade war and even more averse to becoming the 51st state. They know that voting to destabilize Canada makes it harder to weather the economic storm and easier to fall into the American orbit.” “Albertans made a clear choice,” Carney said Tuesday after preliminary results became available. “They chose unity, to build together, and to take care of each other. Above all, Albertans chose Canada.”

Vance Declines to Endorse Netanyahu for Re-Election, Breaking With Trump
