Vance Declines to Endorse Netanyahu for Re-Election, Breaking With Trump

TIME | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump and Vice President J.D. Vance speak together during an event at the White House on May 8, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Vice President J.D. Vance on Monday declined to endorse Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu for re-election, a day after President Donald Trump publicly backed the embattled leader. “The President has the most effective endorsement in American, or international, political history,” said Vance, when asked in an interview if he would also be extending an endorsement ahead of the election on Oct. 27. “I endorse American candidates and that’s where I'm focused on, is the midterms, so I'm not getting involved in the Israeli elections.” When pushed for an answer, Vance added: “I'm not anti-endorsing [Netanyahu], I'm not pro-endorsing him.” Vance’s response stood in stark contrast to Trump's endorsement, as he put his “complete and total” support behind the Israeli leader, while praising his endurance as a “great warrior” Prime Minister. Referencing the Iran war, Trump said: “If Israel has the wrong leader, which would be anyone other than Bibi [Netanyahu], America will have to do this work ourselves, and I am not inclined to do so.” He went on to say that Netanyahu has been “strong, brave, and brilliant in the face of tremendous opposition, including that coming from even Israel” and “despite all of that, he has endured.” Many are viewing the Israeli election as a referendum on Netanyahu, who has faced heavy criticism over his handling of the Hamas-led attack against Israel on Oct. 7, 2023. The subsequent Israel-Hamas war has led to a humanitarian crisis in Gaza. Netanyahu’s government has also been called to task over the reports of settler violence against Palestinian villages in the occupied West Bank. Ahead of the elections, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth reportedly met with Netanyahu on Monday during an unannounced visit to Israel, Axios said, citing two sources with knowledge. The Pentagon declined to comment when contacted. TIME has also reached out to the Israeli embassy and Netanyahu’s office for comment. Vance, meanwhile, has previously issued warnings to Israel regarding its relationship with the U.S. In June, he chastised members of Bibi’s cabinet who had voiced disapproval over Washington negotiating with Tehran to end the Iran war, which started in February with joint U.S. and Israeli strikes. “Donald J. Trump is the only head of state in the entire world who is sympathetic to the nation of Israel at this moment in time. And he happens to be the head of state of the world’s superpower,” Vance said as he led a White House press briefing. “If I was in the Cabinet of the Israeli government, I might not be attacking the only powerful ally that I have anywhere left in the entire world.” While Vance has said his focus is on American candidates ahead of the U.S. midterms, he has previously campaigned avidly overseas. Vance traveled to Hungary in April ahead of parliamentary elections as the Trump Administration put its full support behind then-Prime Minister Viktor Orbán, who was up for re-election against opposition leader Péter Magyar. (Pro-European conservative Magyar went on to win the election, ending Orbán’s 16-year rule.) “Together the President of the United States and the Prime Minister of Hungary have been able to do amazing things,” Vance told reporters at a press conference in Budapest. “We want to build upon those amazing things. And of course, I want to help as much as I possibly can, the Prime Minister, as he faces this election season.” Vance later delivered remarks at a rally for Orbán. Vance also notably met with Alice Weidel, co-leader of the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party, in February 2025, days before a German election. During that trip, Vance spoke at the Munich Security Conference and used the platform to voice his concerns about Europe, claiming that free speech across the continent was “in retreat.” He criticized what is known as the “firewall” approach in Germany—the agreement among other political parties that they will not work with the AfD. “Democracy rests on the sacred principle that the voice of the people matters. There is no room for firewalls,” he said. Trump’s endorsement of Netanyahu comes after the pair have shared an, at times, contentious relationship in recent months. Asked about possible endorsement last week, Trump told reporters “we haven’t even thought about it,” prompting many to wonder if he may stay silent ahead of the consequential Israeli election. Trump and Netanyahu have clashed, particularly over Israel’s war against the Hezbollah militant group in Lebanon. In June, Trump told reporters that Netanyahu has to be “more responsible with respect to Lebanon,” amid concerns the renewed fighting with Hezbollah could derail the U.S.-Iran peace agreement. (Which later collapsed in light of continued clashes over the Strait of Hormuz.) “I'm not happy with the way Israel has handled themselves with Lebanon and with Hezbollah,” Trump told reporters at the G7 summit in France. “It just goes on forever,” he continued, seemingly referencing the exchanging of strikes between Israeli forces and the Hezbollah militant group. “When that happens, it throws a negative light on the big deal, the deal with Iran.” Trump said he has an “unbelievable relationship” with Netanyahu, but claimed “Israel would have been blown up a long time ago, had I not gotten involved.” Days before his public remarks, Trump reportedly called Netanyahu “f-cking crazy” during a fraught phone call after Netanyahu ordered an attack on a Hezbollah stronghold in Beirut in retaliation for alleged attacks on Israel by Hezbollah.

Why Satire Is No Laughing Matter
2026-10-20

Why Satire Is No Laughing Matter

TIME | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The first time I was introduced to satire, my jaw dropped—and not just because it was the first time I’d ever seen a naked woman. I was 8 when my older brothers snuck me into a London cinema to see Monty Python’s Life of Brian. Although my underdeveloped brain may not have fully grasped the critique of religion or inter­necine fights among political leftists, I loved the feeling of being surrounded by 300 people screaming with laughter. Over the next few years, I inhaled an array of comedy greats: Peter Sellers, Peter Cook, and Not the Nine O’Clock News, with its biting commentary on Thatcherite Britain. Then came a personal breakthrough. At the age of 20, I spent six months studying with an intimidating French clown. The late Philippe Gaulier was one of the all-time great teachers of clown and modern theater. Gaulier kept a drum handy while we were onstage, banging it to cut short a performance he considered “sh-t,” which was often. His goal, and greatest gift to us, was helping his students discover what he called their “inner idiot.” Gaulier introduced me to the satirical archetype of the bouffon. In his telling, the tradition dated back to 11th century France, where societal outcasts—gay people, heretics, those with deformities—would be let back into their villages once a year to put on shows viciously mocking the ruling class. It was the humor of the dispossessed. Whereas clowns were naive idiots, bouffons savagely undermined the people in power. They were doing something important: using biting and at times cruel humor to undermine systemic injustice. It’s what satirists have done throughout history. And along the way, some people, instead of laughing, wince—or worse, take offense. The Greek playwright Aristophanes brilliantly lampooned the militarism and demagoguery of his day. Yet because he deployed phallic jokes so obscene they would make even Ali G blush, critics like Plutarch sneered at Aristophanes’ “coarseness ... in words, vulgarity, and ribaldry.” We celebrate the Roman satirist Juvenal, but his contemporaries barely acknowledged him. Nearly two millennia later, Lenny Bruce’s four-letter comedy was branded “sick” in the pages of this magazine in 1959. Note to editors (and to Plutarch): you missed the point. Yes, satire can offend people other than the intended targets. Like that of my bouffon predecessors, mine definitely has. As someone who has opposed bigotry and injustice throughout my life and partnered with civil rights groups, I don’t recognize myself in the warped portrayals of me that I sometimes see on social media, lumped in with the bigots I lampoon. I’ve always designed my characters and comedy as vehicles to expose prejudice, bias, and stupidity, though I realize not everyone sees them that way. Ali G is a white imbecile—a delusional cultural appropriator—who steals the fashion and lyrics from his favorite gangsta rap and dancehall idols, from Compton to Kingston. The joke is on him, and on the predominantly white male establishment who take him seriously enough to believe that HBO would hire the stupidest man in the world as an interview host. Like a good bouffon, he undermines that establishment—not the culture he loves and borrows from. I admit that most of my humor is indeed juvenile. That’s because satire works best not when it preaches, but rather when it lives among the laughter. Then, once you walk out of the theater or switch off the TV, it makes you think. What makes satirical comedy so effective is what it lays bare. I’ll never forget a fox-­hunting rally I attended the first time I created Borat—which involved me rummaging around in the trunk of my car for two minutes, finding a hat I’d bought in Astrakhan, Russia, and sticking it on my head. “Jagshemash,” I proclaimed, riffing off a Polish greeting. I asked the English toffs, “In my country we hunt criminals—do you think we should do that here?” Shockingly, even though on camera, they said yes. A light bulb exploded in my 25-year-old brain. Real people believed this ridiculous character—and they would speak to him more freely than in any documentary. Each of my characters has a specific mechanism to encourage people to drop their guards and show what they really believe, prejudices and all. Borat, an antisemitic journalist, exposes Jew hatred, xeno­phobia, sexism, and casual racism. Erran Morad, a stereotype of a tough-guy Israeli commando—“I’ve been in 14 terror attacks,” he says in my new movie Ali G: Who Iz I?, “and started two”—exposes Islamophobia and the ludicrous things people will do when they’re gripped by an irrational fear of “the other.” Sacha Baron Cohen transforms into Ali G on the set of Ali G: Who Iz I? Courtesy of Michael Legato As the titular Ali G, left, in the new movie Ali G: Who Iz I? Courtesy of Amazon MGM Studios Ali G works on another level. He’s not extreme—he’s just extremely dumb. But his absurd questions lead powerful people to underestimate him and answer in earnest, revealing their own biases. Other characters highlight the problems facing our society without engaging real people at all. The Dictator’s Admiral General Aladeen—the cartoonish ruler of a fictional Horn of Africa nation and a mash-up of personality-­cult authoritarians from Kim Jong Il to Idi Amin—warned, in 2012, how easily democracies could fall into autocracies. Regardless of the madness, the method is the same: make the audience laugh as hard and as much as possible, while holding up a mirror to reflect back things that aren’t always comfortable to see or warn of trends we’d rather look away from. Done well, brutally honest satire can be cuttingly effective. At a time when Hollywood largely avoided anti-Nazi content, Charlie Chaplin—then one of the most famous movie stars in the world—used The Great Dictator to point to the destruction that Nazism would bring. Peter Sellers’ sinister Dr. Strangelove showed the insanity of mutually assured destruction. Abbie Hoffman, who became a hero of mine while I researched my undergraduate thesis on civil rights—and whom I later played in The Trial of the Chicago 7—deployed outrageous stunts to oppose the Vietnam War. In fact, satire doesn’t just needle politicians or regimes—it occasionally helps bring them down. In Hammer & Tickle, the historian Ben Lewis credits Soviet joke-­telling with helping to topple communism, by making it impossible for even the system’s own enforcers to take it seriously. Volodymyr Zelensky was a Ukrainian comedian who once pretended to play piano with his penis—a stunt Aristophanes would have loved. Yet Zelensky’s TV show parodied the country’s corrupt oligarchy so effectively that, just four years after Servant of the People premiered, he was elected President himself. “Every joke,” George Orwell observed, “is a tiny revolution.” Of course, comedy has its limits—more satirists in Weimar Germany could not have prevented Nazism. Still, satire—along with a free press, independent institutions, and a robust civil society—does play a role in promoting free societies. That’s exactly what so many governments seem afraid of. In Nazi Germany, mocking Hitler could be a capital offense. Stalin sent an estimated 200,000 citizens to the gulags for making political gags. In our own time, Chinese censors have scrubbed social media of a meme comparing President Xi Jinping to Winnie the Pooh; one student who shared that and other unflattering posts got six months in prison for “picking quarrels and provoking trouble.” Last year in Tehran, the comedian Zeinab Mousavi was sentenced to six months simply for parodying an Iranian epic poem. Even in democracies, satirists, instead of getting a laugh, sometimes face sanctions. In the U.S., Lenny Bruce and George Carlin were arrested for uttering obscenities. Because of its irreverent treatment of Christianity, Monty Python’s Life of Brian—the very film that introduced me to satire—was banned in half a dozen countries, and even parts of the U.K. Abbie Hoffman holding a Ronald Reagan mask by the nose, after his keynote speech at the CMJ Music Marathon, Aug. 29, 1988. Hoffman's shirt is in reference to Nancy Reagan's campaign of saying, JUST SAY NO! to drugs, but Hoffman had modified it to saying JUST SAY NO! to drug testing. Richard Corkery—NY Daily News Archive/Getty Images Philippe Gaulier and Bridget Brandon (Director of Drama Action Center) at their center at Rozelle Hospital, Church St. Leichhardt. Aug. 21, 1988. Philip Wayne Lock—Fairfax Media/Getty Images Today, some believe the times have overtaken Orwell’s “tiny revolutions.” In an era when the U.S. President and his ilk seem impervious to shame and ridicule, is satire all but dead? Absolutely not. In fact, I believe that satire is more important than ever. The Trump Administration’s own actions show why. If ridicule is now irrelevant, why did CBS cancel Stephen Colbert’s show just days after he mocked his parent company for paying Trump $16 million to settle a lawsuit? Why did Trump’s chairman of the Federal Communications Commission appear to threaten ABC’s broadcast license—leading ABC to briefly suspend Jimmy Kimmel Live!—after Kimmel’s comments about the killing of conservative activist Charlie Kirk? Even Trump doth protest too much. Asked in 2020 about Borat outing his attorney Rudy Giuliani as a creep, Trump dredged up the time I tried to scam him as Ali G two decades earlier: “That’s a phony guy and I don’t find him funny.” So why does my new movie bring back that phony and unfunny guy, which some have criticized as “microwaving a relic from a generation ago?” The fact that Ali G is outdated and problematic is the very reason I resurrected him. The world he’s returned to is, in many ways, worse than the one he left 20 years ago. All the prejudices my characters are designed to expose haven’t just lingered—they’ve metastasized. They’re fueled by the greatest propaganda machine in history—social media—and now by its monstrous child, AI. Back when I first created Borat, it took a few minutes—or a few drinks—to get people to reveal the racism that often simmered below the surface. Now, those views have gone mainstream—whether it’s fans hurling slurs and abuse at Black players during the World Cup or hundreds of white nationalists marching through Washington, D.C., on July 4. In 2004, also as Borat, I got a bar full of people to sing “Throw the Jew Down the Well.” Today Jews make up just 2% of the U.S. population, yet are the targets of nearly 70% of religion-­based hate crimes in America. Whatever your politics, the trend is undeniable. In 2018, disguised as Dr. Nira Cain-N’Degeocello, an ultra-woke developer planning to build a mosque in a small town, I showed people’s hostility to Muslims. One even declared, “I’m racist—towards Muslims.” Now, right-wing U.S. Congressmen readily admit as much. On my 2018 show Who Is America?, I convinced three men to host a fake quinceañera to trap undocumented immigrants. Now, stoked by far-right agitators and viral social media posts, anti-­immigrant protests and riots have erupted across England and in Belfast. Meanwhile, ICE agents are running amok—terrorizing cities and, yes, detaining people at a quinceañera. As Borat, in 2006’s Borat: Cultural Learnings of America for Make Benefit Glorious Nation of Kazakhstan. AJ Pics—Alamy Then there’s the explosion of misogyny. Six years ago, in Borat Subsequent Moviefilm, a plastic surgeon cheerfully discussed giving my fictional 15-year-old daughter Tutar breast implants to appeal to important men. In today’s “manosphere,” that’s nothing compared with how Andrew Tate advises his millions of followers to treat women. Nor was overturning Roe v. Wade and a women’s right to control her own body apparently enough. Christian nationalists are now calling for “household voting”—effectively taking the right to vote from many women and giving it to their husbands. Twenty years ago, Ali G would sit next to world experts spouting absurdities. Today—fed a steady diet of disinformation and conspiracy theories by social media influencers and TikTok news—we have created an entire world where ignorance reigns and truth is inverted. Thanks to this toxic brew of lies, conspiracies, and hate, right-wing authoritarianism is on the march. Intolerance and extremism have surged on the left as well. Political violence has increased, as has our tolerance for it. In one survey, one-fifth of Americans agreed that “true American patriots may have to resort to violence in order to save our country.” We are once again entering an age of “virtuous” violence, where any atrocity—by racists, Jew ­haters, or Islamophobes, the right or the left—can be somehow justified. It is not a place any of us should want to live. In a world where social media and AI hypercharge lies, who decides what’s considered truth? In a culture seemingly obsessed with dividing people by identity, how do we find commonality in our shared human experience? I made this latest film for the same reason I’ve always done this work. Not only to make people laugh, but also to bring them face-to-face with the danger of fanaticism and conspiracy theories. I’m not naive. One film won’t stamp out age-old prejudices or stop the extremism that’s fueling the slide from democracy to autocracy and mass violence. But, as satire has done since ancient times, it can help sound the alarm. It can remind people about the very real dangers of ludicrous lies—whether spread by a thin-­skinned authoritarian or an extremist with an online following. With the U.S. midterm elections just weeks away, perhaps it encourages citizens to mobilize against these dangerous falsehoods—at the ballot box and beyond. Maybe it can even help shame more of our governments into finally making our Silicon Valley overlords—unelected billionaires who decide what billions of people see and believe—liable for the lies, conspiracies, and hate on their platforms that have already gotten people killed and will likely kill many more. Satirists can expose hypocrisy. Bouffons can shed their clothes and show that the emperor has none. But after the credits roll, it’s up to all of us to decide what comes next. If our pluralistic democracies are going to survive, we need to create societies where we stop the hate and tolerate those different from us—no matter who they are, where they come from, whom they love, or how they pray. Because the only way out of this mess is to treat everyone with what Ali G has always preached: Respect.

Offbeat Thriller Phony Is Something Genuinely New on TV
2026-10-20

Offbeat Thriller Phony Is Something Genuinely New on TV

TIME | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The protagonist of Phony, a delightfully genre-defying Hulu series best miscategorized as a thriller, is the kind of teenage boy any mother would brag about. Played by Sam Nivola, who’s best known for portraying gentle sons of privilege in The White Lotus and The Perfect Couple, Sonny Cooke is that one kid everyone in school knows and likes. He’s as comfortable with the jocks as he is with the theater set; his friendly, nonjudgmental presence sets his peers at ease. You’d think this would be especially tough to achieve when your mom, Connie Britton’s Ellen, is the principal, but if anything, the proximity of a single parent with whom he shares a fierce bond adds to his ease. “I actually love high school,” he narrates over a montage of happy moments that opens the show. “I know what people need to hear, and people trust me with their sh-t.” That’s before one anxiously anticipated night upends Sonny’s pleasant life. Driving to his girlfriend Chloe’s (Mahaela Park) house, with plans to have sex for the first time and a blue vibrator in tow as a sort of talisman against performance anxiety, he crashes into an eerie pond. When he awakens in the hospital a week later, banged up but healing, the woman who greets him looks slightly yet decisively different from the mother he knows better than anyone. (Britton’s subtle transformation is rendered, quite effectively, through a combination of makeup and visual effects.) No one else seems to notice the change. They chalk up Sonny’s insistence that this new Ellen is a phony to the lingering effects of his accident. He fights with his skeptical best friend, Vogel (Maliq Johnson), and drifts toward outsiders Mallory (Grace Brennan), a junior-high love interest who’s back in class after years of being homeschooled by evangelical parents, and her geeky cousin, Weldon (Colter Lee), who are bored enough to help investigate. Maliq Johnson, left, and Sam Nivola in Phony David Astorga—Disney With its cliffhangers and deceptions, Phony has the bones of a thriller. But since a big part of the mystery is whether Ellen’s new face is the result of a crime, some supernatural occurrence, or truly all in Sonny’s head, it can’t lean on the overused conventions of any particular thriller subgenre. Instead, its comedy-veteran showrunners, creator Nick Paley (Marcel the Shell With Shoes On, Broad City) and Anthony King (The Afterparty, Rooster), give their half-hour series the vibe of a coming-of-age dramedy, albeit a decidedly uncanny one. There’s a lot of humor, often of the awkward-adolescent variety, in the characters, performances, and dialogue. “I decided to go vegan when I found out that cows have best friends,” Chloe rambles to Mallory, in a stiff early interaction. “And then I saw this cow get its head cut off.” A party that spans most of a midseason episode recalls the shambolic outdoor rager in Dazed and Confused, with groups of kids intoxicatedly yapping and clumsily flirting. That this atmosphere feels natural—grounding Sonny’s crisis in the real social world of teens’ funny, stressful, horny, mundane, phone-mediated daily lives—rather than painfully quirky, is a testament to the show’s delicate execution. People have idiosyncrasies. Sonny and Chloe call each other Bobo; his mom used to cook him a dish they refer to, without expository intervention, as Chicken Nummers. The school counselor (Kelvin Yu) is a frustrated musician, and his faux-casual attempts to get students to listen to his record are played for laughs, but he’s not just a joke. In fact, Paley and King almost always resist tired tropes. Nothing would be easier than a Chloe-Sonny-Mallory love triangle straight out of every teen drama ever. But that’s not the way these relationships are written. The characters are too specific to disappear into clichés. Colter Lee and Grace Brennan in Phony David Astorga—Disney Because Hulu provided half of the eight-episode season to critics (and because only a monster would put a spoiler of that level in a review even if they did have access to the information), I don’t know how the mystery of Ellen and her doppelgänger is resolved. While early signs do point to an explanation more compelling than, say, it was all just a dream, I’m pleased to say that, midway through, the possibilities still seem endless. I don’t think I’d be too upset about an anticlimax, anyway. The vividness and humor Phony finds in the school community makes the process of getting to the big reveal a pleasure in itself. As is watching Britton and Nivola, the prototypical TV mom and son, alternately playing into and against their respective types. Besides, there’s a perceptive story of self-discovery unfurling in the background that may prove to be more important, thematically speaking, than what became of Ellen. Sonny’s conviction that his mom is a fake destabilizes his personality and habits enough to illuminate how he’s been faking it, in a social sense. So eager is he to make others happy that he shifts views depending on who he’s talking to and worries about Chloe having a good first sexual experience to the exclusion of valuing his own. “I don’t even have strong opinions!” he insists. The idea that growing up means developing a self distinct from the person other people want you to be isn’t new to the coming-of-age canon. Yet here it’s approached from an angle, and with a degree of perceptiveness, that I didn’t see coming. In our rigidly genre-bound times, when few shows that call themselves thrillers surprise us in any way, Phony is something genuinely new on TV.

If you ever actually build anything That's the theory, but it's not the practice. They are taking just as long as large reactors. In some senses longer, because none has actually been put into commercial operation, whereas new large reactors have come online. And they turn out to have very little actual modularity and scalability, because there's more than 150 designs out there, so no-one is winning enough of the market for repeatable learnings, and anyway, you'll never get to volume manufacturing for the parts involved, because the quantities are just too small, and everything still requires literal nuclear-grade engineering. Take a motor-operated isolator valve that you'd find in a cooling system. You find plenty of these outside SMR applications, often 4 to 6 inch sizes, and they might cost something like 3 to 10k USD in a typical demanding industrial application. But they will cost more like 30 to 100k USD in an SMR, because they need to be made from nuclear-grade materials with documented material traceability, need seismic qualification, need nuclear-specific quality assurance, inspection and documentation, and need highly secure supply chains (no-one wants a counterfeit component showing up in a nuclear safety system). And they won't get much cheaper, because of strict configuration control, which limits the extent of any changes to components, materials and manufacturing processes. It's also important to recognise that the economies of learning that apply afe fundamentally different and more limited for construction projects, which is what every SMR is, than for component manufacturing. That reflects the facts that (1) different places have different ground conditions, seismic risk, regulatory requirements, humidity, water access, etc, etc, and (2) huge amounts of stuff still have to be constructed on site by humans (even if assisted by machines), compared with a factory line that can be automated. This is still a problem even for solar, where the panels themselves can be cheap compared to the install costs, which is why there are various attempts being made to turn more of the deployment into a manufactured process rather than a construction process. Two Little Words Are Guiding the Next Generation of Democrats. I Know Because They Said It 13 Times in One Night.
2026-10-20

Two Little Words Are Guiding the Next Generation of Democrats. I Know Because They Said It 13 Times in One Night.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The walls and ceiling of the Black Sheep Sports Bar, where the Manhattan Young Democrats are hosting their fourth social of the year, are still covered in World Cup banners and flags. Even though the tournament ended only three days ago, the soccer paraphernalia feels like a forsaken Christmas tree wilting in February. Down the stairs, I am welcomed by the yipping of a dozen Pittsburgh-hating Yankees fans. Oh no! There are both regulars and Young Dems at this sports bar on a Wednesday. How will I tell them apart? Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s galvanizing effect on the city’s youth has done more than swell the ranks of the city’s Democratic Socialists of America. It’s also brought growth to the MYD, the borough’s official youth arm of the Democratic Party. According to the organization’s 27-year-old vice president Courtney Curd, MYD’s membership has doubled from 300 to 600 over the past year. To onlookers, that growth might seem pretty unexpected: One might reason that the anti-establishment sentiment that fueled the rise of the NYC-DSA since Mamdani’s successful mayoral bid would stay within the left flank, not inspire young adults to join up with the very establishment the movement bemoans. Sure, MYD is tiny compared with the roughly 15,000-member NYC-DSA, but the kind of growth it’s seeing is indicative of how leftist politics have begun to take shape within the party’s staffer class. This bar is, after all, stuffed with the political operatives who will be running campaigns in a few years. As the DSA continues to rack up midterm primary victories across the country, and the Democratic Party’s moderates come to understand just how insurgent the insurgency seems to be, the direction of the party is up for grabs in a way it hasn’t been in decades. And if Democratic moderates are serious about making a concerted effort to fend off the left, they’ll have to compete for that ever-lauded youth territory the DSA has capitalized on. What I learned is that, at least inside of this one representation of the Democratic Party on this one very particular island, the establishment is getting ready to compromise. Compared with NYC-DSA’s base, which skews young, college-educated, and not especially well paid—heavy on teachers, social workers, and nonprofit employees—the MYD crowd runs established. (I scan the room to take a head count and quickly find myself counting receding hairlines instead. The mixer quickly revealed two truths 1) I have some coping to do over the apparently brutal reality of male pattern baldness, and 2) not all of these young Dems are quite so young.)The Black Sheep is packed with Democratic congressional and state legislature staffers, fundraisers, and political consultants. The organization is headed by a man on the inside: New York Assemblyman Jordan Wright, 31, who just won his first primary as an incumbent against 33-year-old DSA-backed Conrad Blackburn. Wright was the only incumbent to survive a DSA challenge in the state’s June primaries. Heineken in hand, I park myself at a high top, watching as a mix of blazers and khaki shorts crowd around the bar’s repurposed whiskey barrels. Among the crowd are various politicians, including New York City Council member Virginia Maloney, 38, and Ryder Kessler, 40, who lost his primary for Assembly in June. Attendees strain their voices talking about elections and Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey as they compete with indiscernible house music and the baseball game. When MYD chief of staff Sophia Secor, 28, became involved with the organization in 2023, it was “kind of lame,” she said, with a lack of buy-in from the organization’s few members. The state Democratic Party never really needed a concrete organizing presence in the city, instead sending resources upstate into the districts with viable Republican challengers. But with the rise of NYC-DSA, the party suddenly needed a face in the city. Secor and now-VP Curd, who joined around the same time, started asking how to turn it into “the cool kids’ club.” Their model was the city’s Young Republicans, who were throwing grand balls that “just looked aesthetically cool,” Secor said. At the end of 2024, Secor sat down with Wright for dinner after a Nancy Pelosi fundraiser and pitched her vision for the future of the group: party like the New York Young Republicans and knock doors like the DSA. Less than two years later, the MYD is the second-highest-fundraising Young Democrats chapter in the country, according to Secor. A few feet in front of me, Assemblyman Wright is dressed in all-white linens and Air Force 1s, fielding greetings from all angles. Wright comes from a family entrenched in the city’s political machine—his father, Keith, served in the same Assembly seat for over two decades and now runs the Manhattan Democratic Party as its county chair, and his grandfather was Bruce Wright, a Harlem judge so notorious for freeing defendants on low bail that the tabloids nicknamed him “Turn ’Em Loose Bruce.” In 2025, Wright originally endorsed Andrew Cuomo for mayor, before throwing his full weight behind Mamdani after he won the Democratic nomination. When asked about the endorsement, Wright points to the long history between the Wright and Cuomo families. The three senior leaders have a clear message: MYD is pitching a “wide tent.” Eventually, I start to count. I hear that phrase at least 13 times by the end of the night. It comes from a place where we’ve seen the Democrats many times before: “A lot of us, like myself, are pragmatic,” Curd says. Not that long ago, this was the pitch you’d hear from the party’s moderates: the fiscal conservatives and social liberals who’d remind everyone you can’t win anything without the center and maybe a few Republicans. But it seems those tent poles are getting set up in the opposite direction these days. “All of us work in the Democratic Party whether we like it or not because we run on the Democratic line,” Curd says. She points out that she serves as the campaign manager for Eli Northrup, a non-DSA but progressive candidate who won his primary for a New York State Assembly seat. Curd is, by her own account, “extremely progressive.” She speaks fervently about a platform that sounds indistinguishable from the DSA’s. “There’s a lot of communities that aren’t ready to have a DSA candidate, but it’s important to still bring them in, and still have conversations about universal healthcare, taxing the rich,” she says. Five years ago, Curd and Secor would likely have been written off as the party’s idealist left flank. But now, given the rise of the DSA as a relevant reference point on the spectrum of the left, they find themselves having to insist on just how progressive they actually are. In Mamdani’s New York, the “extremely progressives” might be the party’s new pragmatists. Kieran Blunnie, a New York–based political strategist familiar with the MYD, thinks this phenomenon just might make it out of Manhattan. “In a weird way we’re going to start seeing it more across the country,” he said, “progressives who largely align with the DSA and choose the establishment party to incite change from within.” One might take former President Barack Obama’s decision to campaign with Abdul El-Sayed, the progressive who beat Michigan’s establishment pick in a bitter Senate primary, as a sign the party may be ready to meet the left halfway. For the next hour and a half, the bar remains packed. I float amid the hundred-ish attendees, moving from conversation to conversation with various election geeks and wanna-be Josh Lymans. I join two lovely politicos in a cheers to recent congressional primary loser Alex Bores before downing a shot of some sort of alcoholic iced green tea; I chat with an NYU student determined to get his money’s worth in networking out of the cost of his Heineken; and I listen to Wright give a barely audible speech in which he laments the “nonsense in Washington” and implores attendees to grab a drink. The following morning, just when I thought my Young Democrat adventures had come to an end, I received a call from Curd inviting me to the statewide organization’s annual gala at an Indian restaurant in Nassau County. How could I resist? Inside the marble ballroom of the Las Vegas–ready restaurant on Long Island, the award ceremony flies by as I work my way through a plate of samosas and nurse a mango lassi. The crowd is a mix of younger staffers and more veteran New York politicians, many of whom are receiving awards. Among them, Mike Gianaris, the state Senate’s deputy majority leader (age 56), picks up the FDR Trailblazer of the Year Award. He spends most of his time at the podium reliving 2018. After the first half of the award ceremony, I follow a few MYD members, including Curd and Jasper Price, outside for a smoke break. “They should’ve had an award for GOATED young democrat,” Price says. He’s one of the two known DSA members on the MYD’s 54-person board. (Curd said there could be more, but that the MYD does not inquire.) As a member of both, Price has a lot to say about the relationship between MYD and DSA. He seems to oscillate between a collaborative and combative view. “It’s not a permanent opposition,” he says, “There’s enough overlap already that we might see a staffer class that starts in DSA and is willing to entertain joining MYD.” While Price and his colleagues go to take advantage of the open bar, I chat with New York State Young Democrats chief of staff Avi Presberg, 28, upon the restaurant’s lion-headed thrones. He describes a vision for collaboration between the two organizations but comes off a bit more matter-of-fact than the MYD folks. “At the end of the day, they run as Democrats, so they need us,” Presberg says. After the event, some of the Manhattanites peel off to attend a pool party, while the rest pile into the bus and turn back onto the Jericho Turnpike. On the ride back into the city I picture Assemblyman Wright pitching a comically large tent over the island. If these optimistic progressive Dems are right, he’s going to need an even bigger one. Abdul El-Sayed’s Viral Moment With a Heckler Was Revealing—but Not in the Way the Heckler Intended
2026-10-19

Abdul El-Sayed’s Viral Moment With a Heckler Was Revealing—but Not in the Way the Heckler Intended

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Viral moments from the campaign trail often crystalize both the key issues in a race and how the candidates are responding to them. That’s what happened on Saturday when a heckler confronted Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate in Michigan, about whether he condemns the Muslim Brotherhood, which many consider a terrorist group. El-Sayed, who is Muslim, quickly answered—“Sure, yeah, absolutely”—before questioning the heckler, who identified himself as “Tommy,” about the thinking behind it. “You’re asking the question because implicitly you assume that I’ll have dual loyalties, right?” El-Sayed asked before paraphrasing John F. Kennedy’s famous line about whether his Catholic faith would make him allegiant to the pope. “I’m not running to be a Muslim senator. I’m running for the U.S. Senate and happen to be Muslim,” El-Sayed said, to applause. Identity isn’t incidental to El-Sayed’s candidacy. He has talked about being the child of Egyptian immigrants, harshly criticized Israel’s war in Gaza, and taken heat for campaigning with Hasan Piker, a controversial left-wing streamer. Yet Republicans have openly attacked El-Sayed’s faith as he competes to become America’s first Muslim senator. Former Rep. Mike Rogers, El-Sayed’s opponent in next month’s election, has claimed without evidence that the Muslim Brotherhood funds El-Sayed’s campaign and suggested that El-Sayed’s family members have terrorist links. Donald Trump mocks his middle name, Mohamed. And Tommy, it turns out, is reportedly a former Republican staffer. So I asked my colleague Aymann Ismail, who has covered El-Sayed for Slate—and is also a Muslim Egyptian American—about what that viral exchange says about El-Sayed’s history-making candidacy. Ian: What sticks out to you about the clip? Aymann: I think it’s part and parcel of the Muslim American experience. Everybody who shares that identity can immediately think of a time where they were asked the same thing. When I was in line to get my credentials at the Republican National Convention one year, somebody heard I was Muslim and asked me if I condemned the Prophet Mohammed. So I think it’s important to acknowledge that El-Sayed isn’t just speaking for himself, he’s speaking for all of us. You’ve written about how El-Sayed has sometimes de-emphasized his faith even as his opponents try to center it. Why do you think that is? There’s an entire political ecosystem out there trying to convince people that there’s something about Muslims that makes them dangerous. El-Sayed’s opponents are also feeding voters clips of him, say, campaigning with Piker, which might be their first experience of El-Sayed. He needs to take that into consideration when trying to reach the highest elected office any Muslim has ever held. It was striking to me that El-Sayed’s campaign shared the clip of his exchange with Tommy. My sense is that some Michigan voters appreciate El-Sayed’s confrontational rhetorical style, while others find it overly aggressive. What have you seen in terms of how voters respond to him? I’ve seen both. Before El-Sayed was the nominee, I followed him at a local Democratic convention, where he talked to different groups of community leaders. Talking to the Arab American caucus, he was confident. When he talked with the Jewish American caucus, he had a more reserved, “I’m here to listen” kind of energy. I check in every couple days with a woman I met in that room, a community leader involved with organizing Jewish voters. She’s repeatedly told me that El-Sayed seems like an overly masculine guy who’s doing a right-wing machismo act. So it’s not just conceivable that voters could think of him as too aggressive—I specifically hear that it’s off-putting for some. But at the same time, one of the stereotypes against Muslim men is that they’re domineering. I’ve watched this clip a thousand times. El-Sayed isn’t threatening; he doesn’t call Tommy a racist. He’s asking him to think a little bit deeper about why he would ask that question in the first place. You’ve also covered El-Sayed’s struggles to win over Black voters. Given all that, how do you feel about his chances next month? Some polls show him way ahead, and betting markets give him around a 70 percent chance of winning. I’m a bit of a skeptic. I think it’s going to be much closer specifically because of what I’ve heard from voters on the ground. And El-Sayed has made comments that upset people, like when he was talking about an attack on a Jewish temple in March and said, “Hurt people hurt people.” Some are urging Jewish voters to leave their Senate ballots blank in a campaign that mirrors the “uncommitted” movement during the 2024 election. Right now, that community leader I’m in touch with is thinking that she’s not going to vote at all. That’s a lot of politics for a Monday, so let’s pivot to something more relaxing—like my colleagues’ suggestions about what to eat, read, and watch tonight: Nibble on an appetizer: The main course used to be the whole point of eating out at a restaurant. But now, with rising prices, the spread of appetite-curbing GLP-1s, and the ubiquity of small plates, entrées are getting left behind more and more. Maggie Hennessy explores what diners’ changing preferences mean for dinner time. Add this book to your reading list: Lessons in Chemistry, the debut novel from Bonnie Garmus, was one of the most popular books of 2022. Now, Garmus is back with her second book, Peck & Peck, which is “a workplace comedy, a New York City coming-of-age story, and a giddy satire.” Slate book critic Laura Miller found it charming. Learn about whale biology: If you’ve seen or heard of the movie Whalefall, in which a scuba diver is swallowed by a whale, you have probably wondered whether the premise is at all plausible. Lucky for skeptics, Nadira Goffe interviewed a whale biologist about how realistic the film is. It turns out the movie actually gets some important details right! Take stock of your streaming subscriptions: Hollywood is in a state of flux. A small number of corporate giants now control a massive slice of the industry—exemplified by Paramount Skydance’s recent acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery. As the entertainment industry consolidates, Netflix and Disney are taking some unprecedented steps to survive. Slate business and tech writer Nitish Pahwa breaks it all down. OK, that’s all for me today. Enjoy your night, and watch out for hungry whales! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Putin Is Throwing Trump a Bone—but It’s Laced With Poison
2026-10-19

Putin Is Throwing Trump a Bone—but It’s Laced With Poison

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Russian President Vladimir Putin has thrown Donald Trump a bone that’s so big and juicy, Trump must feel sorely tempted to chase it—but it’s laced with so much poison that even Trump might calm himself and resist. The bone, according to the Wall Street Journal, is the proposal of a tripartite summit—just Putin, Trump, and Chinese President Xi Jinping—to plot an ending to the Russia–Ukraine war. Clearly the idea appeals to Trump’s idealized view of the world: great powers carving their spheres, calling the shots, collecting the spoils, and Donald J. Trump taking his seat—recognized by all—as one of those great powers. No small or medium-size democracies need apply—thus no other pesky Europeans, and certainly no Ukrainians, need take part in determining their fates. The view is of a piece with Trump’s adoration and envy of men holding absolute power. He basks in their reciprocated respect (whether feigned or genuine), feels his own power elevated, and yearns for more. During the first year of his first term as president, Trump spoke in awe of North Korean dictator Kim Jong-un: “He speaks and people sit up at attention. I want my people to do the same.” He was similarly enamored by the red carpet and sword dance at his first meeting with the Saudi royal family. These were people who ruled a country, the heirs to generations of rulers. By comparison, meeting with elected elites, whose fleeting power depends on the whims of the populace, reminds him all too much of his own limitations. So a summit of the era’s three great men—Putin, Xi, and Trump! That’s the stuff of history: like Churchill, Stalin, and Roosevelt at Yalta, or Metternich, Talleyrand, and Tsar Alexander at the Congress of Vienna. But any such comparisons are mere self-glorifying fantasy, for several reasons but two in particular. First, and most obvious, Trump is no Roosevelt or Talleyrand (nor, it should be noted, does either of his collaborators resemble Churchill or Metternich). Second, and equally problematic, Trump would enter such a game not merely outmatched but holding (to borrow one of his own metaphors) no cards. The 19th-century statesmen gathered at Vienna to stabilize Europe in the aftermath of Napoleon’s ravaging. The three main Allied victors of World War II met at Yalta in 1945 to carve new European borders after the Nazis’ defeat. The great-powers summit that Putin proposes is a very different thing. Putin started the war against Ukraine and continues to wage it relentlessly. Xi isn’t quite an ally—he isn’t assisting Putin with troops or weapons—but he is providing Russia with technology and economic support. Trump could exert some leverage by threatening to send Ukraine tons of missiles, planes, and drones—or upping sanctions against Russia—if Putin continues the war. But as everyone plainly sees, Trump has cut off aid to Ukraine and waived the sanctions that Congress passed by a strong bipartisan majority. Trump’s leanings toward Putin have long been clear, as has his hostility toward Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky. But neither sentiment has been unambiguous—he has helped Ukraine with arms and intelligence, and he has punished Russia with sanctions—until now. As has been noted even by Zelensky, who has gone out of his way not to appear confrontational with the ultrasensitive American president, Trump has now declared himself a Russian ally in the war on Ukraine. And so, if this Putin–Trump–Xi summit actually happens, it will likely be a forum for sketching out the precise terms of Ukraine’s surrender. Putin has stated that the goal of his war is to eliminate Ukraine as a sovereign nation, which he views as illegitimate. The point of the summit would be to codify that view—and to take the first steps toward enacting it. This ambition is not implausible. Zelensky recently admitted that if Trump proceeds on this course, if the United States officially allies with Russia in this war, Ukraine cannot possibly win. And all this—Putin’s proposal for a summit, Trump’s waiver of anti-Russia sanctions, and (not to be left out of the equation) the severe limiting of Elon Musk’s satellite feeds to Ukraine—comes at a time when Ukraine had been making some headway in the war and Russia had been suffering enormous setbacks. As for Xi, it isn’t clear whether the Chinese president had any advance knowledge of Putin’s proposal, but a summit would serve his interests as well. Xi doesn’t care much about Ukraine’s fate, or whether the war goes on for years or stops tomorrow. But he is keen to see the United States’ influence dwindle, and Trump’s abandonment of Ukraine would accelerate that trend. Europe, which still depends on America’s leadership of NATO for its defense, would seek to make separate deals for trade and security—as would much of Asia, which would view Trump’s retreat from Europe as a prelude to the scotching of alliances in general. And so the question at the moment is this: Will Trump actually agree to attend this summit? He said on Monday that he’d be open to meeting with Putin and Xi about “denuclearization,” but that evades whether he’d meet with them about Ukraine. It’s worth noting that, according to the Wall Street Journal article, Putin proposed it in the same conversation where he offered to sell the U.S. massive quantities of diesel fuel. Trump took that deal, in hopes that it would lower gasoline prices and thus boost the chance that the Republican Party retains its House and Senate majorities in the upcoming midterm elections. Is it significant that, even as he publicized the diesel deal, Trump said nothing about the summit—alluring as it must have been, and continue to be, in his quest for a peacemaker’s legacy (whatever the cost of “peace”). Most of his top aides would probably advise against the notion. Then again, those same aides advised against the war on Iran, and many of them weren’t even canvassed about the fuel deal. The lambasting that Trump has received for his diesel-fuel deal and his waiver of Russian sanctions—not only from almost all Democrats but from many Republicans—may have surprised him, and given him a taste of the outrage he’d incite by taking another step toward full appeasement of Putin. We can hope, but, as the saying goes, hope is not a strategy—nor can it offer any insight into how Trump might weigh his desperation for recognition as a powerful leader against the consequences of abandoning Ukraine, or even whether he understands those consequences. I Faced Huge Hurdles to Get Veteran Benefits. The Supreme Court Has a Chance to Make Things Better.
2026-10-19

I Faced Huge Hurdles to Get Veteran Benefits. The Supreme Court Has a Chance to Make Things Better.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. While stationed in Cold War–era Germany, Army infantryman Floyd Johnson experienced a training exercise that turned deadly. Decades later, while incarcerated in Florida, he was finally diagnosed with service-connected post-traumatic stress disorder from his experience. The Department of Veterans Affairs assigned him a 70 percent disability rating, later increasing it to 80 percent on appeal. However, because Johnson is incarcerated, the VA only pays his disability compensation at a 10 percent rate under a federal law governing benefits for incarcerated veterans. Johnson is challenging that law as unconstitutional. But first, the U.S. Supreme Court has to decide whether a district court will hear the case at all. At oral argument earlier this month, the government argued that it cannot. It said instead that Johnson must first pursue his claim through the VA’s administrative review process and then seek review in the Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims. This process can take years and requires veterans to navigate multiple layers of review before reaching a court, which is problematic enough. Worse though, the conventional understanding is that VA administrative bodies cannot declare an act of Congress unconstitutional, so Johnson would have to spend years in waiting for guaranteed administrative denials before ever setting foot in a court. The dispute arises from the Veterans’ Judicial Review Act of 1988 and the Supreme Court’s decision in Johnson v. Robison. In 1974, the court held that an earlier statute restricting judicial review of veterans’ benefits decisions did not prevent federal district courts from hearing constitutional challenges to a law governing veterans’ benefits. In response, Congress established the current system, routing most disputes through the VA and its specialized appellate courts. Johnson argues that in doing so, Congress did not eliminate district court jurisdiction over constitutional challenges to the statutes themselves. There are good reasons for specialized review systems. Veterans’ benefits law is governed by a complex constellation of federal statutes and regulations, and adjudicating claims often requires expertise in military records, medical evidence, and the connection between military service and disability. Congress established the current system to promote consistency and provide veterans with a dedicated process for challenging adverse decisions, but the benefits of specialized review are difficult to defend when a claimant must navigate years of administrative proceedings before a court can even consider resolving the legal question at the heart of the dispute. I know how much that delay can cost. I left the Army in 2009 with a general discharge under honorable conditions after behavior related to PTSD from my time in Iraq. Like it did Johnson, the government told me that I needed to go through a time-consuming administrative process with little chance of success before I could bring the case to a district court. About six years into trying to navigate that process with no success, by chance, I mentioned my case to the clinical professor in charge of the Veterans Legal Services Clinic at Yale Law School. The clinic took up my claim, bringing a class-action lawsuit challenging the Army’s handling of discharge upgrade applications. Even then, it took a small army of law students, professors, and pro bono attorneys, along with the direct intervention and oversight of the district court, for me to receive my honorable discharge and access to desperately needed benefits—nine years after being discharged with severe PTSD. As in Johnson’s case, the government moved to dismiss my lawsuit in part because I had not exhausted my administrative remedies. But my claim was that the administrative process itself was broken. The Army Discharge Review Board had repeatedly denied my applications without considering legal guidance from the Secretary of Defense to give liberal consideration to veterans whose service-connected mental health conditions contributed to their misconduct. I was told to keep pursuing the very process that I knew was not being conducted in accordance with the law. Fortunately, the judge disagreed with that demand. He rejected the government’s motion to dismiss and agreed to hear the case in district court. In response, the Army granted my discharge upgrade with no additional evidence and then moved to dismiss the class action because I had already been upgraded, which would have allowed it to continue illegally ignoring the secretary’s guidance. The judge again rejected the motion to dismiss, and then, only under the threat of further court intervention, the Army agreed to settle and allow new reviews for almost 60,000 veterans, now following the proper guidelines and giving liberal consideration to the impact of mental health conditions in each veteran’s conduct. The Army denied wrongdoing, but the settlement required it to reconsider cases and change procedures. Despite the Army’s earlier protestations, the sky didn’t fall when the court was allowed to get involved. Veterans just got another chance to have their cases fairly heard. Johnson’s case raises a different legal question since he is challenging the constitutionality of a statute rather than the administration of an existing policy, but my experience shows why courts should be wary of rules that require veterans to exhaust administrative remedies even when they allege that the system itself is failing them. Specialized processes can offer expertise and consistency, but they should not become barriers to judicial review when the costs of delay are high and the claimant’s legal argument cannot possibly be resolved through ordinary administrative decision making. The Supreme Court doesn’t need to dismantle the veterans’ review system to recognize that some cases warrant a more direct route to federal court. Johnson’s case asks whether Congress intended to foreclose district court review of constitutional challenges to veterans’ benefits laws. My case showed what can happen when the government insists that veterans keep navigating an administrative process even when that is the very system that they take legal issue with. Specialized review can provide expertise and consistency, but those benefits cannot justify making access to justice contingent on years of procedural hurdles. During my lawsuit, I often found myself wondering how many veterans had died waiting for benefits that might have saved them. That thought still haunts me. No court can give someone back years spent without healthcare, housing, or the education benefits they earned through military service and desperately need. When the government’s own procedures will not, or cannot, deliver the relief the law promises—or as in Johnson’s case, a potentially unconstitutional law cannot provide—veterans need somewhere else to turn. Access to an independent court cannot be a prize reserved for those lucky enough to find a lawyer and persistent enough to survive years of delay. It is a necessary safeguard against a government that fails to keep its promises to the people who served it. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. How going dual-native means VM/container co-existence and sovereign security in the AI era
2026-10-20

How going dual-native means VM/container co-existence and sovereign security in the AI era

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Seasoned techies recall with nostalgia a simpler time when the “single-stack” model of enterprise computing held as the default approach to solving interoperability headaches. Back then IT decision-makers were prepared to forfeit some choice and flexibility in how they specified IT systems in return for the availability and manageability that came with a homogenous operating environment. With the subsequent rise of multi-clouds, application-specific hardware, ever-changing business requirements, and the race for AI, the old “one-size-fits-all” playbook has been largely abandoned. Most IT estates have embraced technological diversity, some by design, some through standardization, some by inertia. Modern enterprise environments have become innately heterogeneous – and innately complex. They run a diverse combo of system software, hardware devices, cloud services, legacy applications, et al. The advance of AI has brought standard CPUs, specialized GPUs, fast networking fabrics, distributed data pipelines, and new deployment models that span private clouds, public clouds, edge – and ever-pressing ROI expectations. But that doesn’t mean that companies’ existing applications and data simply disappear. One trying but inevitable outcome of rapid AI deployment is the so-called “VM-container divide”. This is where critical business applications tipped to transition to containers continue to run on virtual machines, because cloud-native services are taking precedence, nabbing container and Kubernetes availability. So companies face the challenge of operationalizing AI, without being forced into complex migrations or creating new infrastructure silos. Dealing with this technological schism poses acute challenges for IT management. Many new AI deployments are going straight to containers, while existing data and applications – which the AI needs to access – continue to run on VMs. Running separate stacks for VMs and containers creates a number of issues, such as duplicate capacity plans, separate support contracts, and fragmented governance. And after years of trying to get beyond the pitfalls of siloed data, exasperated enterprises are now having to deal with siloed infrastructure. Separately managed VM and container environments often mean inefficient resource utilization. This in turn can lead to “stranded capacity” – installed computing resources that cannot be used to support live workloads. VM clusters are frequently underutilized while container pools (like Kubernetes) are overprovisioned, with no easy way to share resources without manual intervention. Furthermore, VM/container infrastructure fragmentation can create an operations bottleneck for agentic AI. Infrastructure fragmentation can complicate the deployment, integration, governance, and scaling of agents thus making it harder to operationalize and scale agentic AI securely and consistently. When AI agents cannot autonomously communicate across environments, their utility droops from autonomous problem-solvers to plain localised macros. Containers are gaining because of the widespread embrace of AI-enabled applications. Eighty-five percent of IT executives polled by Nutanix’s most recent Enterprise Cloud Index survey think that AI is accelerating their organization’s adoption of containers in a meaningful way; 29 percent think it is speeding-up adoption “to a great extent”. “AI is accelerating the shift to containers because machine learning models require strict dependency packing, while critical applications and data remain spread across both virtualised and containerised environments,” says Harsha Kotikela, Senior Director of Product & Solutions Marketing at Nutanix. Agents are multi-tier by nature he explains. “Inference is typically GPU-intensive and container-based, while agents are often CPU-intensive and container-based, and existing enterprise applications and data largely remain in VM environments. The challenge is to bring these tiers together so agents can work with enterprise applications and data, without forcing migration or creating additional infrastructure silos.” The application landscape, meanwhile, has become just as diverse. Many organisations simultaneously operate traditional enterprise applications running on VMs, containerised apps, distributed AI applications and intelligent agents, with workloads deployed across private cloud, public cloud, and edge. Each environment has its own management tools, operational processes, security rules and governance models. “Management capability has struggled to scale up commensurately,” says Kotikela, but adding more consoles or building separate operational teams for every technology stack does not necessarily solve this complexity: “In many cases, it increases it.” However, Kotikela challenges the notion that the predicament enterprises face in straddling the VM-container divide is intractable. Rather, the challenge is to move towards a unified operating model that allows them to operationalize agentic AI while retaining flexibility across their existing and new environments. “Rather than trying to work around heterogeneity, the objective now should be to make heterogeneous environments operate consistently with purposeful policies,” Kotikela says. “This is where Nutanix's dual-native architecture comes in, enabling enterprises to operationalize agentic AI across container-based inference and agent workloads alongside existing VM-based applications and data, through a consistent operating model.” The reality is that the VM-to-container migration trend is sluggish, Kotikela suggests. Few enterprises are starting from scratch with containers. Many have long-established business applications on legacy architectures, on top of which they are building container-based applications, but mostly not to replace what they already have running on VMs. Rather, they are prolonging their VM investment. It’s customer interest that’s informed how Nutanix provisions a governed, dual-native architecture through its Nutanix Cloud Platform – NCP – which runs traditional VMs and containerised/Kubernetes-based AI workloads concurrently on a single, consistent infrastructure. NCP’s hybrid-cloud infrastructure foundation unifies compute, storage, virtualisation and management across on-premises resources, public clouds and edge. It works in concert with Nutanix Enterprise AI (NAI), which provides the software capabilities to deploy, govern, and scale AI workloads. Technology partnerships are vital to building AI support functionality into Nutanix's AI portfolio, explains Kotikela. “Nutanix and NVIDIA work together to provide an integrated platform for enterprise AI, combining Nutanix’s hybrid-cloud infrastructure and software with NVIDIA’s accelerated computing and AI software. Nutanix Enterprise AI simplifies the deployment, management, and secure operation of AI models, integrating with NVIDIA NIM and NeMo microservices to support enterprise AI inference and agentic AI workflows.” Meanwhile, “Nutanix Kubernetes Platform provides the foundation for managing Kubernetes environments, while Nutanix Unified Storage delivers the high-performance data access required by AI workloads. Together, these capabilities help enterprises deploy and scale AI while maintaining control over their infrastructure, models, and data.” “In an ideal IT world you do not want to have to manage two different operational stacks – but the world of IT is rarely ‘ideal’,” Kotikela points out. “So you have to approach the challenge from a realistic perspective. You cannot bend your current stacks to a different reality without the risk of something breaking, and there’s no sense in that.” He adds: “A platform that unifies VMs and containers improves developer time-to-value by getting rid of those infrastructure silos, and providing a single control plane.” Rather than development teams having to manage separate pipelines for existing VMs and containerised applications, a unified platform “treats both as services of equal status and importance”. NAI delivers a unified and secure platform to deploy, manage and scale AI workloads, Kotikela says. It gives enterprises governance over their agents and models, full visibility over token usage, and streamlines AI development with a straightforward interface that integrates with existing infrastructure.” According to Kotikela the next phase of agentic AI will be measured by the ability to operate agents with governance, visibility and control: “That’s what we set out to solve with the newest NAI release. Release 2.8 continues to evolve NAI with new control and governance capabilities across Nutanix Agent Gateway and Nutanix Private Inference. This means organisations can run AI across VMs and containers, bridge public-cloud LLMs with private self-hosted models, and deploy AI where data already is.” Kotikela adds: “When I speak to customers, they tell me they want the freedom to choose between frontier and open-source models based on performance and cost, as well as the flexibility to run AI workloads in the cloud for experimentation and move them on-premises when they need greater control over their data or have sovereignty requirements.” For Kotikela the biggest challenge going forward is “operationalizing agentic AI - bringing together all the components required to make it work - from GPUs and underlying infrastructure to Kubernetes, AI models, inference engines, and gateways. These components must work together seamlessly across cloud, on-premises, and edge environments. Integrating and operating this multi-tier architecture requires significant time and effort from developers, IT architects, and infrastructure teams, diverting resources from delivering business outcomes.” This is where Nutanix delivers value, says Kotikela. “We’ve done the heavy lifting of bringing together the infrastructure and software components required to run Agentic AI - within a unified operating model across hybrid environments. This allows IT teams to focus less on integrating and managing the underlying stack and more on operationalizing AI to deliver business value.” Find out more about from Nutanix at NVIDIA GTC Berlin at booth #7042 SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Enterprises blow nearly a third of software budgets on upgrades they never asked for
2026-10-20

Enterprises blow nearly a third of software budgets on upgrades they never asked for

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Enterprises say nearly a third of their software budgets goes on upgrades, migrations, and licensing changes they wouldn't have chosen, according to research suggesting that keeping vendors happy has become an expensive business. The study, conducted by Censuswide for software support provider Origina and published with Thinkers50, surveyed 2,018 senior IT decision-makers in the US and UK. Respondents put the average share at 28 percent. And customers aren't necessarily getting anything better for their money. Some 95 percent of respondents said they had paid for at least one vendor-driven change in the past three years despite expecting worse reliability, performance, or value. The most recent vendor-driven change cost respondents' organizations an average of $12.87 million. Origina calls this the "software tax," but unlike most taxes, it comes with the added pleasure of potentially making your IT systems worse. The costs don't stop at software either. According to the survey, 91 percent of respondents said their organizations had retired working hardware in the past three years to meet software support deadlines, sacrificing an estimated 1.65 years of remaining useful life on average. Then there's the small matter of finding people to carry out all these supposedly essential changes. Some 84 percent said experienced engineers were regularly diverted from other projects to handle vendor-driven upgrades and migrations. That leaves less time for the work businesses actually want done, including the AI projects that software vendors themselves have spent the past few years telling everyone they urgently need. Asked about the last time an AI or data project was delayed, paused, or canceled, 90 percent said vendor-driven costs contributed at least moderately. While customers might be tempted to tell their suppliers where to stick their next upgrade, many apparently don't feel they have much choice. Some 76 percent said they have little negotiating leverage with their major software vendors. Origina founder and CEO Tomás O'Leary reckons customers have been backed into a corner. "When the alternative to an unwanted upgrade is losing support on systems the business runs on, saying yes is often a rational response. That's exactly the problem," he said. "The market has left some of the most experienced technology leaders in the world with little room to refuse, and that cost shows up in budgets, in engineering time and in innovation that never gets funded." The findings also suggest that IT bosses are getting rather fed up with the arrangement. Some 91 percent backed legislation that would give enterprise software customers protections comparable to those enjoyed by long-term residential tenants, while 82 percent supported software maintenance requirements similar to EU hardware repairability rules. Origina has a commercial interest in the argument: it sells independent support for enterprise software, offering customers an alternative to vendors' own maintenance and upgrade arrangements. Still, the findings suggest plenty of customers feel they're spending millions following their suppliers' priorities rather than their own. ® SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end The first technology created with a self-disrupt button...
