HPE Spaceborne Computer 4 to launch AI straight to the Moon aboard Astrolab’s FLIP rover
2026-10-20

HPE Spaceborne Computer 4 to launch AI straight to the Moon aboard Astrolab’s FLIP rover

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end Forget space datacenters. Before the end of the year, Hewlett Packard Enterprise (HPE) will have AI running on the Moon. This isn’t some exec’s loony fantasy either. HPE has been building orbital compute platforms for the International Space Station (ISS) for years. Now, it’s sending its fourth-gen Spaceborne Computer to the Moon aboard Astrolab’s Flex Lunar Innovation Platform (FLIP) lunar rover. Part of NASA’s Commercial Lunar Payload Services (CLPS) program, the rover is set to be deployed near the Moon’s south pole by the Voyager Technologies Griffin-1 lander later this year. Assuming a safe landing on the surface, FLIP will serve as a test bed for a range of emerging technologies including HPE’s latest space-faring compute platform. Specifically, Spaceborne Computer 4, which for the sake of brevity we’ll just call SBC4, will carry out a number of AI and HPC-centric experiments during the rover’s year long mission on the lunar pole. The actual silicon powering SBC4 is still a bit of a mystery. We’re told the reason for the secrecy largely comes down to the high-risk nature of the mission. If the hardware fails, HPE’s compute partner doesn’t want their name tied to it. No guts but for the glory, we suppose, though Kioxia doesn't seem too worried. What we do know is that it is equipped with a low-power central processing unit, a graphics processor, and will be equipped with a pair of mirrored Kioxia SSDs totaling 1 TB of capacity. That said, while it'll be among the most powerful computers on the Moon, it's still a pretty small system. The FLIP rover doesn't have an unlimited power or thermal budget. The rover's radiators can only dissipate so much heat. Measuring in at around 5x10 inches and about an inch thick, while not a particularly big system, it's still large enough to house any number of existing compute platforms. For all we know, it could be powered by Nvidia’s GB10, AMD’s Xilinx Versal AI, or the guts of an old smartphone. We won't know until the thing powers up. Less of a mystery is what SBC4 will be running and where. NASA plans to run a series of tuned LLM tests on the system, including a retrieval augmented generation (RAG) system to assist with searching and interpreting onboard documents. Additionally, the system will be tasked with running machine learning algorithms against camera and LiDAR data. However, without knowing how large these LLMs are, it’s hard to infer the nature of the underlying hardware. We’re told to expect more details about the chips powering SBC4 once it’s up and running. Alongside AI, SBC4 will also run several high performance computing (HPC) benchmarks designed to push the system to its limits. These benchmarks include high performance Linpack (HPL) and high performance conjugate gradient (HPCG), which means HPE and Astrolab will soon hold the distinction of the first Top500 submission made from a celestial body. Not only do HPE’s systems power some of the fastest supercomputers on Earth, but they could soon hold the number one spot on the Moon — not that that’s a particularly high bar. Beyond bragging rights, a workload like HPL could have other benefits like helping to keep the rover from icing over during the roughly 14-day-long night cycles the rover will repeatedly endure over the course of its mission. The Rover and by extension SBC4 will have to contend with extreme vibration stresses during launch and landing, wild temperature swings, not to mention cosmic rays and solar radiation since the Moon lacks the thick atmosphere and magnetic field that shields terrestrial compute. But, as it turns out, radiation might not be as big a problem as you’d think. According to Fernandez, SBC4 is largely assembled from off-the-shelf components, including the solid state drives. The compute platform is using standard Kioxia BG6 M.2 drives with the main line of protection being a software RAID 1. “It’s a silent mirror. The boot order is to try this and if something has happened to it, go here,” Mark Fernandez, HPE’s chief scientist of space tech and solutions, explained. “With our nine years' experience in the Space Station using drives, we’ve even convinced them we don’t need to do anything special for space.” However, this wasn’t always the thinking. As we reported at the time, early Spaceborne Computers suffered from high SSD failure rates. But as it turned out, so did the same drives deployed terrestrially. The cause of the failures wasn’t cosmic rays or energetic particles from the Sun, but rather bad capacitors, he explained. “I’ve had zero SSD failure since 2019.” While off-the-shelf electronics are surprisingly radiation resistant — at least within FLIP’s mission parameters — HPE has invested heavily in software based redundancy and recovery. Fernandez calls this “hardware hardening in software.” The idea being that the spare CPU cycles are used to continuously monitor and compare hardware metrics against a state table. If a parameter exceeds the specified threshold, SBC4 will take action to protect itself. “At a high level, I say, I like to be running at full speed. I can't do that. I'll run at a slower speed. If I can't do that, I'd like to be booted, but not running anything, and then maybe the server will recover. And worst case, I'd like to be powered down rather than get destroyed,” he explained. FLIP is primarily intended as a proof of concept vehicle, which is to say it’s a test bed for technologies that may eventually find their way to the Moon on other missions. The inclusion of SBC4 reflects the realities of operating at the far edge of Earth’s orbit. The latency between Earth and the Moon is nowhere near as bad as Mars's up to 44 minute round-trip communication time, but even 2.5 seconds makes real-time control from Houston, or in this case Astrolab’s mission control, which by the way is also powered by HPE's Greenlake Private Cloud stack, in Hawthorne, California a bit unwieldy. Further complicating matters is the bandwidth afforded the rover. When Artemis 2 traveled around the Moon earlier this year, astronauts weren’t able to send back high-res photographs in real time due to the lack of bandwidth. It was only after the mission was on its way back to Earth that they were able to establish a big enough pipe to transmit the full-res images. For this reason, SBC4 is designed to operate “at the edge of the edge,” Fernandez said. Given the bandwidth constraints, it’s far easier to process as much of the data and telemetry gathered by the rover’s array of cameras, lidars, and environmental sensors locally. “It’s important to ship back insight to Earth rather than all the raw data whenever possible. So having the edge compute capability to process the raw data locally, save on bandwidth back to Earth,” Astrolab CEO Jaret Matthews said. Matthews isn’t the only one to share this perspective. Earlier this year, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang described the challenges and opportunities of space-based computing with analysts during the company’s Q1 earnings call. "There are many different computing problems that really want to be done in space," he said at the time. "One of the best use cases of GPUs in space is imaging. To be able to do imaging at very large, very high resolutions, extremely large scales and very, very fast, it's hard to do that by sending petabytes and petabytes of imaging data back here on Earth and doing that work, it's easier just to do it out in space." Though others' ambitions are far greater. Google, Amazon, and SpaceX are all touting orbital datacenters powered by the Sun as the answer to terrestrial power constraints — Elon Musk wants you to believe he'll eventually be manufacturing deep-space datacenters on the Moon and lobbing them into the far reaches of the solar system using mass drivers — the economics of getting those bit barns into low Earth orbit remain unproven and so far unviable. Despite this, executives and administrators at HPE, Astrolab, Kioxia, and NASA believe that space-based datacenters are still feasible long term. HPE’s Spaceborne Computer 4 will be one of several payloads aboard the FLIP rover. FLIP will be the largest rover launched by the US since the Apollo program’s manned Moon buggy. And it is by no means small. Designed by Astrolab while measuring roughly 7 feet (2.13 meters) in length and weighing about half a ton, the rover is packed to the gills with cameras, sensors, and even a projector which will eventually be used in place of LCDs on future manned missions to the lunar surface. For FLIP’s purposes, it’ll mostly be used to project partners' logos onto the Moon’s surface as a sorta advertising gimmick to keep the lights on back at Astrolab’s facilities in Hawthorne while it works on its FLEX rover, which Matthews characterizes as a sort of F150 or Caterpillar tractor for the Moon. Beyond the scientific equipment, solar panels, and radiators, arguably FLIP’s most striking feature is its disproportionately large wheels. Developed by Venturi Space, along with the rover’s battery pack, these wheels are designed to function much like a pneumatic tire, just without air or rubber. Their size is also intentional. The same wheels found on FLIP are intended for use on NASA’s CLV-1 rover, which is slated to launch in 2028. Much like FLIP offers HPE as a proving ground to test its lunar compute platform, the rover will do the same for Venturi’s wheels. Spaceborne Computer 4 and FLIP are slated to launch aboard a SpaceX Falcon Heavy rocket later this year and will fly to the Moon’s south pole by Astrobotic’s Griffin-1 lander. Griffin is classified as a medium class lander measuring roughly 15 feet around and 6.5 feet tall (that’s about 4.5 and 2 meters for those of you living in a nation civilized enough to use metric outside the sciences). The lander is equipped with seven main engines responsible for everything from trans-lunar injection, trajectory correction, lunar orbit insertion, and powered descent to the surface. Four clusters of attitude control thrusters, meanwhile, will (hopefully) keep the thing pointed the right way up. FLIP is expected to touch down near the Mons Mouton area along the edge of the South Pole before the end of the year. ® SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... 'Password1' and a forgotten test account land Welsh outfits in hot water
2026-10-20

'Password1' and a forgotten test account land Welsh outfits in hot water

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
ICO reprimands Ogi Networks and Data Cymru after ransomware breach exposes info of 3,300 people, mostly children A test account protected by "Password1" helped an intruder steal personal data relating to 3,300 people, mostly children, in a ransomware attack that has drawn regulatory rebukes for two Welsh organizations. The Information Commission's Office (ICO) issued reprimands rather than fines to Ogi Networks Limited (ONL) and the Local Government Data Unit – Wales, also known as Data Cymru. According to the ICO, altnet ONL provided server and connectivity infrastructure, licensing, and support to Data Cymru, which helps public bodies across Wales make effective use of data. On November 1, 2024, Data Cymru suffered a ransomware attack after a miscreant exploited a test account called Test2 to infiltrate its network and encrypt 182.7 GB of data housed in a file server and an internal database server. "Before deploying the ransomware, the threat actor exfiltrated 42 GB of data from a primary domain controller, a file server and an SQL server," the ICO said on Tuesday in its reprimand of ONL [PDF]. ONL told the ICO it created the test account on January 13, 2012, during its initial project for Data Cymru. "ONL confirmed that the creation of the 'Test2' account was likely intended to test user account access before migrating 'real' users to its services," the ICO said. "When it was created, the 'Test2' account was given the password of 'Password1'." The ICO cited Bitwarden's password strength tool, which rated "Password1" as "very weak" and estimated a cracking time of less than a second. "The local administrator accounts on the three impacted systems all had the same password. This meant that once one system had been breached, the local account hashes could be collected and used by the threat actor to authenticate access to other systems on the Data Cymru network," the regulator added. "ONL confirmed that it did not carry out any reviews of local administrator accounts following their initial deployment. This meant these accounts remained active, regardless of whether they were used, and continued to use the same default passwords set at the time of their creation in January 2012, as part of ONL's initial project with Data Cymru, for more than 12.5 years." The data watchdog reprimanded ONL under Article 32 of the UK GDPR because it failed to put in place "appropriate technical and organisational measures" to ensure processing systems and services were safe, and it didn't regularly assess or evaluate those measures. It found ONL had no auditing procedures to review user accounts within Data Cymru's Active Directory. The ICO's separate reprimand of Data Cymru [PDF] also detailed failures in its security arrangements. "Password1 is not an appropriate password, it is a wide-open door to nefarious actors," said ICO head of investigations David Doodson. "This incident shows how a single weak password, attached to an account that was no longer needed, can leave the door open to a serious cyber-attack that can affect thousands. "The responsibility for these failures did not sit with one organisation alone. ONL created the test account whilst Data Cymru retained overall control of its network, including the ability to instruct ONL to create or remove accounts. Neither had put in place sufficiently clear and effective measures to ensure that old accounts were routinely identified and removed." ® SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Google Cloud says a 'power event' caused today's outage
2026-10-20

Google Cloud says a 'power event' caused today's outage

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Fells London zone, taking banks, parcel firm and property sites with it – and recovery is 'tentatively' due by 1300 UTC Google Cloud has blamed a “power event” for taking down multiple online products for customers in Western Europe, and is pledging to restore normal service within the hour. The unplanned downtime for customers began at 0608 UTC when europe-west-2-b, one of three local availability zones in the London region, started playing up. According to an update on its status page, Google Cloud said at 1158 UTC that full recovery workloads should be “completed, tentatively” by 1300 UTC. Customers can expect another update at that point, with Google either giving the thumbs up, thumbs sideways or having to admit engineers are still working on a fix. Services that went AWOL included BigQuery Data TransferService, Cloud Filestore, Cloud Run, Google App Engine, Google Cloud NetApp Volumes, Google Kubernetes Engine, and Managed Service for Apache Kafka. So what caused online services to melt at customers including Lloyds Bank, HSBC, Royal Mail, Rightmove, DPD, BT, Cloudflare, Screwfix and Revolut? Power. "A power event caused an outage within europe-west2-b zone. The power event is mitigated. We are seeing the recovery of services and are prioritizing the recovery of existing workloads.Customers may not be able to yet create new workloads." The Register has asked Google for further elucidation but it said the only information is available on the status page. The "fragility" of the cloud model is again on display, Dr Amro Al-Said Ahmad, a lecturer in computer science at Keele University told The Register. He estimates that a total of 38 services were down and out for the count. "The incident highlights the dependency effect of modern cloud ecosystems. When a core cloud region experiences failures, the impact extends far beyond the directly affected services. Applications, APIs, databases, authentication systems, payment platforms, analytics pipelines, and third-party SaaS products that depend on those services can all experience cascading failures." "A problem in a single cloud region can therefore propagate through an entire digital supply chain, affecting organisations that are several layers removed from the original outage. While this is a regional outage centered on europe-west2, the breadth of affected services highlights a key cloud engineering lesson: resilience is not achieved by simply moving to the cloud. True resilience requires designing for regional failures through multi-region deployments, workload portability, and tested disaster recovery procedures." ® SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Bullhorn sees red after cyberattack knocks recruitment platform offline
2026-10-20

Bullhorn sees red after cyberattack knocks recruitment platform offline

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Staffing software giant calls in CrowdStrike as customers face login headaches and questions remain over possible data theft Bullhorn is the latest tech giant finding itself on the wrong end of a cyberattack, forcing the recruitment software provider to shut down its platform while it investigates. The US-based company, which serves more than 10,000 customers worldwide, says its systems are now back online in the UK, US, and Australia. Unfortunately for customers hoping to get back to work, online doesn't necessarily mean working. In its latest status update, published on Tuesday, Bullhorn confirmed that some users were still struggling with single sign-on (SSO), password resets, and customizations, while several products continued to report degraded performance. Things started going sideways on Friday, October 16, when Bullhorn flagged problems getting job postings onto Indeed. By the following morning, the platform was suffering a full-blown outage, and Bullhorn confirmed later that day that it was dealing with something rather more serious than a technical hiccup. "We have detected a cyber incident on the Bullhorn Platform. We have temporarily disabled access to our systems in order to contain the activity while we investigate further," the company said, noting that it had roped in cybersecurity specialists from CrowdStrike to help investigate the attack and restore its services. For recruitment agencies using Bullhorn, the outage meant losing access to candidate records, job applications, placements and, in some cases, the systems used to get people paid. Services began returning on Monday, but recovery has been anything but seamless. As of Tuesday morning, Bullhorn's status page still listed degraded performance across several products, including its automation tools, Talent Platform, Onboarding365 and Time & Expense. While Bullhorn is making progress getting customers back into their accounts, it has yet to answer arguably the bigger question: whether the attackers stole anything. In its customer FAQ, it said a forensic investigation is ongoing, and has not yet determined whether attackers exfiltrated customer data. Bullhorn says it will contact affected customers if the investigation finds their information was compromised, and it also warned people to watch out for opportunistic crooks looking to exploit the chaos. "Threat actors may pose as Bullhorn personnel via email, text, or phone calls, using the incident as a pretext to request credentials or sensitive information," it warned on Tuesday, stressing that its employees would never ask customers to disclose passwords, multi-factor authentication codes, or verification tokens. The company has also required password resets for users who don’t log in through SSO, while customers using API integrations have been instructed to rotate their credentials. The cause of the intrusion remains unknown, and Bullhorn has not publicly identified the attackers or confirmed whether ransomware was involved. The company didn’t respond to The Register’s questions at publication time. The outage may be coming to an end, but Bullhorn's investigation into what the attackers actually did is far from over. ® SPONSORED FEATURE: Nutanix says running VMs and containers within the same operating environment shouldn’t be a brake on AI ambitions Keeping vendors happy is costing businesses millions, even when everything was working fine in the first place Mystery silicon and mirrored SSDs set to touch down before year's end The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Over $963,000 worth of graded Pokémon and sports cards were stolen from Fedex
2026-10-20

Over $963,000 worth of graded Pokémon and sports cards were stolen from Fedex

The Verge | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Last week over 200 packages of graded trading cards being shipped back to their owners were stolen from a Fedex truck parked outside the headquarters of Professional Sports Authenticator (PSA) in Santa Ana, California, according to a report by The Athletic. PSA is one of the largest third-party sports and hobby card grading companies in the world and it reported to the Santa Ana Police Department that the majority of the stolen packages had an approximate value of over $963,000, but that amount could further increase as PSA continues the process of confirming exactly which cards included in the shipment were taken. In a statement shared to its website, Collectors Holdings, the parent company of PSA, acknowledged the theft and that it’s working closely with local law enforcement and Fedex as the investigation continues. In response to the incident the company says it has “notified marketplace partners so they can be on the lookout for rare and serialized items” to help prevent the stolen cards from being resold. All cards graded by PSA are assigned a unique certification number that the company has since deactivated for all the cards that have so far been identified as stolen. Collectors Holdings also says it has contacted affected customers to arrange compensation based on the fair market value of their missing collectibles, but “up to the maximum insured value for each item selected by the customer prior to grading,” according to the report. In a video posted over the weekend, YouTuber Sky-B Cards identified themselves as one of the victims of the theft and shared the response they were sent by PSA. They had paid $8,468 to have 104 collectible sports cards graded by the company, many of which are irreplaceable, and had opted to insure the order for up to $156,000. It is not yet known what compensation they have received. Other collectors affected by the incident have started to reach out to another YouTuber, Boston Card Hunter, who is working to compile a shared list of serialized cards that were stolen including detailed descriptions of each one in hopes it can help other collectors identify them. Spotify pulled 50 million AI slop and spam tracks from its recommendations

The Verge | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Spotify has also made changes to its music recommendation system to highlight songs from human artists. Spotify has also made changes to its music recommendation system to highlight songs from human artists. Spotify says it has removed more than 50 million songs considered AI-generated slop or spam from user recommendations over the past year. That’s according to an announcement on Tuesday, which says Spotify has also made changes to its recommendation system to highlight more music made by human artists. “Our editors hold the same standard and don’t program content identified as slop or spam,” Spotify says. “Since we made these changes, we’ve seen a marked decrease in listener concerns about this content showing up in recommendations.” Though Spotify has stopped short of mandating labels on all AI tracks, it launched an “AI Persona” badge for artists that use an AI identity and don’t represent a real person. It also won’t recommend music from AI personas. Other music streaming platforms are taking a tougher stance on AI music, with YouTube Music putting labels on AI songs, Deezer giving users an option to filter out AI slop, and Bandcamp banning AI music entirely. Spotify says it uses a combination of listener engagement and feedback, upload patterns, metadata, and third-party AI detection tools to determine whether a track may be AI-generated slop. Last year, the company said it took down more than 75 million songs identified as spam. The previously unreported depositions of TikTok staffers were filed in federal court in March, and connected to a multi-district litigation initiated in 2022 that brings together hundreds of lawsuits alleging that TikTok, Meta, Snap, and Google knew that they social media platforms are harmful to children, and failed to disclose those risks. One deposition from Reagen Maher, who held senior safety and investigations positions between 2021 and 2024, described flagging the prevalence of “transactional sexual content” to then-TikTok CEO V Pappas, regarding minors stripping on TikTok’s Live feature in exchange for “gifts” that can be converted into real currency. Forbes reported on the issue in 2022, and Maher said the company was already aware of the sexual exploitation concerns before the article was published. “I have been fighting about fixing this for months with people who didn’t think the harms are severe enough,” Maher said in an excerpt from an internal chat with a colleague. “Put simply, senior leaders know about this issue. There are some ongoing projects to fix parts of it, and frankly, I’m hoping [that] this article puts a fire behind other potential solutions.” Another deposition from TikTok’s former head of safety Eric Han says he was concerned about harmful algorithm “rabbit holes” in 2020, describing them as having a “real contagion effect” regarding “suicidal ideation and behavior” in an internal TikTok report. Despite flagging that “minor safety is an urgent issue that we must address effectively,” Han said that TikTok failed to warn parents of the potential dangers of their child using the platform. Adobe Lightroom mobile can now open closed eyes in photos with a simple tap

Adobe Lightroom mobile can now open closed eyes in photos with a simple tap

The Verge | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
When the mobile app detects closed eyes in a photo it will present users with a new Open Eyes Quick Action tool.﻿ When the mobile app detects closed eyes in a photo it will present users with a new Open Eyes Quick Action tool.﻿ If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Adobe announced yesterday that the mobile version of Lightroom, available for iOS, iPadOS, and Android, can now open closed eyes in photos using generative AI with just a single tap. When the app detects a person, or a group of people, have their eyes closed in a photo it will automatically present users with a new Open Eyes Quick Action that appears as an icon over the image providing easy access to the new feature. Being able to easily save an otherwise great photo by opening a subject’s eyes has been a feature in Adobe’s Photoshop Elements for nearly a decade, but as DPReview points out, Elements sources the replacement from another photo of the subject with mixed results depending on variations between the two images. Open Eyes in Lightroom mobile instead relies on generative AI so the replacement eyes match the lighting and pose, and the app will use other images of the same person in your Lightroom library as reference so the open eyes will accurately match the color, size, and shape of their own. You can perform a similar fix in other Adobe apps like Photoshop using its own generative AI tools, but it’s not a one-tap fix, and it’s not free. Those using the free version of the Lightroom mobile app can try the Open Eyes feature twice before they’re given a prompt to subscribe to an Adobe Creative Cloud plan that includes more credits for its various AI-powered tools, according to DPReview. Wii Sports is back on the Switch 2, and it rules
2026-10-20

Wii Sports is back on the Switch 2, and it rules

The Verge | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Nintendo Switch Sports Resort is quietly one of the console’s most important games. Nintendo Switch Sports Resort is quietly one of the console’s most important games. Wii Sports holds an important place in Nintendo’s history. It’s the best-selling piece of software the company has ever made, one that permeated culture in surprising ways, and its success helped rejuvenate Nintendo after a period of steady decline. It also kicked off one of the company’s most important and unassuming franchises. While subsequent sports titles from Nintendo never sold quite as well as the original Wii Sports, they’ve become a fixture for each new console, much like a new Mario Kart or Pokémon. And now the Switch 2 has its own version, Nintendo Switch Sports Resort, and it’s the best so far. You’d be forgiven for not realizing that Nintendo Switch Sports Resort is launching this week. The game is coming out during a particularly hectic time for new video game releases, and it’s sandwiched between two flashier Switch 2 titles in Fire Emblem: Fortune’s Weave and the upcoming remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. Amid all of that, it’s easy to forget a game about thumb wrestling and bowling. But Sports Resort is also a refined and expansive version of the concept, and one that will likely become a constant over the Switch 2’s life. The core of the game is the same as always: giving you a bunch of athletic activities — they’re not exactly all “sports” — that require some form of motion controls to play. That means swinging a Joy-Con controller to simulate using a tennis racket or tossing a bowling ball. There are 12 sports in all, and they vary quite a bit. The aforementioned returning favorites tennis and bowling are joined by things like skateboarding, thumb wrestling, and flying a plane. What makes the game work is its simplicity. Almost each activity is incredibly easy to pick up; I don’t know much about golf, but it only took me a minute or so to get down the fundamentals of swinging a club using a Joy-Con. This means that Sports Resort excels as an arcade-style experience, the kind of game you pull out when friends are over and you want to have some fun. Thumb wrestling is ideal for this. It’s so simple — you move your hand back and forth and use a single button to try and “pin” your opponent — yet gets extremely intense and fun in the competitive two-player mode. And unlike IRL thumb wrestling, no one can actually cheat. There are some exceptions to the general simplicity. I still find basketball to be a little too complex, and I just can’t seem to figure out a successful strategy in table tennis. But there are enough games in Sports Resort that you’re almost certain to find a handful of favorites you’ll stick with, so having a few duds isn’t the end of the world. There’s also a decent amount of depth, both in terms of the skill ceiling for games — I have spent hours trying to perfect the spin on my bowling release — but also the various modes for each sport. Bowling, for instance, includes the returning option to play with 100 pins at once, which is chaotic as it sounds. But the biggest addition to Sports Resort is that it now takes place in an open world. That may sound strange for a collection of arcade games, but it also allows it to introduce three new activities that are centered on exploring the beautiful island setting. There’s prop plane (where you hold the Joy-Con like a paper airplane to fly), power cruising (where you hold two Joy-Con horizontally, like the handlebars of a Jet Ski), and skateboarding (where you use the Joy-Con’s mouse controls to simulate controlling a tiny skateboard). There are competitive ways to play these games, like a skateboard race, but I’ve spent most of my time with a sightseeing mode where the goal is to find as many landmarks and secrets across the resort as you can within a five-minute time limit. It’s sort of like a more fleshed-out version of the free roam mode in Mario Kart World. The island is surprisingly detailed and interesting to explore, with lots of caves and hidden paths, and the three modes of transportation let you check it out via land, water, and air. These exploration-focused modes add a nice new dimension to the experience, fitting snugly within the more traditional sports while offering a very different vibe. I’ve become particularly drawn to skateboarding — there’s something very satisfying about pulling back on the Joy-Con to simulate pushing a board — especially since you can collect items that let you purchase new clothes and other accessories to customize your character. One of the biggest keys to Nintendo’s success during the Switch era has been consistency. Even in the midst of a tumultuous period for the broader games industry, the company has managed to put out a steady stream of games, many of which have had a surprisingly long life. Nintendo’s biggest hits aren’t always what you expect. The first Nintendo Switch Sports has sold more than 18 million copies, topping beloved games like Super Mario Bros. Wonder and Splatoon 3. A little over a year into its existence, that style of perennial seller is exactly what the Switch 2 needs, the kind of game that people pick up alongside Mario Kart when they buy a new console. Wii Sports showed just how transformative a game like that can be, and Nintendo Switch Sports Resort is quietly keeping that spirit alive. Nintendo Switch Sports Resort launches on the Switch 2 on October 22nd. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Final Fantasy dips back into the SNES golden era with Resonance
2026-10-20

Final Fantasy dips back into the SNES golden era with Resonance

The Verge | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Square Enix’s latest HD-2D game carries the Final Fantasy name — and a lot of what makes the franchise great. Square Enix’s latest HD-2D game carries the Final Fantasy name — and a lot of what makes the franchise great. Final Fantasy is a lot different than it used to be. While many of the early Final Fantasy titles look and play similarly to each other, the series has since grown to encompass blockbuster games like Final Fantasy XVI and the gargantuan Final Fantasy VII Remake trilogy, which don’t play much like the classics. But there’s still an audience for retro RPGs and old-school Final Fantasy games, especially thanks to the HD-2D style that mixes gorgeous pixel art with detailed 3D graphics to great effect. Square Enix has made a bunch of HD-2D games already, like the remakes of early Dragon Quest titles, but this week, it’s launching the first HD-2D Final Fantasy game: Final Fantasy Resonance. Resonance, thanks to the HD-2D art, kind of looks like what the classic Final Fantasy games feel like in my memory, but I was surprised at how old-school Resonance feels to actually play, too — which, for the most part, is a good thing. In Resonance, I’ve spent a lot of time wandering around a big world map to get from place to place, tackling random battles along the way and investigating little sparkles to pick up items. The towns have a familiar feel, with the classic inn, item shop, and weapons shop setup. Similarly, battles are traditional turn-based fights, so I’ve found myself spending a lot of time in various battle menus to plan out my attacks. Enemies even flash right before they attack, just like in older RPGs. At times, Resonance can almost feel rudimentary; I’ve recently been working my way through Final Fantasy VII Remake again ahead of Revelation, and Remake’s action-heavy fights feel much more contemporary. But there’s something soothing about Resonance’s familiar patterns. I really like just grinding through some random baddies to level up. The story is pretty standard fare, at least during the 10 hours I’ve played. You play as Rain, a plucky blonde hero who is morally pure and fights for those less fortunate than him. After a great magical crystal is destroyed, you and your best bud Lasswell head out on an adventure to stop the destruction of other crystals. So far, Resonance’s story hasn’t done a lot to innovate on a familiar formula — I’ve already come across two people who have amnesia, a JRPG staple — but it all moves along breezily enough that it’s not a huge deal. Resonance isn’t all old-school, though, as there are a lot of modern touches. In fights, enemies have health bars and stagger bars, forcing you to be strategic instead of just mashing basic attacks; it’s usually smarter to whittle down a stagger bar by exploiting weaknesses through magic and skills. You can speed up fights and even skip long attack animations (of which there are many). On the world map, you can fast travel to any location you’ve already visited, which is especially useful during the multipart side quests. In dungeons, you can teleport from a save near a boss fight back to the entrance if you need to resupply before a big battle. Resonance also lets you bring in characters from other Final Fantasy games to aid you in battle. (It’s a holdover from Resonance’s roots as a mobile title; the game is an “adaptation” of the first story arc of Final Fantasy Brave Exvius, which was shut down in 2024.) As you move through Resonance, you’ll recruit “Visions” of characters like Final Fantasy VI’s Terra Branford or Final Fantasy VII’s Cloud Strife, and each of the game’s characters can “equip” one Vision to give them new moves in battle. Over time, while using a Vision with a Resonance character, you unlock more moves and abilities that you can use even when you don’t have that Vision equipped. It’s a clever system that offers a lot of possibilities for interesting builds, making the hunt for more Visions feel like more than just a way to get a hit of nostalgia. But I’m already getting sucked into Resonance like I used to with classic Final Fantasy titles. At one point, as I was nodding my head to heart-pumping music during an intense boss fight on a moving train, I realized I was completely absorbed by the game in a way that only a Final Fantasy can pull off. The game’s old-school structure with modern improvements lets Square Enix have it both ways, as the old-school approach sits comfortably alongside a big-budget game like Final Fantasy XVI. The series has come a long way since the games that Resonance uses as inspiration, but it pulls in enough from newer titles that playing it feels like enjoying the best of both worlds. Final Fantasy Resonance launches on October 22nd on Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, and Xbox Series X / S. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. MIT Technology Review
AI adoption by industry shows what other sectors can learn
2026-10-20

AI adoption by industry shows what other sectors can learn

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
The current narrative around artificial intelligence in the workplace is largely driven by Silicon Valley. But treating the tech sector as an island misses the actual trends in technology adoption–tech is just the first industry in line. As generative AI and automation tools mature, the shifts currently reshaping software engineering and product management will inevitably filter into healthcare, education, retail, and manufacturing. The variation in adoption rates today isn't a sign that non-tech industries are falling behind, but rather a reflection of the different speeds at which industries absorb new workflows. For leaders and their teams outside of tech, this differential pace provides a practical blueprint for how AI adoption unfolds, and where the opportunities lie. Recent survey data from TripleTen highlights just how stark the current adoption curve is across different sectors of the economy (see Figure 1). Figure 1: The AI adoption curve remains steep across industries, but non-tech industry adoption rates are increasing. While tech leads the way, finance emerges as a clear second: 48% of finance workers have been asked to use AI chatbots, and 32% have been asked to automate parts of their workflow. This demonstrates how rapidly knowledge work integrates AI once clear use cases emerge. By contrast, sectors like healthcare (18% asked to use chatbots) and education (25%) reflect lower initial exposure. Because these broad industry categories encompass a mix of specialized hands-on professionals, clinical or instructional personnel, and back-office administrative staff, universal adoption was never going to happen overnight. Integrating automated tools into compliance-heavy or human-facing operations requires a more deliberate pace, which is reflected by the lower numbers. Across all surveyed industries, 79% of workers report being asked to learn at least one skill they didn't previously have. Looking at the specific competencies demanded in tech today offers a preview of the skill sets likely to shift into other sectors (see Figure 2). Figure 2: Workforces across industries are learning new skills. Although some of these sound like niche skills confined to tech, many of them will translate to frontline work across sectors—from hospital systems managing patient logistics to school districts processing administrative reporting. One skill gets less attention than it should. Only 30% of managers and owners call judging whether AI output is right or wrong an absolute must, which puts it sixth on a list of seven. As AI tools reach more industries, this is the skill that catches mistakes before they cause problems. It is also where work experience helps most: To spot a wrong answer, you need to know what the right one looks like. Early AI adoption offers clear professional leverage: TripleTen’s survey data shows that when managers learned a new skill or automation process from an employee, 88% of those employee-led AI innovations became standard company practice. In early-adopting industries, employees who identify repetitive administrative tasks and use AI tools to streamline them have a voice in shaping organizational policy. As healthcare networks, educational institutions, and retail operations begin broader rollouts of AI-enabled software, workers who understand how to validate outputs, prompt models, and automate routine tasks will hold a strong advantage. Being the person who can streamline administrative overhead promises better chances for retention, promotions, and wage growth. Ana Riabova, the AI strategy and enablement lead at TripleTen, says, “Everyone talks about saving time, but the real win with early adoption is expanding what you can actually do. People who get ahead are using it to handle things they used to have to hand off. It doesn't matter how senior or junior you are. You keep your core skills, but suddenly you aren't boxed in by them anymore.” If non-tech industries absorb these tools more gradually, the challenge for workers lies in getting access to quality learning resources. Workers’ out-of-pocket investment in training varies significantly by industry: Nearly half of healthcare and education workers spent nothing out of pocket on professional development last year. Furthermore, tech workers actively leverage a larger variety of learning channels (see Figure 3). Figure 3: Professional development efforts mirror the AI adoption curve. Tech workers are nearly twice as likely as all workers to use AI chatbots as learning tools (48% vs. 27%), and more than twice as likely as workers outside tech (21%), effectively using AI to accelerate their acquisition of other skills. However, ask workers whom they trust most to teach them a new job skill, and they choose someone currently doing that job (31%). Fewer pick an official certification program (19%), an AI tool (16%), or a university professor (6%). The difference in AI adoption rates across industries is not a story of who is succeeding and who is failing. Rather, it’s a timeline of how technology diffuses through an economy. Tech and finance are testing the mechanics of AI integration today. For workers in healthcare, education, retail, and manufacturing, the current phase provides a valuable window to observe which skills generate real productivity gains—allowing them to build those competencies before they become standard industry expectations. This content was produced by TripleTen. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. MIT Technology Review
How to study a swiftly melting climate disaster
2026-10-20

How to study a swiftly melting climate disaster

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Andrew Haverdink, a tall grad student in a rain bib and rubber boots, wrenched a double-handed drill from the frozen soil that underlies much of Alaska’s Denali National Park and then unclipped the hollow barrel from the end of the bit. He knelt in front of a blue tarp, lined the barrel up with a plastic tray, and pushed a rod through the opposite end. A perfect solid, cylindrical core of ancient soil emerged—dark, rich permafrost at least a foot long. On that late morning in early July, Haverdink was among a team of Northern Arizona University students and staff gathering samples and data from a research plot, divided by surveyor’s tape unspooled atop tussocks, alders, and fireweed. In late June of 2013, a lightning strike sparked a fire in this stretch of mostly treeless tundra, in the northern backcountry of the park. Whipped by the Chinooks, intense downslope winds created as moist ocean air surmounts and then descends the Alaska Range, the fire ripped across nearly 35,000 acres. It burned through much of the vegetation and deep into the topsoil, stripping away the insulating layer that preserves the frozen permafrost, a massive storehouse of carbon that underlies 85% of Alaska. During their first survey, in 2016, the researchers found that in places where the fire burned hottest and deepest, the thawed soil above the permafrost (known as the active layer) extended between a foot and nearly two feet deeper than in plots outside the burn scar—an indication of deep thawing. Crucially, the fire also crossed two sets of survey grids that the National Park Service established in 2001, including the one where the researchers stood that morning. That pre-fire snapshot offered the Northern Arizona group a rare opportunity to study how the blaze has altered the vegetation, carbon, and permafrost across time, says Xanthe Walker, one of the principal investigators on the project, who was watching over Haverdink’s work that morning. These direct observations from high-latitude fires are helping scientists to better understand the impacts of wildfire and permafrost loss on climate change, she says. “There’s a lot of uncertainty,” Walker says. “These are huge biomes with massive fires that no one’s ever been to or ever looked at.” They’re among a growing group of researchers striving to untangle what happens in the aftermath of fires in the Arctic-boreal region as they occur more often, burn hotter than before, and expose the landscape and frozen permafrost to a warming climate. The work is providing critical clues into the complex mechanisms driving cascading climate feedback effects: As warming temperatures fuel more frequent and severe fires, more permafrost thaws, releasing greenhouse-gas emissions that threaten to fuel more warming, fires, and permafrost loss. Understanding just how powerful these effects are, how they compound, and to what degree other transformations of the landscape may mitigate them is essential to gain a clearer picture of our climate future. Notably, past modeling suggested that the carbon dioxide uptake from expanding trees and plants may largely offset the release of carbon from warming soils and melting permafrost, at least over long periods and under moderate levels of warming. But the improving observations and analyses have revealed worrisome signs about the impacts of rising Arctic-boreal emissions in the immediate decades ahead, as the world struggles to keep global warming within relatively safe boundaries. Permafrost makes up 15% of the land surface of the Northern Hemisphere and holds about 1.5 trillion tons of carbon, roughly twice the amount in the atmosphere. Most of it formed during the last ice age, but it’s been thawing at an accelerating rate during the last half-century, amid rising temperatures and worsening fires. As it melts, microbes begin to break down that organic matter, releasing it into the air in the form of carbon dioxide and methane. Both are powerful greenhouse gases that fuel climate change. The impacts of permafrost loss, however, aren’t as straightforward as they may initially seem. Notably, there has been a long-running scientific debate over what would have the strongest effect on the exchange of greenhouse gases over the next few centuries: the loss of carbon in soil and permafrost or the carbon uptake from so-called “Arctic greening,” which describes the spread of carbon-absorbing plants across the landscape as temperatures rise. Thawing permafrost itself can contribute to the latter phenomenon as the unfrozen soil layer deepens and makes more nitrogen, phosphorus, and other nutrients accessible to plants. Carbon-sucking trees and shrubs with deeper root systems can take hold and thrive in places where they wouldn’t have been able to in the past. But as more Arctic-boreal fires ignite and more permafrost melts, researchers have been able to closely study what exactly happens in the aftermath, using on-site surveys like those conducted by the Northern Arizona University team, satellite observations, lidar flights, a widening network of greenhouse-gas-measuring towers, and other tools. The findings have alarmed many researchers. In particular, permafrost seems particularly prone to abrupt thawing rather than the gradual version that major climate models have simulated. A 2020 paper found that this could release far more carbon than previously understood, doubling the emissions from thawing permafrost and offsetting the carbon uptake from future plant growth. In addition, even if carbon levels eventually net out through Arctic greening, the emissions from high-latitude fires and permafrost loss appear to be accelerating warming right now. Susan Natali, an Arctic climate scientist at the Woodwell Climate Research Center, and her colleagues have published several papers in recent years concluding that the combined emissions from these feedback effects will push the planet past 2 °C faster than currently predicted. She and other researchers  also found that about 40% of the Arctic-boreal zone has already flipped from a carbon sink into a carbon source, when the emissions from permafrost melting, fires and other forces are taken into account. “The more we learn about processes like abrupt thaw, deeper-burning fires, and cold-season emissions, and the more we see disturbances increasing, the more likely it is that the sink will weaken and source areas will expand,” Natali said in an email. But there’s still considerable uncertainty about the differences between the ways fires and thawing permafrost might alter landscapes, under different circumstances in different biomes. “This is just a challenge of the science, because it's really hard to measure such a huge area, which is not one system,” she says. “It’s many, many, many, many different types of ecosystems.” The Northern Arizona University researchers have been at the forefront of efforts to study the aftermath of intensifying fires across different parts of Alaska. Over the last two decades, Michelle Mack, a professor of ecosystem ecology who was previously Walker’s postdoctoral advisor, has overseen repeat survey work on fire sites across forests and tundras, from the relatively warm southern interior to the frigid North Slope. Her husband and frequent collaborator, Ted Schuur, is also a professor at the university and oversees several permafrost monitoring and experimental research sites at the Eight Mile Lake study area, just north of Denali National Park.If there’s a single takeaway from the body of science that the Northern Arizona scientists have produced, it’s this: It’s complicated. The local climate and ecosystem, the severity of the fires that have occurred, the type of permafrost, and numerous other factors can substantially shift the uptake or loss of carbon, the amount of permafrost that’s thawed, the extent to which it may recover, and the future flammability of the landscape. In one early study, the researchers and their collaborators surveyed dozens of stands dominated by black spruce trees in the aftermath of a series of large boreal fires in 2004, between the Brooks Range and the Alaska Range. One of the notable findings: Severe burns that left little behind of the decaying organic matter in the topsoil induced a shift toward faster-growing and less flammable deciduous trees. The sites where deciduous trees dominated stored about five times as much soil carbon as stands that returned to black spruce, Mack, Walker, and their coauthors noted in a 2021 Science paper. That means the subsequent uptake of carbon on those stands “more than compensated” for the amount released in the initial fire, Walker says. But the 2007 Anaktuvuk River tundra fire, which remains the largest fire on record in Alaska’s North Slope, offers a starkly different case study. In the initial analysis there, Mack, Schuur, and their coauthors found that the fire released more than 2 million tons of carbon to the atmosphere, basically negating the amount that tundra ecosystems around the world removed from the air that year. Subsequent studies found that more than a third of the scorched landscape sank, slid, and crumpled as the ice wedges below collapsed, the telltale signs of abrupt permafrost melting. Nearly two decades later, much of that thawed, decaying organic matter continues to release carbon dioxide and methane in the most severely burned patches of the fire scar, says Adrian Rocha, an associate professor at the University of Notre Dame. It will take decades to centuries to recover all the carbon lost there, if ever happens at all, according to Rocha, who continues to monitor the site using Eddy covariance flux towers, which measure the exchange of greenhouse gases between Earth and atmosphere. Mack says these on-the-ground measurements are critical to understanding what sorts of ecosystems recover, which shift into new states, whether they release a lot of carbon or a little, and when and where plant growth can offset that.That said, the stakes of thawing permafrost are clear. “If we step back as ecosystem ecologists, we know that there’s a lot more carbon stored below ground in permafrost than there is above ground in vegetation,” she says. “If permafrost carbon is released, it could swamp all of these other interesting details.” The Northern Arizona University research team shipped a pallet of soil samples back from Denali. The material included plugs of moss and decaying plant matter known as “brownies,” as well as permafrost cores and samples of the unfrozen soil layer. In the months since, they’ve cut, dried, weighed, combusted and analyzed various samples and inputted their field and lab findings into databases. Walker says they’re exploring a variety of research questions, including how much that top organic layer has recovered since the initial post-fire survey and how much carbon has been lost and recovered since the fire. The analysis is still underway, but in the most severely burned sites, it appears that the thawed layer of soil above the permafrost remain deeper some 13 years. “Thaw still reaches further into the soil each summer than it probably did before the fire, which exposes more soil to decomposition,” Walker explained in an email. They’re now hoping to use lab analyses to determine how much of that carbon is being lost. On my last afternoon in Healy, Alaska, I followed Schuur along a narrow walkway of wooden boards suspended above a tundra field just north of Denali, hoping to get a glimpse into our climate future. In 2008, he and his research team devised a way to subject permafrost to the temperature levels expected two or three decades from now by setting up snow fences around dozens of designated research plots. That allowed extra snow to build up—which, counterintuitively, acts like a blanket, adding thermal insulation during the winter that kept the test plots warmer than the controls. Over the course of the 13-year experiment, the soil carbon levels in those 10-by-10-meter plots declined by 23%, Schuur and his coauthors found. The land collapsed in many of the sites, forming craters several feet deep. As I stepped heel-to-toe along the planks into the middle of one plot, I descended about four feet, experiencing subsidence in three dimensions. Looking up at the edge from the walkway, I felt as if I were diaphragm deep in an empty pool. Schuur, though, had another thing to show me. As we crested a ridge between plots, he pointed down to a shallower—though no less obvious—pool on the other side. It was a control site that had melted and collapsed on its own, without any assistance from simulated warming or fires. “Every plot is just going off in its own trajectory,” he said. “This stuff is happening because the whole place is warming … and all these changes just go faster if you first burn the surface.” Schuur expects that the permafrost underlying the whole field will thaw deeply by the end of 2040. “There won’t be any surface permafrost based on our current warming,” he says. Unless the world radically cuts emissions and gets fires under control, this same phenomenon will creep up the state, decade by decade, until even the permafrost under Alaska’s North Slope sinks to similar depths and gives up the carbon it has held for millennia. Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. A visit to Samsung’s massive new semiconductor hub
2026-10-20

A visit to Samsung’s massive new semiconductor hub

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Generative AI models run on powerful chips known as GPUs, which depend on stacks of specialized memory chips packaged with the processor. To meet growing demand for memory, Samsung is building a massive new semiconductor manufacturing complex in Pyeongtaek, South Korea. Three fabs already operate around the clock, and a fourth is coming online. Construction of a fifth was halted during a drop in demand for memory chips in 2024 but restarted when the AI boom sent demand soaring. It’s just before noon on a Monday, and construction workers are pouring out of Samsung’s fabs in Pyeongtaek, a farming city south of Seoul where rice paddies abut a sprawling chipmaking complex. Thousands of workers, dressed head to toe in black with sky-blue vests, take off helmets bearing their names and fab numbers. They walk to lunch, sweaty and flushed, in chunky sneakers capped with yellow rubber toe guards. Some hop on scooters or motorcycles to get around the campus, which covers an area about the size of New York City’s Central Park. They’ll dine at buffets, diners, and beef stew joints, walking past banners advertising short-term leases for studio apartments. Some stop by real estate offices to inquire, stepping up to the same counters where Samsung’s salaried engineers buy new homes. In front of the fourth fab, I meet Choi Hye-rin, a 35-year-old safety officer, as she wipes her forehead with a handkerchief. At dawn, she boarded one of the shuttle buses that ferry workers in from Songtan, an older, scrappier district. She oversees the safety of a crew responsible for building cable ducts. She writes reports, inspects equipment, and checks the chemicals the workers handle. She’s one of hundreds of women working on site, many employed as spotters who walk ahead of heavy equipment. Safety is a big deal here. After lunch, construction picks right back up. Excavators move past rust-red steel structures rising high under tower cranes. Banners that hang from the highest girders read, “Even if the work runs late, don’t rush.” A giant LED scoreboard announces the winners of a contest for writing a poem about safety. Helmets stickered with a pledge, “I will not work if it’s not safe,” perch atop lockers. The finished fabs glisten from across the street. Along with the cautionary messages, there’s a sense of urgency in the air. Samsung predicts that demand for memory chips will continue to surge through 2028. And for years the company watched its domestic rival SK Hynix pull ahead in the global market for the high-bandwidth models that power Nvidia’s AI accelerators. (Meanwhile, the Taiwanese chipmaker TSMC has dominated the foundry business, manufacturing chips that other companies design.) The Pyeongtaek build-out is Samsung’s attempt to reclaim its lead on memory chips. Although the heat can be brutal and the work zones are crowded, Hye-rin has no plans to leave. “The rate here is higher than other construction sites, and the work is relatively easy,” she says. She gets paid 140,000 won ($97) a day and expects the boom to keep her busy for some time: “You just keep coming back.” Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. In a new interview, the billionaire philanthropist sounds an alarm on the urgency of getting our AI policies in order. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. MIT Technology Review
These startups hope “blended” food will cut our appetite for red meat
2026-10-20

These startups hope “blended” food will cut our appetite for red meat

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
In the fall of 2025, Jeff Dodge set up a six-foot folding table in the cafeteria of Cane Bay High School in Summerville, South Carolina, to conduct a taste test. When the first rush of about 150 hungry students hit, he was waiting with slow cookers filled with steaming marinara sauce and meatballs. Kids love a taste test, but they’re also discerning, and Dodge wasn’t sure what they would make of his product, a blend featuring both meat and mushrooms. As students swarmed the table and tapped out their opinions on an iPad or scanned a QR code to use their phones, Dodge asked those who lingered to guess what kind of vegetable might have been mixed into the meat. Few of them could—the meatballs had tasted like moist, flavorful beef. The school enrolls a few thousand students, and Dodge estimates that the majority of them came by. In the end, the survey showed that 92% of those who tried the meatballs wanted them on the menu; they easily cleared the bar to be added for the 2026–2027 school year. Comments described their flavor as “bussin’.” Through his consulting company, K–12 Food Solutions, Dodge was testing a new product called 50/Cut from the company Joyn. The proprietary blend of seemingly simple ingredients—available as meatballs, burgers, and ground meat—could help solve one of the most intractable challenges of the climate crisis, at least on the individual level: getting people to eat less meat, particularly beef. Livestock production accounts for somewhere between 10% and 20% of all greenhouse-gas emissions related to human activity, and nearly two-thirds of that comes from ruminants like cows raised for meat and dairy products. Beef herds create four times the emissions of dairy herds per kilo of food produced. And for US households, food makes up as much as 30% of emissions. Unlike other polluting sectors, such as electricity and transportation, cattle farming has little potential for a big fix. Ruminants produce about 30% of the methane associated with human activity, and this gas has 28 times more global-­warming potential than carbon dioxide. What’s more, it would be virtually impossible to raise them in a perfectly climate-­neutral manner. “We don’t have a technical solution to change the stomach of a ruminant,” says Fredrik Hedenus, a professor in sustainability and systems analysis at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden. Technologies to dampen the impact of their burps have been in the works for a while but have yet to scale. They also fix only part of the problem with red meat: Cows need a lot of land for pasture and for feed, which has made livestock a top driver of deforestation in the Amazon and elsewhere around the world. Even if the world stopped burning all fossil fuels tomorrow, emissions from agriculture would push the planet past 2 °C over preindustrial temperatures by 2070, Hedenus says. According to his research, more efficient livestock farming methods would still leave agriculture eating up over half of humanity’s carbon budget by that time. Only with the addition of dietary changes does his model show a winning scenario. He’s far from alone in his conclusions: A 2024 survey of more than 200 climate scientists, conducted at Harvard, found that the vast majority believe reducing emissions from livestock is important to meeting climate goals. People have to eat less beef. “Essentially, you need a culture shift,” Hedenus says. Yet the culture has proved intractable. Not only do people like the taste of meat, but across most of the world, meat-eating tends to be associated with status. As societies have grown wealthier, it has increased. A United Nations report showed that global meat production quadrupled between 1961 and 2022, while vegetarianism has held steady at about 4% of the population in the US since 1999. What could move the needle is a carbon-light and delicious meat replacement. But from a food-tech standpoint, all we have to show is a menu of mostly failed solutions. Lab-grown meat has generated endless headlines since the 2000s but has yet to become commercially available (and affordable) at scale. And while breakthroughs in taste and texture inched plant-based meats from companies like Impossible Foods and Beyond Meat toward mass adoption, the hype—and the appetite—has somewhat fizzled. So instead of asking people to change their preferences, startups like Joyn are trying a different tack. They’re creating what are broadly referred to as blended meat products, which combine plant proteins with the real deal. The logic: The best meat replacement is … better meat. Paul Shapiro, cofounder of the startup BMC Ingredients, which markets a plant protein related to mushrooms, says the math is easy to understand: If we imagine that Impossible Whoppers at Burger King account for about 2% of sales, that’s only a 2% reduction in meat. But if a conventional Whopper were 20% plants, that would be a 20% cut right off the bat. Those aren’t quite the numbers climate models are calling for, but some estimates say they could lead to outsized gains relative to the status quo: A 2022 study in Nature found that replacing 20% of beef globally could halve the annual rate of deforestation. And unlike the other solutions tried thus far, this particular Hail Mary is one a lot of omnivores seem open to giving a try. The idea of blending meat and vegetables together is, of course, nothing new. Food cultures from around the world boast an array of dishes that combine small amounts of meat with plants. Today, home cooks still add veggies and legumes to dishes to stretch tight grocery budgets or to sneak extra greenery into their children’s supper. But in the mid to late 2010s, however, the focus was largely on companies that appeared to have cracked the code for ditching meat entirely. When Impossible debuted its namesake burger in 2016, it impressed with an ingredient called heme, an iron-rich molecule in the protein soy leghemoglobin that allowed all-plant burgers to “bleed” like beef. Impossible and its biggest competitor, Beyond, swaggered through the media and the markets. In 2019, Impossible’s founder told the New Yorker he intended to wipe out all animal agriculture by 2035. Beyond’s 2019 IPO was the most successful since the 2008 financial crisis. A flood of imitators followed. But within a few years, tastes shifted. In 2023, Impossible employees’ stock values tumbled 89% from a 2021 high. That same year, Beyond laid off 20% of its staff, and a bruising Businessweek feature labeled the entire sector a fad. Sales have been slipping for years, with a 10% year-over-year drop in 2025, according to the Good Food Institute, a think tank focused on alternative proteins. Any quest to wipe out animal agriculture could now seem farcical. In 2025, Americans downed $45 billion worth of beef. Several factors were likely at play. Megan Krogius, a spokesperson for Impossible Foods, says the dip is a “correction not a crisis” after “an outsized pop in demand” between 2020 and 2022. She also says that a flurry of plant-based products muddied the category’s reputation. Indeed, some disruptors (and their imitators) have struggled to compete on the sensory level. Nectar, an organization that conducts research on meat alternatives, has found that taste and texture are among the major factors that keep people from adopting plant-based meats. “Our consumers identify products as having bad aftertaste and off flavors five to six times more than in animal counterparts,” says Caroline Cotto, the group’s director. At the same time, discourse around processed foods tarnished plant-based meat’s halo. In 2019, the Center for Consumer Freedom, a since-dissolved lobbying group backed by the meat industry, launched an ad campaign against plant-based alternatives, demonizing them as “ultra-­processed imitations.” While Impossible’s Krogius warns against conflating processed food with junk food (she points, for example, to a study in The Lancet finding mock meat to be nutritionally preferable to red meat), some of the bad press stuck. Now, the term “ultra-­processed” has become something of a political cudgel—one wielded by the meat-loving MAHA movement and the US secretary of health and human services, Robert F. Kennedy Jr. In 2025, he flipped the food pyramid and stacked a steak on top. Any quest to wipe out animal agriculture could now seem farcical. In 2025, Americans downed $45 billion worth of beef, and consumption rose by 4.3% over the previous year, according to an industry report. Rising prices could be slowing this down a bit, but cost can dampen only so much demand. Blended meat, which has meat’s own juiciness and texture, rose alongside the plant-based superstars, but it did not get New Yorker profiles or headlining IPOs. At the height of mock-meat hype, companies like Tyson and Perdue launched blended products, and firms working on lab-grown meat explored cutting their products with plants. The big advantage of blended meat is that large numbers of consumers—what some call “flexitarians”—are curious about it, and when they try it, they like it. A blind taste test co-funded by the USDA in 2022 showed that diners preferred blended burgers to fully plant-based ones. And a report from the major food processor ADM found that large proportions of every generation of Americans were interested in trying blended meats, including 75% of millennials. “This is a solution that we can do today, that consumers will accept today,” says Tim Dale, a director at Food Systems Innovations, the nonprofit group that oversees Nectar. At this point, even Impossible has acknowledged the potential. In a wide-ranging interview with the Wall Street Journal in 2025, the company’s former CEO Peter McGuinness admitted the brand’s struggle to win over consumers, and suggested a hybrid burger could bridge the divide. Plenty of newcomers are entering the fray. “Shiitake-infused beef burgers” from Australia-based Fable Foods are in restaurants and moving “incredibly well” at the high-end grocery store Central Market in Austin, Texas, says CEO Michael Fox. The Both Burger from Los Angeles–based 50/50 Foods is available in a smattering of California stores and eateries. And Spare Food in New York signed a deal with Premier, one of the nation’s biggest spenders in the health-care sector, for its blend of surplus produce and ground beef. One major insight for all these upstarts is that consumers react best when marketing focuses on pleasure, not environmental virtues or health benefits. Though many companies actually create a burger or meatball with a better nutritional profile (fiber!) and fewer of meat’s downsides, like saturated fat, that doesn’t get people to buy it. They like that it tastes good. Beef and mushrooms, for example, contain complementary flavor compounds, including the glutamates that yield deep umami, which is why they’re so often paired in dishes like beef Wellington and stroganoff. Food-production companies are a big target. BMC Ingredients, which collaborated with Perdue on a line of blended nuggets, markets a plant-based protein that can be mixed with meat to up its nutrition and flavor. BMC grows mycelium—the root-like structures of fungi. It underpins popular meat alternatives like Quorn and also features alongside mushrooms in Joyn’s 50/Cut. Shapiro likens his factory to a brewery, but instead of yeast fermenting barley, a fungus called Neurospora crassa digests starch to produce a protein with a dense network of fibers that makes for a meat-like chew. “We grow four hours before we get a harvest,” Shapiro says. “It’s among the most efficient ways to produce protein on Earth.” Joyn also sees its path forward outside the grocery store. Food eaten away from home accounts for more than half of what US consumers spend on grub, according to the USDA. Padraic Doherty, who joined Joyn from the meat distributor D’Artagnan and holds the title of chief growth officer, says its main areas of focus are K–12 and college cafeterias and other major food service providers, like stadiums. These organizations, he says, often have sustainability goals and also are pressured to find both nutritious and affordable options. The 50/Cut Joyn sells to cafeterias is between 5% and 15% cheaper than beef. But can cafeteria mushroom burgers prompt a larger cultural shift? The folks touting the idea of going halfway see it as a bridge. BMC’s Shapiro thinks the movement toward meat-light eating reflects the reality of how these changes happen. Nectar’s Cotto compares blended meat to a hybrid car. “Moving people toward blended products [is] a way to drive demand for some of the fully plant-based ingredients that don’t scale right now to bring the price down,” she says. “So it’s both a short- and a long-term approach.” Not everyone is so sure market forces will be enough, though. Nil Zacharias, a longtime entrepreneur in food sustainability who offers plant-based options to New York City delis through his company Plantega, isn’t sold on the notion. “Too many people in the space believe that as long as we create meat that is not from an animal but tastes like meat and costs the same, people will eat it,” he says. “I think the plant-based backlash to ultraprocessed foods shows you that’s not necessarily how things are going to go.” He believes blended meat’s main impact will be on institutional dining, in places like schools and health-care facilities. “People in this space believe that as long as we create meat that is not from an animal but tastes like meat, people will eat it.” Meat hybrids could, however, taste just familiar enough to open consumers’ minds to alternatives. Some research has found that thinking about mock meats prompts people to reconsider the whole system. In the 2010s, Clemens Driessen, a cultural geographer who teaches at Wageningen University & Research in the Netherlands, worked on a project that asked people in pubs, supermarkets, and other public spaces what they thought of lab-grown meat. “There’s a first response, which is ‘Oh, that sounds horrible,’” Driessen says. Then people tended to reflect that the meat they consume is also heavily industrialized. “A whole train of thought emerges out of proposing something very new,” he says. If the category succeeds, climate advocates will need to make peace with imperfection. A life-cycle analy­sis Joyn commissioned on the 50/Cut burger confirms that the product has half the greenhouse-gas footprint of a beef patty, which—if you go by company claims—means it has about five times the impact of a burger from Impossible or Beyond. Hedenus, the climate scientist, says that even if blended products don’t reduce impact as much as plant-based ones, he’s all for trying any solution that works. “It is very difficult to change how we eat, so I think most good suggestions should be explored,” he says. Meanwhile, the products are finding their way to more plates. They’re in cafeterias, high-end grocery stores, and small burger chains. They’re in the catalogues of major distributors like Sysco. The Both Burger has even been at Disneyland. The 50/Cut meatball is on the menu this school year in seven states. At Cane Bay High it’ll take the form of a sub. The school is buying 200 cases at a time, with 150 meatballs in a case. Jason Ciaccia, the systems specialist for child nutrition in the school district, says he won’t be using the word “mushroom” to describe it. “If they hear ‘mushroom,’ they’re going to skip over it,” he says. He is, however, considering his branding. He’s asked AI to help him come up with something fun. He thinks “Marvelous Meatball Sub” has a nice ring. Meghan McCarron is a food and culture journalist based in Los Angeles. Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. Could new fertilizers help combat high prices? Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more.
