MIT Technology Review
Making the internet better for AI agents might make it worse for everyone else
2026-10-19

Making the internet better for AI agents might make it worse for everyone else

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
This story originally appeared in The Algorithm, our weekly newsletter on AI. To get stories like this in your inbox first, sign up here. Until recently, you could divide internet users into just two categories: bots and people. Websites have long allowed bots to perform automated tasks, like capturing the content of web pages, but rules govern what they can access and how they behave. The web, after all, is designed primarily for people, even if we occasionally have to solve puzzles and captchas to prove we are not bots. But this is being entirely upended by the release of personal AI agents, which can do things for you online, like booking flights or hotel rooms. They have recently been unleashed at scale by the launch of apps like OpenAI’s Dots and Meta’s Muse. Muse was downloaded 5 million times in its first month, and agentic web traffic tripled from August to September. The mingling of these agents with human visitors—on an internet designed largely to prioritize people—has gone poorly, with many websites blocking AI agents that consumers have sent to negotiate prices or plan vacations. Still, if the demand for agents keeps going up, the internet will be increasingly redesigned for them instead of us. Such changes are coming because agents really do mark a third category of web visitor. They’re not humans, of course, and they use the internet at machine speed. But they’re not quite bots, either, because they’re acting on behalf of individual people to do tasks that can be perfectly legitimate and useful. They just work faster than a person would. Since agents don’t fit into the systems we have for differentiating bots from people, some platforms (especially the more security-conscious ones) have opted to just ban them entirely. TechCrunch recently reported people having trouble deploying their agents to websites ranging from Pizza Hut to Yelp, which doesn’t allow AI to book restaurant reservations. Amazon has blocked Muse’s agents and is waging a court battle against the AI startup Perplexity to block its agents too. Though a judge recently ruled in favor of Perplexity, Amazon has filed an appeal in which it argues that AI agents threaten its advertising business by making it harder to distinguish between ads seen by humans and those served to bots. These blocks are annoying for early adopters, but Ethan Mollick, an associate professor at the Wharton School of the University of Pennsylvania, predicts it will soon affect the whole web. Once it becomes common knowledge that AI agents can be sent to request refunds or negotiate prices, for example, agents will overwhelm customer service departments to the point where they become unusable by real people. “Humans will eventually get very annoyed when their agents aren’t allowed to do this on their behalf,” he recently wrote on LinkedIn. If demand for personal agents grows, platforms will find ways to let them in. That’s especially true because the vast majority of agentic web traffic is to e-commerce sites, meaning that platforms have money to be made if they allow the legions of new AI agents to shop on behalf of real people. Indeed, the e-commerce giant Shopify is already welcoming the agents by giving them their own checkout lanes that bypass pages designed for humans. The pressing question is what will happen to the internet for the rest of us in the process. We’ve grown used to a web where, even as it’s increasingly designed to exploit our attention, humans have at least remained the intended users. But that internet might yield to one optimized instead for the agents that will eventually outnumber us, making it even more miserable for the humans who are left. How might this go? Perhaps websites become increasingly neglected as destinations for real people. Humans who want to browse and compare products themselves encounter worse interfaces. Verification systems are imperfect; imagine constantly being asked to verify that you’re a human, not an agent (and hey, maybe you’ll need to get your iris scanned to do so). That sweater you want doesn’t have one price listed for everyone but instead displays different prices to different people’s agents. You can never tell when your agent’s recommendation is influenced by advertisements or platform commissions. That’s a far cry from the future promised by Google, Anthropic, and OpenAI, in which AI agents will give consumers greater power and bargaining leverage. But that promise is not outlandish. Agents are capable of genuinely helping people by fighting bogus medical bills or researching more affordable car insurance, for example. Whether they can actually be used for these purposes depends on what rules are set for how agents interact with websites and what exactly they’re allowed to do. These rules are still being written. Some of them will benefit all of us—neither consumers nor businesses want credit card information to be waved around by agents wherever they roam the web, for example, and rules to keep agents more secure are a good thing. It’s the more contested ones, like whether businesses can block agents from their websites or prevent them from negotiating better deals for consumers, that will determine whether the web becomes a highway for agents working for us or against us. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. In a new interview, the billionaire philanthropist sounds an alarm on the urgency of getting our AI policies in order. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. The Next Web
MIT’s AI agent society result holds up on consumer hardware
2026-10-20

MIT’s AI agent society result holds up on consumer hardware

The Next Web | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
In August, researchers at MIT dropped a society of AI agents into a shared world with no instructions. They showed it could invent, build and maintain technology better than the same number of agents working alone. The study ran on a hosted frontier model. A research team working with Apex Fusion has now reproduced the central experiment on four RTX 3090 gaming GPUs. Apex Fusion is a Swiss foundation building infrastructure for AI agents. It used MIT’s code and commands unchanged. The run took about 26 hours and 12,800 model calls. The paper, posted on Apex Fusion’s GitHub, and the full trace data are public. SwarmWorld is by Pal, Wang and Buehler at MIT’s Laboratory for Atomistic and Molecular Mechanics. It placed 50 or more language-model agents in a shared simulated world. There were no assigned roles and no recipes. The agents explored, gathered materials, ran tests, built persistent technologies and wrote programs that kept running after the agents were gone. The societies built broader and more resilient portfolios of technology than the best of an equal number of solo agents. They also split into builders and explorers on their own. Markus Buehler has framed the work as a warning for AI safety. If agents can coordinate through changes to a shared environment, monitoring their messages to each other isn’t enough. The MIT team released its code, prompts and complete traces, which is what made an outside replication possible. The replication, led by Časlav Nedeljković of HAL8, set out to test whether SwarmWorld’s result depended on a frontier model. The team swapped in Qwen3.8-27B, an open-weight model compressed to 4-bit precision. The team ran it on four consumer RTX 3090s. Nine of the 12 paired effects matched MIT’s in direction. That included every portfolio-resilience gain, two of which came out larger in the replication. The remaining three fell within noise. Self-organization also reproduced. Societies with shared culture clustered around their technologies, as in the original. MIT’s own analysis tools drew the same picture from the new traces. The most striking result was the gap between solo and group performance. Working alone, the compressed Qwen model reached 6 of 16 research milestones. That was slightly behind the 7 to 10 MIT’s frontier model managed. In a society, it reached all 16, where MIT’s reached 14. “MIT released everything a second lab needs to check its work, and that made this possible. We changed the model, the precision and the hardware, and the central result still held. The society gap widened with a weaker model, which suggests the collective effect is robust.” Časlav Nedeljković, HAL8 That conclusion comes from a single run at one population size. The larger populations in MIT’s study haven’t been rerun yet. The replication turned up a detail that matters to anyone running agents on their own infrastructure. The KV cache is the working memory a model uses during inference. Its precision changed results more than the gap between a 27B and a 235B model. Teams often compress the KV cache to fit longer contexts or more concurrent requests onto limited GPU memory. The finding suggests agent benchmarks run on self-hosted inference can reflect serving configuration as much as the choice of model. The team also found that MIT’s released traces reproduce every observable event and metric, with one exception: a single internal check. Both findings went back to the MIT authors with evidence, as a GitHub issue and a pull request. Apex Fusion’s interest in the study goes beyond reproducibility. Groups of agents from different organizations are starting to work together. Someone has to answer which agent did the work, what it was worth, and whether the record can be checked later. Apex Fusion’s answer is Vector, which it describes as a settlement layer for AI agents. Agents carry an identity, stake behind their claims, exchange bonded work and settle with a signed receipt on-chain. It is MCP-native and runs on Prime, the Apex Fusion base chain. The replication itself ran directly on the team’s own GPUs. The team also registered its model server as a supplier on the Vector marketplace, an OpenAI-compatible gateway that meters each request and settles usage on-chain in AP3X, the network’s utility token. A 32-call pilot through Vector behaved the same as the direct run. “As AI agents from different organizations start working together, the most valuable thing in the system is a trustworthy record of who did what. In the pilot, calls ran through Vector, each one metered, settled and backed by a receipt anyone can verify.” Christopher Greenwood, CEO, Apex Fusion Foundation Whether multi-organization agent networks will need a blockchain for that record, rather than conventional logging and billing, is still an open question. The replication itself doesn’t settle it. The foundation is also in the process of joining the Agentic AI Foundation, the Linux Foundation body that hosts the Model Context Protocol and Agent2Agent standards. A full-scale run through Vector, for a side-by-side comparison with the direct run, is underway, alongside the next population size in MIT’s study. Apex Fusion says it is opening Vector to frontier labs, research institutions and universities. Activity on the network can be followed on a live dashboard. The broader point may be the one Buehler raised in August. If agent societies like these can run on a few consumer GPUs, watching how agents coordinate is no longer something only frontier labs need to worry about. OpenAI pulls back from China chip controls, Politico reports

OpenAI pulls back from China chip controls, Politico reports

The Next Web | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
OpenAI has stopped pushing for strict US limits on selling advanced chips to China, according to Politico. For much of last year the company openly called for such limits. Capitol Hill aides and lobbyists with direct knowledge of its position now say it has gone quiet. TNW has not independently verified the report. The shift is a win for Nvidia, which is fighting the proposals. It also puts OpenAI closer to President Donald Trump, who has built close ties with the chipmaker and its chief executive, Jensen Huang. OpenAI’s officials “were with us on this and now their position is a bit more fuzzy,” one congressional aide told Politico. “Silence is doing Nvidia’s work for it.” The timing matters. China hawks in Congress want to attach new chip restrictions to the annual defense spending bill. They plan to push after the midterm election, in what is known as the lame-duck session. They were counting on OpenAI to back the measures, Politico reported. Its pullback leaves them without one of their most prominent industry allies. One lobbyist put OpenAI’s reasoning simply. “They got the message,” the lobbyist told Politico. “There is no appetite to challenge the president at this moment.” Anthropic has taken the opposite path. It backs several of the chip bills, and quit a major tech trade group in September over the group’s push to soften them. OpenAI, more than its rival, now appears to be tracking the policy goals Trump wants. OpenAI depends on Nvidia’s chips, and the two have grown closer. Nvidia finalised a $30bn equity investment in OpenAI in February. Under a separate deal in August, Nvidia agreed to be the sole chip supplier at an Ohio data centre OpenAI will lease, and to guarantee up to $105bn for its first phase. The company’s public line has softened over time. In March 2025, OpenAI told the administration it supported keeping China under “strict export controls”. A federal framework it proposed in June 2026 had a single line calling to “strengthen export controls”, without naming China. In a letter last October, it asked the White House to loosen some export rules for AI safety testing. In a statement, OpenAI did not address its meetings with lawmakers or officials. “We want the US to continue to have the most powerful AI models, maintain and widen its compute advantage, and lead in setting global standards,” an OpenAI spokesperson said. “US labs having a substantial lead in advanced chips is key to achieving those goals, and effective export control policies are one part of that broader strategy.” The hawks say chip limits answer the public’s rising fears about AI security. They argue the US is a more responsible steward of the technology than Beijing. A steady run of headlines about breaches and rogue AI agents has sharpened their case. Not everyone is convinced the retreat is wise. Nathan Calvin, general counsel at the advocacy group Encode, linked the two debates. Its mind-boggling to me that so many people are hesitant to support AI safety rules on the grounds that it might help China, but some of those same folks want to sell far more advanced AI chips to China. Sad to see OpenAI retreat on this issue (to keep NVIDIA happy?) Nathan Calvin (@_NathanCalvin) October 20, 2026 The White House wants US firms to sell more high-tech chips abroad. Trump has reversed limits on some of Nvidia’s powerful chips to China. In return, the chipmaker agreed to give the US government a share of the revenue. A White House official disputed that the president has eased the rules, and said the administration runs “the most rigorous export control regime in modern history”. The pressure is unlikely to ease. The move lands a day after the US ordered federal agencies to remove DeepSeek products, and days after China proposed its own export controls on AI models and chips. Chinese firms, meanwhile, are still ordering billions in Nvidia chips that US rules still allow. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Terra Security’s AI Native Cybersecurity podcast opens with Amazon’s CISO
2026-10-20

Terra Security’s AI Native Cybersecurity podcast opens with Amazon’s CISO

The Next Web | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
A cyberattack that once might have taken weeks or months to unfold can now go from initial access to a complete system takeover in minutes. That was the lesson CJ Moses, CISO of Amazon, shared with Shahar Peled. Peled is CEO and co-founder of Terra Security, and they spoke during the opening episode of AI Native Cybersecurity. It is a new podcast examining how artificial intelligence is changing the balance between attackers and defenders. The conversation offers a look at the operational challenges facing security teams as AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, reconnaissance, and exploitation. Through the podcast, Terra Security aims to bring security leaders together. They discuss practical lessons from deploying AI, separating hype from reality, and preparing for the next phase of cyber defense. One of the most striking examples Moses shared came from Amazon’s MadPot honeypot infrastructure, which monitors adversary behavior. In one observed incident, an attacker moved from having no access to taking over the compromised system and expanding through its available access. It took 12 minutes and 42 seconds. “In the past, this would have been a campaign by an adversary. Weeks, sometimes months, would go on forever. This was 12 minutes to just shy of 13 minutes.” CJ Moses, CISO, Amazon The example illustrates a fundamental change in the assumptions underlying vulnerability management. Security teams have historically operated with patching windows measured in days or months. But attackers can now move through reconnaissance, exploitation, and lateral movement in a fraction of an hour. When they do, the time available to identify and contain a threat becomes much shorter. For Moses, the central issue is velocity. AI gives adversaries the ability to do more within a shorter period. That makes speed of response as important as the number of vulnerabilities discovered. Security programs have traditionally relied on layers of defensive controls to prevent intrusions or slow attackers down. Moses argued that this strategy still has value. But AI-enabled adversaries can increasingly connect individual weaknesses into longer attack paths. That changes the role of offensive security. Rather than relying exclusively on defensive controls, organizations need to test their environments continuously. They need to determine which vulnerabilities can actually be exploited. Referring to security checks during software development, Moses warned against slowing down. “The reality is if you slow down, you’re actually playing into the hands of the thing we talked about in the beginning.” CJ Moses At Amazon, the approach includes integrating security scans into continuous integration and continuous delivery pipelines. AI-assisted scanning can identify issues as code is developed, while additional testing can validate the finished product. This allows security teams to address weaknesses without unnecessarily delaying software delivery. Moses also described how Amazon uses intelligence from its MadPot infrastructure to inform patching priorities. Understanding what adversaries are targeting helps teams decide which weaknesses require immediate attention. The risks extend beyond vulnerabilities in applications and infrastructure. Moses described North Korean operations that use AI to generate personas and support fabricated identities. Their aim is to obtain jobs at companies. Amazon has stopped at least 2,900 North Korean actors during its hiring process, according to Moses. The example demonstrates how AI can amplify identity-based threats, complicating the work of security and hiring teams alike. Amazon’s integrated security model brings together cyber intelligence, personnel security, investigations, and other capabilities. The objective is to connect information across business units so that warning signs do not remain isolated in separate organizational functions. As AI makes it easier to scale deceptive operations, that ability to combine information becomes increasingly important. The episode concludes with a challenge to one of cybersecurity’s most familiar metrics: the number of vulnerabilities a team finds or patches. “I think the measure of a good security program won’t be how many vulnerabilities you found.” CJ Moses Instead, he expects the industry to focus on the time between discovering a vulnerability and patching or otherwise mitigating it. The appropriate response may involve blocking access, isolating a system, or applying another effective control rather than waiting for a conventional patch. That shift would measure security performance by how quickly an organization reduces exposure to real attacks, rather than how many weaknesses appear on a dashboard. As AI continues to accelerate both offense and defense, the practical question for security leaders is becoming less about how many vulnerabilities they can identify. It is more about whether they can close the window of opportunity before an attacker acts. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Anthropic to rewrite part of its leaked AI bill after safety backlash
2026-10-20

Anthropic to rewrite part of its leaked AI bill after safety backlash

The Next Web | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Anthropic has told US lawmakers it will rewrite part of its own draft AI safety bill. The leaked text drew days of criticism from the AI safety community. The confidential draft was published by Politico on Friday. Anthropic has been circulating it on Capitol Hill. Critics say the bill is weaker than the main AI safety bill already before Congress. They say it would make a dangerous model harder to stop, not easier. That is an awkward position for a company that built its name on pushing for tough AI rules. The draft lets the government block a model’s release or use only in narrow cases. The risk has to be foreseeable and imminent. It has to involve more than $1bn in damage, or the death or serious injury of more than 50 people, Politico reported. It also has to come from a bioweapon, a model escaping its developer’s control, or a cyberattack. The thresholds mirror California’s AI laws. To block a model, the Commerce Department would have to petition a federal court and make the case itself. The draft bars any other agency or official from suspending a model. It would also preempt some state AI laws. Those cover public disclosure, third-party evaluation and incident reporting. Child-safety and civil-rights laws are left alone. Independent evaluators would register with Commerce and check the labs’ models. They could be paid by the lab, as long as the payment did not depend on the result. They would have to report an imminent risk to Commerce within 72 hours. The comparison critics keep drawing is with the FRONTIER Act. That is a bipartisan bill from Representatives Jay Obernolte and Lori Trahan, introduced in July. Writing for Transformer, Shakeel Hashim said Anthropic’s draft is weaker in two main ways. It sets no standards or licensing for the evaluators, who can self-certify. And it makes a model much harder to block. Charlie Bullock of LawAI said the lack of licensing means auditors would compete. They would offer the least demanding checks at the lowest price, he said. Nathan Calvin, general counsel at the advocacy group Encode, said the draft made him “viscerally angry”. He said it appeared to stop state courts from suspending a model on safety grounds. California State Senator Scott Wiener, who wrote the state’s frontier AI law, said the proposal “falls far short of protecting public health and safety”. He said it set no minimum standards for safety plans or auditors. He also said it misused a definition from his transparency law, SB 53, to “gut” the draft’s emergency shutdown powers. “Anthropic’s AI safety proposal falls far short of protecting public health and safety. Congress must go further,” Wiener wrote on X. A second line of attack comes from the other direction. David Sacks, until recently the White House AI czar, said the draft’s lower size threshold would “sweep in most of Silicon Valley” and hurt startups. Anthropic’s draft counts a model as frontier at a lower training threshold than FRONTIER or California law. So it covers more companies. Anthony Cimino, who leads Anthropic’s federal affairs team, responded on X on Saturday. He said the preemption wording was “not precise enough” and would be updated. I lead our federal affairs team at @AnthropicAI and wanted to share a few thoughts on the draft federal AI safety bill text that’s been circulating this weekend after a version was shared with the press. First, some process. We’ve been advocating for the concepts in our Advanced AI Framework proposal for months. Understandably, federal policymakers wanted to know what this could look like in bill form. This draft is a translation of those ideas into legislative language, shared with a small number of congressional offices that have been working on these issues. It doesn’t include everything we would ultimately like to see passed into law. It is worth saying where we stand on some of these issues, and we agree there are areas where the draft could be improved. I’m seeing four main points of criticism. 1. Preemption. Since 2024 we have said that Congress should only override state law on AI safety if it passes a very strong federal standard. We’ve emphasized that any preemption in the AI safety context should be narrow, even scalpel-like. We plan to update our discussion language on preemption to better match the intentions of our AAIF framework. 2. Model blocking. Our aim is for Commerce to be able to ask a federal court to block a model that has dangerous capabilities and insufficient safeguards, backed by an independent evaluator’s assessment. We are trying to strike a reasonable balance here. But the bar can’t be so high that the power never gets used when it’s needed. 3. Risk thresholds. The threshold in our draft language pulled directly from existing standards in California law. It’s still probably time to re-evaluate thresholds to make sure the national standard is as strong as it can be. 4. Updates. We did make some adjustments, and we’re still working to strengthen the framework. We plan to have policy proposals soon on requirements for independent evaluators, and we’re still thinking through the options to prevent “evaluator shopping”. At its core, our proposal is intended to be the strongest on the table. Feedback is essential, and I’m grateful for it. anthony cimino (@ajcimino) October 18, 2026 He said Anthropic would keep states’ role in areas such as evaluator standards and a lab’s ability to shut down a model. It would publish further proposals on independent evaluators, he added. An Anthropic spokesperson told Politico the bill reflected policies “we believe Congress should act on”. The draft also follows a difficult summer for the company. The Trump administration used export controls to shut down two of Anthropic’s most advanced models earlier this year. Chief executive Dario Amodei has argued the industry must slow down. A series of rogue-AI incidents over the summer has raised the pressure for stronger rules. Other AI bills, including a Senate proposal to let Washington block models, are still in play. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! DeepSeek open-sources DeepInfer, the engine that runs its AI models
2026-10-20

DeepSeek open-sources DeepInfer, the engine that runs its AI models

The Next Web | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
DeepSeek has open-sourced DeepInfer, the software it uses to run its AI models in production. The Chinese AI lab announced the release on X on Tuesday and published the code on GitHub under the Apache 2.0 licence. For now, it supports only DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. 🚀 Introducing DeepInfer, our in-house inference framework, now fully open source! Powering DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash at scale with high throughput and low latency. We’re sharing our production stack to help the community lower compute costs, reduce energy use, and make AI more affordable. — DeepSeek (@deepseek_ai) October 20, 2026 Inference is the work of running a trained model to answer requests. The same model can cost much more or less to run, depending on the software that serves it. DeepInfer bundles optimised code for DeepSeek’s models with ways to split the work across many chips. It also includes speculative decoding, a technique that drafts several tokens ahead and checks them in one pass. Other features keep outputs consistent from run to run and force answers into set formats, according to the GitHub page. It runs on two kinds of hardware: Nvidia’s SM100 data-centre GPUs and Huawei’s Ascend 950 chips. DeepSeek said code for future models and further improvements will land in the same place. The release is not a complete service on its own. It leaves out a cache system, the split between processing prompts and generating answers, and load balancing across machines, the GitHub page says. DeepSeek also released deepseek-recipe, which matches how its official API turns text into tokens and back. DeepInfer was first built mainly on vLLM, a widely used open-source inference engine, DeepSeek says. It also drew on SGLang and Nvidia’s TensorRT-LLM. The vLLM project thanked DeepSeek on X for crediting the open-source community. It said it would keep improving its own support for V4.1-Flash with ideas from DeepInfer. DeepSeek measured its published benchmarks on four SM100 GPUs for processing prompts and 64 for generating output. They are the company’s own figures. There is no independent benchmarking of DeepInfer’s gains yet, Bloomberg’s Saritha Rai reported. Cloud and hosting companies that already run DeepSeek’s models can now use the same methods DeepSeek uses. Companies running the models on their own servers could also cut costs, Bloomberg reported. AI companies usually guard these techniques closely, Bloomberg noted. The release follows DeepSeek’s September model launch of V4.1-Flash. Earlier this month, it published Huawei chip tools as an alternative to Nvidia’s CUDA. Open-weight models now take 23% of spending on Vercel’s AI Gateway. It comes a day after the US ordered civilian federal agencies to remove DeepSeek products from their systems. By Tuesday afternoon, the GitHub repository had more than 8,000 stars. US Army picks a bid for ECHOTRIBBLE, its plan for autonomous AI agents
2026-10-20

US Army picks a bid for ECHOTRIBBLE, its plan for autonomous AI agents

The Next Web | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Army Cyber Command has picked one company’s bid for ECHOTRIBBLE, a programme for AI agents that hunt down targets in computer networks. The command’s technology centre and the Cyber Fusion Innovation Center (CFIC) made the choice on 8 October, according to CFIC’s website. A statement of work and contract will follow, it says. CFIC does not name the company. The Intercept reported details of the programme on Monday, from documents it obtained. TNW has not seen those documents. CFIC runs the effort with the technology centre, known as ArCTIC. Army Cyber Command, or ARCYBER, leads the Army’s defensive and offensive cyber operations. The two held an in-person event in Augusta, Georgia, on 24 June. The tool would move through infrastructure on its own, “identifying and neutralizing identified threats/targets”, says the event summary. CFIC’s event page adds that the tool “must operate with minimal human oversight” and work in “contested, air-gapped, or cloud-native systems”. The summary lists 43 attendees from 29 organisations. They included Dakota State University, Amazon Web Services, the Army’s C5ISR centre and the US Air Force’s 305th Cyberspace Operations Squadron. The slides shown at the June event are also public on CFIC’s site. In one example, the software lands on a laptop, finds other devices on the same network and makes copies of itself for an iPad, an Apple desktop and a Windows 11 PC. The slides plan prototype delivery around 23 November, about six months after work starts. Companies then submitted 65 white papers. Five presented to an assessment team at a virtual event from 6 to 8 October. The team picked one. All companies were told of their status on 13 October, CFIC says. A question-and-answer document for vendors says offensive missions are the programme’s main focus, The Intercept’s Sam Biddle reported. Neutralising a target could mean “disabling the threat completely or temporarily, deleting the threat from the target”, it says. The agents would move from device to device inside a foreign network until they found their target. They would report back as little as possible and then delete themselves, ideally before anyone noticed. The Army could set “predefined triggers” for what and where to attack. The plan follows months of reports about AI agents breaking into systems they were not told to attack. OpenAI’s agents hit RubyGems and Hugging Face during testing. Another broke into Australia’s Medicare portal. Last week, Anthropic said small AI models may soon copy themselves onto devices. David Krueger is a machine learning professor at the University of Montreal. He told The Intercept that giving autonomous systems a mandate to neutralise threats is “incredibly dangerous”. Hyrum Anderson, chief executive of the AI security firm Abundant, said an agent could pass through civilian systems on the way. He gave the example of a US water utility, if an adversary had set up its command servers there. “The target may well be hit. But what path will it take?” Anderson told The Intercept. William Marcellino is a senior behavioural scientist at the RAND Corporation. He said today’s public AI lacks the world knowledge that stops a soldier doing something they should not. ARCYBER did not respond to The Intercept’s questions, including how it would stop ECHOTRIBBLE causing unintended damage. The command’s head spoke about its AI agents on the same day, at the Association of the US Army’s conference in Washington. Federal News Network’s report of Lt. Gen. Christopher Eubank’s remarks does not mention ECHOTRIBBLE. He described Task Force Lexington, which has built a team of AI agents for defensive cyber work. Eubank said “no agents have strayed to this point” because of strict guardrails. “We are treating each one of these agents just like a human operator, so they are basic. And with that mindset, they are only allowed to do so much. It is a really tight framework,” Eubank said. Humans check the agents’ work every day, he said. The Pentagon set up an Autonomous Warfare Command this month. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription.
